GEI RID OF BAD KINGS.

How Quaker Thinks Permanent Peace May Come.

The Principal of Dalton Hall, Manchester (Mr. J. W. Graham) gave an interesting and thoughtful address , on "Permanent Peace in Europe" at the Friends' Meeting house, Buttermarket-street; on Sunday evening, there being a larger attendance than usual, many, no doubt, recalling the fine addregs which the Principal gave on "Christianity and the War" some months ago.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0009.txt | included=true | included =====
A DISCREDITED MOTTO?

The old motto "If you want peace, you must be prepared for war" savoured—said the Principal in the course of his address—of the worship of force, and was indicative of the fact that the spiritual and more Christian thoughts had not really ruled the relationships of the peoples of the world. Closely examined, the phrase would be found NS be one that had been entirely discredited. Evil had ever produced evil, and if the present war was to result only in defeat and in terms being imposed by the conquerors on the conquered it would, like all previous wars, only raise the seed for further trouble. In the past the relationships of the nations one with the other had been mainly, if ,not all wrong, and to avoid a repetition of the present horrors in the more or less distant, future, they must impart into international re- lationships something of that spirit which self- respecting people impart into their ordinary, every day social and business relationships. The mechanism of modern government was bad. The people had not a proper means of carrying their will into effect; they were in the hands of a number of highly-placed, well-organised people, and their governments were all organi-, sations for war, with war offices, generals, admirals, armies, navies and arsenals. Again the diplomatic machinery was too much mixed up with war and was too weak to achieve the purpose for which it was ostensibly set up.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0010.txt | included=true | included =====
The Many In The Hands Of A Few!

It was intolerable that a free people should, in that which concerned them most, be in the hands of a coterie of men of wealthy families, none of them having much knowledge of, or

sympathy with people outside their own more or less cramped orbit. If the war was to end 'in permanent peace, the final adjustment of the differences which at present set nation against nation must be along such lines as would leave

everybody with as few grievances as possible, and there must be no annexation of territory. They must encourage the wiser heads of Ger- many, and not discourage them by talking non- sense about crushing or dismembering that country, and the best way to remove all the sore places would be to have Europe divided on lines of nationality, as there was something about a nationality which was deep in the human mind, which survived• even differences of lan- guage and religion--the influence of the history of a people. The only chance of a perpetuation of the British Empire was for it to become less and less an empire and more and more a federa- tion. As Christians and lovers of peace they must do what they could towards the establish- ment of a machinery that made for peace in Europe.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0011.txt | included=true | included =====
Suggestions For Prevention Of War.

There was already a Hague Court, which dealt with matters lawyers could settle, but great questions which caused the war were not capable of being settled in that way. There must be a European Court of Conciliation, which could deal with disputes not capable of diplomatic settlement, and fwelve months must be given for investigation and decision. If any power refused to lay its disputes before that Council, or refused to abide by its decision, there must be a commercial boycott without bloodshed, and only if, and when, that .failed a recourse te arms. It was absolutely necessary that disarmament should take place, and there must be universal free trade, for then the whole argument of imperialism would fall to the ground. In conclusion, the speaker said that the people of to-day were living under a regime of fear, and all such fear would vanish if only the world could rid itself of the kings and statesmen and diplomats who lived by war and fattened on the pride of conquest.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDA Jr, NOVEMBER 27, 1915.

a display of boisterous 'youth as a pathetic evidence of the want of just and proper control at home. It was useless to gird at the Churoh for lack of spiritual resulta whilst the home was so supine in its control over the young. The day of revival of religion in England would come when the homes became the, centre of religious instruction. On Monday evening the schoolroom was filled with an audience who gathered to listen to a lecture on "The Life and Death of Worlds" given by Councillor G. Dale. Mr. A. Appleton was in the chair. The lecture was illustrated by lantern slides, and proved to be very in- teresting. Councillor Dale dealt with the respective sizes of the sun and the planets, and of the wonder- ful bodies which go to make up the Solar system. The proceeds were in aid of the Soldiers' Comforts Fund.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0013.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CHURCHES.

SOLDIERS AND RELIGION.

Bishop Chavasse Quotes From

===== 0002601_19151127_art0014.txt | included=true | included =====
Soldier Sons' Letters.

CONFIRMATION AT PARISH

CHURCH.

The Sacramental Ordinance of Confirmation was administered at the Parish Church on Sat- urday afternoon by the Lord Bishop of Liver- pool, the Right Rev. Dr. Chavasse. There were many confirmees and a large congregation wit- nessed the laying on of hands. "FATHERLY HAND EVER STRETCHED

OUT."

In the course of his address to the candidates, the Bishop sought to remind them how they might keep the Holy Spirit. They might, he said, keep the Holy Spirit by faith in God, by service for God, and by communion with God. One great feature of the Confirmation Service was that it brought before every one of them a sense of God's love—His love for them one by one. His (the Bishop's) hands had been placed on the head of each candidate to remind him or her that the Fatherly Hand of God would ever be stretched out for them—to keep them, to guard them, to guide them, to strengthen them.' and to help them. All their lives through they would walk in the shadow of the Fatherly. Hand of God. He urged them to believe in the love of God for each one of them; to believe that they were His children and that He was their Heavenly Father; to believe that He never ceased to love them, to watch over them and to bless them; to believe that they could turn to Him at all times and in all places and count on His presence and help. Let them have belief in God as their Father, their Saviour, their Sanc- tifier, and they would keep the Heavenly Faith. Each candidate had that day enlisted in Christ's Army and had promised to be a faithful Chris- tian soldier—a good soldier and servant of the Lord. Let each of them resolve to go forth that afternoon to fight for Christ, to stand up for Him, and never to be ashamed of confessing Him before men. Let it always be their endeavour to stop what was evil and to help forward what was good. Let them endeavour to serve Christ in their homes by being good sons or good daughters, good brothers and good sisters—by making their homes as holy and as happy as they could.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0015.txt | included=true | included =====
Serving the Divine in the Daily Round.

Further, let them endeavour to serve Christ in their respective places of business, by doing their work as thoroughly and as .well as they could. Never let . them lose sight of the fact that they could serve God quite as much by doing their work well, as he (the Bishop) could by doing his work well, for in God's sight their work was just as needful as his. Let them go and serve . Christ. in their daily work, so that men could trust them, rely upon them and know that they would never fail them. Again, let them serve God by trying to speak kind words and doing kind things; by seeking to make the world happier than it was when they came into it; by trying to bring their friends, and those who lived near them, to know and, to love the Saviour. Let them try and interest their friends in good things and set them an example of what good Churchmen and Churchwomen ought to be. Let them never underrate the power, the help of communion with God—the benefit to be derived from speaking to God and letting God speak to them. He hoped that not only would they kneel down and pray to -Him every night and morning, but during the day they would lift up ,their hearts and speak to Him, for God loved to hear His people speaking to Him in prayer. Their soldiers at the Front were praying. He was much struck by an ex- tract which he read the other day from a letter from a soldier-in the trenches. "There's not a man in the' trenches, near, me," *rote the sol- dier, "who does not say some kind of a prayer every day. Men who never prayed before,. they tell me, are praying out here 'to-day." If the men in the battle line found prayer so helpful, those at home must not neglect it. The Bishop went on earnestly to advise the candidates to read the Bible, assuring them that there was no book in all the world like it; no book that could take its place. Just as they were praying, so also were'the soldiers at the Front readihg the Bible. The other day two soldiers came to say good-bye to him, prior to going to the Front; the one an officer and the other a private. The Book That "Tommy" Treasures.

To each he offered a copy of the New Testa- ment, only to find that both had already pro- vided' themselves with one. If the soldiers found help and comfort through reading the Bible, those at home could nof afford to be without the Book. Dwelling on the help and comfort to be derived from communion with God, the Bishop exhorted his hearers to make their Holy. Communion regularly throughout life, and not merely to come once or twice just after their confirmation and then drop their 4t- tendance altogether. The men at the Front were eager in their attendance at Holy Com- munion. One ot his sons, who was a chaplain with. the Forces, told him that there was no service which the men liked so much as the Communion Service, and that before a battle they would come to him and ask him to let them have the Holy Eucharist. Sometimes in a ruined farmhouse, sometimes in a barn, and sometimes in the open air he had administered the sacrament to the men;and they had gone into the fight heartened and strengthened there- by. Another of his sons told him how on June 12th, before the Liverpool Scottish made their historic and heroic charge, 150 of the men of the regiment came to a Church of England Communion Service, eighty going to the Com- munion of the Church of Scotland„and 50 more to the Communion of the Church of Rome. His son wrote that as he looked round on the set and solemn faces of the soldier-communicants he could not help feeling that he was gazing upon one of the most wonderful pictures of prayer before battle that the world had ever witnessed. Every, man seemed to be commend- ing his squl to his God. Shortly afterwards' the brave lads Charged, and how-they took the Ger- man trenches was now a matter of history. If the brave 'men at the Front found the Holy Communion so helpful, was it not strange and sad that so many hundreds in the Churches turned their "hack on the Lord's Supper, and were scarcely ever to be seen at His table from one year's end to the other?

===== 0002601_19151127_art0016.txt | included=true | included =====
NEARLY 200 CONFIRMEES.

The candidates numbered 183, of whom 62 were males and 121 females. They included twelve boys from the Grammar School, three students from the Training College, two from St. Andrew's, Wigan; one from Christ Church, m Latchford, and one from Hollinfare. The re- maining 164 were from tho Parish Church and its, two missions of St. Clement and St. George.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0017.txt | included=true | included =====

===== 0002601_19151127_art0018.txt | included=true | included =====
CH I NESE FAITHS.

Miszionary Talks Of auddhism

And Taoism.

The foreign missionary anniversary of the Wycliffe Congregational Church, Warrington, was kept on Sunday, the special preachers being the Rev. W. J. Hatch, of South India, and the Rev. A. J. Hutchinson, of South China.

The deputation were given a welcome at a gathering that was held on the previous even- ing. The Rev. C. P. Pitt was in the chair, and addresses were given by both the Revs. Hatch and Hutchinson. The thanks of the meeting were accorded to them on, the motion of Mr. J. Monks, seconded by Mr. J. Witts.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0019.txt | included=true | included =====
IF INDIA BROKE AWAY FROM THE

EMPIRE

In the course of his address: the:-Rer..W. J. Hatch dealt with, the importance of the poSi- tion -of India in the British Empire. He main- tained' that: if by any chance India was to sever her connection with the Empire, it would mean the beginning of the ,Empire's downfall., Both from the standpoints of Imperial politics' and of trade, it would be better to lose South Africa or Australia. The chief reason for India's loyalty to the Empire was that. Eng- land had been pouring Christian truths and ideals into her. The best men of our Churches had been going to India to preach Christianity, and the inost heroic women of our Churches had been working amongst and influencing the women of India, so that now the people were showing a wonderful spirit. UP to the begin- ning of the war, England had been helping India; India was now helping England. Mr. Hatch went on to say that he was convinced that unless India was Christianised it would be impossible to keep her in the British Empire: India had not been won, or ,kept by brute force and England could only hold her by love and sympathy. • The Rev. A. J. Hutchinson spoke of the edu- cational, medical and evangelical •work ' of the London Missionary Society in South China. The services on Sunday were well attended. That in the morning Was conducted by the Rev. W. J, Hatch, who also preached the sermon.•• In the afternoon Mr. Hatch addressed the scholars.

INSPIRATION LOST IN IDOLATRY. An eloquent and interesting sermon was preached at the evening service by the Rev. A. J. Hutchinson, who spoke of some of his ex- periences in South Africa. He mentioned that in China there were three great religions— Buddhism, Taoism and Confucianism. Buddh- ism in China, he said, was corrupt, and the lofty teaching originally propagated under the inspiration of Bilkldlia was buried under a mass of idolatry. Taoism was a system of philosophy. Its original object was to search out the "elixir of life," but in the course if time it degenerated into a system of magic and witchcraft. At the present time it was used by the Chinese on such occasions as births, marriages and funerals. Confucianism was really a system of ethics. Confucius did not claim to be the originator of a religion, but he collected a number of wise maxims and gathered them into one system. There were a great num- ber of these maxims that really were admirable The reverend gentleman went on to say that the Chinese were a highly superstitious people. They were afraid of one another—mutual sus- picion was one of their characteristics and one of the things which made life in China so diffi- cult. They had a very strong belief in the spiritual world. The speaker went on to describe how the Chinese attempted to provide for the "bodily" wants of their dead ancestors He commended the werk of the London Mission- ary Society to the liberality. and prayers of the congregation. During the service the choir rendered the anthem "How lovely are the messengers !"

SORRY SUNDAY SPECTACLE

Rev. H. Ogden on Sankey-street On Sabbath Nights.

The chapel anniversary of the Working Men's Mission, Bank Quay, Warrington, was celebrated on Sunday, when special addresses were given by the Rev. H. Ogden (pastor of the Golborne-street Baptist Church). A musical service was held in the afternoon, the choir rendering the cantata, "Penitence, Pardon and Peace." The soloists were Miss Eva Tanner (soprano) and Mr. H. Foden (baritone). The choir were under the able conductorship of Mr. F. Atkinson, whilst Mr, T. Tanner pre- sided at the organ. , Mr. 11. Brocklehurst, sup- ported. by Mr. T. Dale, officiated as chairman.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0021.txt | included=true | included =====
THE RIGHT INCENTIVE NEEDED.

At the morning service the Rev. H. Ogden preached on the claims for devotion on the part of the Church, his text being- Phil. in. 13-14, ‘" Forgetting those things which are be- hind and reaching forth unto those things which are before, I press toward the mark for the prize of the high calling of God in Christ Jesus.", The preacher pointed out that a keen observer of child life would notice that any little task that a child attempted was done with con-. centration and with the utmost eagerness. It needed but the tight incentive and: Men And women would 'show the same eagerness and concentration. That eagerness .and .concentra- tion was demanded of Christian men an women in their Christian life and in their work for the Church. One deeply' deplored the scant power of concentration apparent to-day in our young men and women. If a young man had as his sole reading papers of the "snippety " order it was hardly .possible to expect those men to follow any consecutive reasoning. The preacher concluded by expressing his hope and trust that whilst Christian men and women would secure the things of this life, to be used for the glory-of God, they would remember ,pre- eminently that 'the greatest prize.of all was the Fellowship- of Christ and devotion to duty.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
PLACE OF THE CHURCH IN WORSHIP.

At the, evening service the Rev. H. Ogden's subject was the duty of a Christian to the Church as a " visible emblem." His text was "I dwell in a house of cedar, but the Ark of God dwelleth in curtains." He said that there were many noble .traits and characteristics in the Israelitish King, David. He. never (rat- grew his attachment to his native village in the time of his prosperity: David believed i- I a meeting place for worship and he believed lhat it should be. magnificent. The king remem- bered that he lived in a p:4lace, and. the ,temple of God was in a tent! Free Churchmen had done their work 2onsiderab!e harm by the over- statement and the illegitiinto-a Lse of a doc- trine. Often it was reiterated that they could worship "in the green fields and by the brook running through the woods " quite as well as in a building prepared and consecrated, lor wor- ship. But his (the preacher's) experience was that the folk who wandered into the woods and meadows and by the streams for their worship gave scant attention to the God who gave those blessings. Their thought of worship, almost universally, was some vague, indefinite, nebulous idea of God.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
Dynamic Power Of Combined Worship.

He (the preacher) believed in the Communion of Saints, and that there was a tremendous dynamic power put into operation when the childreu of God came together "with one accord and of one- mind" for prayer and praise. Free Churchmen had lost, rather than gained by genially tolerating indefinite ideas of worship and,ffrayer. The preacher donfessed that he had a tremendous admiration for the solidarity and cohesion of Roman Catholic wor- ship. He was lost in lidmiration at the results attained, but had nothing but the iciest regard for the methods by which such results were obtained. He believed that the day was approaching when the Free Churches ,must find Some - distinct basis of authority without tip tremendous compulsion of the Roman 'Catholic Church. -

The Home Losing Its Hold.

After referring to the home life of David. the preacher said that there was little doubt that in the social and religious ::fe of our time, the home had lost much of its authority. Sankey-street on Sunday night was'nOt solnueh

===== 0002601_19151127_art0024.txt | included=true | included =====
CHURCH MEMBERS' DUTY.

Call For Personal Service At Methodist Free Church.

Anniversary services were held at the Buck- ley-street Methodist Free Church on Sunday, the special preacher for the day beinf, Mr. R. Pinches, of Chester. Preaching in the morning to a goodly-sized congregation, Mr. Pinches dealt with various phases of church life, and said that the secret, of success in I.very 'lurch wat unity, and with unity, IJve. membets must all work together in unison, and the compelling power was, that Christian love for each other which led one to work for all and all to work for one; which led them to pray for their fellows and ever to be ready to extend the hand of fellowship. Again, to be successful a church must be a working church. The members of it must put their shoulders to the wheel; each of them must do something—some- thing beyond mere perfunctory attendance and the feeling of a passing interest.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0025.txt | included=true | included =====
"A Bit" For All To Do.

Every member could and should come forward and do his, or her "bit," and if they all did so then the church would be a real live force, and an abundant source of blessing to the locality.' It was the duty of every church mem- ber to be anxious about the salvation of others. as well as about his, or her, own salvation; and he wanted to lay special stress on that fact be- cause for some reason or other they did not seem to Shave so many conversions in their churches nowadays as they did a few years ago. It was essential to the church life of the future that great attention should be paid to Sunday school work. They must get the children into the schools; get a real hold of them—and keep it! The old notion that a child could do nothing was exploded, for in hundreds of cases the fact that the child had taken the message home from Sunday school had been the means of drawing father or mother, and, often, both to the Throne of Grace. In conclusion, the preacher deprecated lukewarmness among church workers; they wanted workers who were hot with zeal—real hat!

Mr. Pinches also preached in the evening, in addition to giving an address at a special gathering in the afternoon; over which Mr. T. Hewitt presided, and at which Misses May Hockenhull and E. Stanway sang solos.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0026.txt | included=true | included =====
WYCLIFFE P.S.A.

Successful Missionary Sunday Gathering.

There was a good attendance at the P.S.A. gathering at the Wycliffe Congregational Church on Sunday.

Mr. B. Farrar was in the chair, and he ex- pressed a hearty welcome to the following sol- diers: Sapper Pye, Pte. Helsby (Maidstone), Pte; Horrocks (Blackpool), -Pte. F. A. Perkins (Leicester), and Pte.' Culton (London. He also. read a letter containing good wishes from Ptes. G. Unsworth, and John Oxley.

The Land of the Religious.

The Rev. W. J. Hatch (South India) gave an earnest and interesting address upon missionary work in India. People, he thought, did not take sufficient interest in India, which had been spoken of as "the brightest jewel in the British Crown." Along with the Colonies, the great subipentinent had sent her sons to fight for the common, cause of our Empire in the present great European war. That was not the only way in which India had served Britain; she had served her well in many other directions. The natives of India were, on the whole, very re- ligious—very observant of the duties enjoined upon them by their particular being. Their whole thoughts—morning, noon and night—were upon their religion, and it was the principal subject of conversation with them. That was in great contrast to the attitude of the Euro- pean, concerning his religion. He had said that the natives of India were religious; and though that waS true, the pity of it was that they fol- lowed after false religious tenets; they stood in sore need of the Great White Light of the Gos- pel. It was the duty of all Christians in Britain .to do something to help to send their dusky brethren that Light. Missionary work in India was beset with difficulties. It was a great trial for a native to renounce his old faith and embrace .Christianity, for it lost him the friend- ship of his family and his fellows. But the native who had seen the. Light rarely rgeanted ; rather would he suffer any hardship. If a son were suspect of Christianity the parents would bring in the astrologer and the native dOctors, and 'resort to all means in their power to win their dear one back from bis new faith. But hundreds of thousands had kept that faith un- waveringly, .and thousands of them were now working as assistants in the mission field. Message from the Rev. T. T. James.

• During the afternoon the following extracts from a letter from the Rey. T. T. James—the pastor of the church: who is serving with the Y.11.C.A. at the Front—was read by the Chair- man :

"It will be a great loss to be without Mr. Osborne in our P.S.A. He has done good work, and we shall miss him much. Will you kindly ,remember me to him, and say how deeply I appreciate the splendid serviee'he has. rendered? I am sure Mr. Bond will do his. very best, and. will faithfully give his abilities in filling the vacancy. I motored one hundred miles to Rouen to see Harry Kent, but found him already dead and buried. I saw his grave in the Rouen Cemetery, and a great circle of graves where all the brave lads, are buried. They have given their lives for us. I hope to get a photo of Harry Kent's. grave for his mother. It stands there with a simple white cross upon it, and with his name stamped on the cross. And soon the flowers will grow there.. It is a pleasant spot. He was a good lad—brave, patient and cheerful—and God has taken him .to better things. I am glad. to hear of the P.S.A. meet- ings. I should like to be with you and share them, and hope it may be so before long. May God give you good times, and may you all be greatly encouraged." Christmas Cheer for Soldier-Wycliffians.

At the close of the meeting the Chairman asked the members and visitors to forward to him the correct names and regimental numbers of all Wycliffiians who have joined the Forces, in order that a parcel of Christmas cheer may be forwarded to each of them.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0028.txt | included=true | included =====
" TONIRIIES' " TESTANIENT.

What Bible Society Is Doing For

Friend And Foe.

The interesting ,fact that soldiers speaking ' no fewer titan thirty different laßguages are engaged in the present war and that the British and Foreign Bible Society has been able to supply the fighting men with Testaments in each of those several tongues was made known at the annual meeting of the Warrington Auxi- liary of the society, which was held in the Co- operative Hall on Thursday evening, and was presided over by the Mayor (Alderman Peter Peacock). Supporting his Worship were the Revs. 'l3. Percival,. J. Martin, J. Hirst, John Yonge, W. Thompson Derry, 11. Ogden, Horatio C. Moor (Vicar of Earlestown) and Tom Dring (of Wallasey), And Messrs. C. E. Parker. Segar Owen (secretary of the auxiliary), W. T. Wil- liams and J. C. Holland. It was announced that illness .prevented the attendance of the , Rev. William Reid.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0029.txt | included=true | included =====
LOCAL AUXILIARY'S FINANCES.

At the outset.. Mr. Segar Owen submitted the financial report, Which showed that the year began with a balance in hand of oel 18s. sd. , The amount rethitted to the parent society during the twelve months was ..R45, and there was now a balance in hand of 3s. 6d. The amount remitteC“o the parent society this year was exactly the ...Same as it was in 1914, which, in turn, was .an %Crease on 1913's total. (Ap- plause) It was only fair, however, to say that they had not achieved that very satisfactory result quite off their own bat, for Mrs. Parker had very kindly made up the amount raised so .that the same figure as last- year could be re- mitted. Mrs. Parker was a good friend to the society on all occasions. (Applause.) He did not know that they ought to congratulate them- selves on only sending as much as they did last year, though he was glad that it was not less. No doubt money was hard to get hold of in these troublous times and a great debt of gratitude was duo to the collectors for the fact that they had managed to raise as much as they had. (Hear, hear.) Some people, nowa- days, were making plenty of money and others had to strive hard to make ends meet, but what- ever their position they should not make their subscription to the Bible Society a matter for exercising economy upon. (Hear, • hear.)

MAYOR AND NEED OF BIBLE TEACHING.

The Mayor Said it was gratifying to find that the society was striving so well during the present critical times, when everybody was asking for something ,off everybody to h'elp the distressed and sorrowful and those who had been hard hit by the war; when money, too, was wanted to help the poorer parents of brave lads at the Front and on the high seas to be able to send parcels of good things to their loved ones, and when it was also required to swell the various war funds. He had had a lot to do with raising money for the local war funds and other effcrts in connection with the war, and he was afraid he was rapidly becoming a bit of a nuisance in the town. (Laughter.) If ever there was a time in the history of the world when the teaching of the Bible was required, it was the present time. It was a great source of comfort to the men in the trenches, and he had had it from fellows at the Front who had hardly ever looked at a Bible before that they now read the Book in their .lonely hours, after going through the perils of the battlefield. They had told him of the comfort they had gained from reading the little khaki-covered Testament which they carried with them. The " Book " In Five Hundred Tongues.

Not only did the Bible Society see that the British soldiers received supplies of the Testa- ment, but also that the soldiers in the armies of the other countries engaged also got them. The society had been the means of having the Scriptures ;translated into nearly 500 languages, at an enormous cost of time, trouble and money. Moreover, the society was, not a profit-making concern, for it gave away an enormous number of the Bibles it produced: and the others it sent out for sale at less. than Cost price. = A lot of copies of the Book produced by the -society were' sent to Germany and Austria, but he .was afraid that those in high places in those two countries had not received copies, or. had not taken much notice of the teaching contained therein. If the people he referred to had studied the Book more they would not have brought about the present day of destruction and misery.' (Hear, hear.), Perhaps they would wake up after the war—when they had had that stirring up which they were going to get—and would pay greater heed to the teaching of the Bible.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
"THE COMMISSARIAT DEPARTMENT OF

THE CHURCH."

The Vicar of Earlestown then addressed the meeting, claiming that a subscription to the Bible Society was, in a sense, a war fund in- vestment and that the Society wac, as a .I.,lerical friend of his described it during the days of the Boer War, the commissariat department .of the Church of Christ—supplying everything tLat was wanted to sustain the spiritual life in the fight against evil, sin and ignorance. Hit was glad that the Society was still able to wori: in Germany and Austria, for the people of thcse countries, as a, whole, had not been at fault, but the destructive criticism of the Bible whieh had been indulged in in certain quarters—that and the teaching of such books as, those about .whizh so much had been heard in these latter, days. If the teaching of the Old Book had been stuck to and its message taken .to heart things would have been ;very. different on the Continent to what they were to-day. (Hear, hear.). Why The Bibles Are Sold.

Whenever possible the Bible Society made a charge for its copies of the Scriptures, and the reason was that it believed in the Old truism that what a person gave something for he valued. The price that was asked was not always what the Book cost; in fact it was generally far from it, the principle followed being. that the people should be asked to give what they could afford. Roughly the Society only got back by sales about 30 per cent: of the cost of the Bibles printed. A little time ago one of the newspapers propounded the question "Is this the time to give money to societies for the very problematical work of bringing a few' of the lowest classes into the Christian religion?" In answer to that he would merely point out that, a, generation ago if the• writer of that, or any other. stranger, had set his foot in the Fijis he would not have a head to bring out shortly •afterwards. To-day all that was changed, and it was the Bible that had wrought that change.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0031.txt | included=true | included =====
SOCIETY BORN IN WAR TIME.

The Rev. Tom Dring, in his addre, dealt exclusively with the war work of the Society, recalling the fact :that the Society was born in war tim6—in. 1804, 'when .England was wrestling. with that despot of Europe—Napoleon Bona- parte. Then, as. to-day, :She was in the throes of war not :-on her own behalf, but as the trustee of the liberties of Europe ,and of the world, and in defence of the weak. He believed that as in the beginning of the last century, so now—purified, it might be, by suffering; triumphant indeed through sacrifice—in God's good time this country would win her way through to a larger and cleaner, place, and to a sphere of influence greater than she had ever, had before. Let the nation be true to that great tradition of morality and spiritual lite 'which came •to her through the Great Book. The Bible-Society had never made a distinction between friend and foe, 'believing that down- in the human heart, however crusted over by materialism and militarism, there was a unity of need and a yearning that ultimately,' in response . to thh genial and gracious Gospel. of Divine Love would express itself in a regime of universal justice; brotherhood and peace. Hear, hear.)

===== 0002601_19151127_art0032.txt | included=true | included =====
GREAT TRUTHS THAT ABIDE.

Mr. C. E. Parker proposed a vOte of thanks. to the Mayor .for Presiding, and, to the speakers, remarking that it was a comfort to be able to attend a meeting Of that sort and to get away for a time from the turmoil and agitation of the world and ,turn their eyes to the abiding things. Despite all:that was happening; despite the fact that empires.- were rocking and. king- doms seemed to be marching to ruin, the Bible, with .its great truths and glorious message abides, untouched and unaffected—the salve that would alone heal the 'wounds of the world.

The Rev. W. Thompson Derry seconded, and the proposition having been carried with ap- plause, .the Mayor briefly returned thanks and the meeting terminated. During the evening Miss Emily Taylor sang Gouned's "Entreat Me Not to Leave Thee," Mr. Ivan Jones accompanying at the piano.

STORIES FROM THE TRENCHES.

CALL FROM THE TRENCHES.

Rifleman Who Wonders How Eligible

Men Can Stick At Home.

Mr. Joseph Makin, of 53, Oldham-street, Latchford, has received interesting' letters from a friend—Rifleman Percy Cliffe, of Hatton— who is ,serving with the 10th Battalion of the Rifle Brigade, "somewhere in France." "I am still going strong, and all the boys from Warrington in our Brigade are doing the same." the gallant Rifleman writes. "We have been having some awful weather lately, but it is lovely at present—nice frosty mornings; just the sort to buck us up, for we get a, bit dull some- times! We cannot realise thht there is a war on at times; it is so quiet, except for an odd shot or two now and again. Of course the Artillery livens things up every now and then." In a later letter Rifleman Cliffe says:—" We are not kept in the trenches so long now—only four or five days at a time. .. . I see you are having another rally in Warrington! I don't know how some of the Young .men-can ki-ek it ' at , home so long. I should like them to -see how the boys here are fixed and then they'd 'come."

TWO MONTHS AGO.

Latchford Man Dn Dardanelles Position.

Pte. J. Shaw, 58, Slater-street, Latchford, sig- naller in the Mediterranean Expeditionary Force, writes to us, *under date September 11th, a letter which came to hand on Saturday. November 6th. He says:— " Dear Sir,—Once again I take the oppor- tunity of writing a few lines. We are Row in the second line of trenches, taking a well-earned rest, after being in the firing line 15 days. As I write an artillery duel is going on and shrapnel is bursting thirty to fifty yards from me. I must say that one is far safer in the firing line than in the reserve- trenches. We had a grand firework display at midnight on August 27th. This is the time the, Turks usually choose to attack. All was quiet, when suddenly a volley of rifle fire was poured into us. Then shrapnel and bombs were flying through the air, and ywherever 'they burst, huge flames lit the place up. This was carried on till the early hours of the morning, when the Turks appeared to grow tired of it. I -must say our boys were not slow to reply, and I think we gave them more than they bar- gained for. I was only sorry that night that I could not stand alongside of them, but I was on duty at this time on the field telegraph, which, you know, plays a great part in this present war. "One of my fellow signallers. who is also a Warrington lad, and also on the Liberal Roll of Honour, was slightly wounded in the calf on September 7th. For the benefit of those who are most dear to him, they have not the slightest need to worry about him, as when I shook hands with him he was in the best of spirits, and I suppose by now he will be lying t Lemnos or Alexandria. Australians and Ghurkas.

" Whilst I am writing I must pay a tribute to the fighting powers of the Australians and Ghurkas, who are on our extreme right. They are at it iitght and day, and I am certain the 'Auks must live in dread of these .powerful men. I also take off my hat to the Ghurkas wit° are doing transport work with their little mountain mules. They have done a wonderful amount of work since the day of landing, bring- ing food, water and ammunition up to the firing line, slung over the backs of their mules. I have several times stopped and spoken to them. Sometimes they have understood. They can speak a little broken English. The chief thing they, can say is, 'Have you cigarette or bacca.' The talk here at the present time is that Bulgaria and Rumania are about to throw in their, lot with the Allies. I only hope this is true, as I believe it would finish this job out here. I also believe it would be the commencement of the crumbling up of the German Empire. The bad weather is .ju,,,c cOm- mencing. It is very hot during the daytime, hut extremely cold at night. I taust say a word about the fly. I never saw so many in. 01 my life. We get them for every =tea!: they even fly into one's mouth. We get littl.3 peace during the daytime with them. It is very a musing to open a tin of jam: there are hundreds of them getting their tea, and they won't give you a chance, except for pulling out the ones that have got stuck.

"I and my chum. A. Harris. are in the best of health, and hoping it continues till peace is declared. •

" Beli3v.) ran, .1. Shaw." BACK TO CI VI LISATI ON.

The following letter, posted by Private Shaw six .weeks later, came to hand the following post:— . _

October 22nd, 1915

Sir,—Once again I have the pleas-ure in writing a few lines to you. We have seen several changes and experienced many exciting times since last I wrote to you for myself and on behalf of my chum. After making that memorable landing at Sulva, or Anafarta Bay, on August 7th, we were continually in the firing line for eight weeks.' On September 30th we were taken from the trenches, and despatched by boat to Lemnos for a well-earned rest, and to bp re-strengthened, although we had previously, whilst in the trenches, re- ceived a draft. Some of these had been in France, and most of them say "Give me France every time!" Well, during our short stay in Lemnos we endured bad weather, being wet and extremely cold at nights. We then left Lemnos, arriving at SaloniCa (Greece). The sight of this city brought back to us civiliSation once more; in fact, we saw railway engines and tramcars, which to most of us acted as a tonic. Of course, these were the first we had seen since leaving Old England's shore. The streets here are crowded with Greek, French and English soldiers, and all appear to get on well together, although not always understood with regard to language. Everywhere points to business here. Wherever the next move will be I cannot say, but, no matter where, we are always ready. ,I was sorry to see in your issue of September 25th the case of I. Williamson, an employe of the St. Helens Cable Co. I knew Ilim well, and, many times out here I have tried to get in touch, with him. -Although be- longing to the same division we never really were close together. I have just heard that E. Coyne, who was one of our fellow signallers, who was wounded a short time ago, and of whom I wrote in a previous letter, is now lying in Edinburgh; and will shortly proceed home for a few days. If this is so I hope he will visit my home and speak a few words to .ray wife and people, whom I know' will make him very welcome. Pte. J. SHAW (Signaller), Pte. A. HARRIS (Signaller),

ORFORD TERRITORIALS.

Varied Experiences And Comments On

Passing Events.

We take the following extracts from letters sent by members of the 4th South Lanes, to the "Orford Parish Magazine" for November:

Confirmation Classes at the Front.

"Our chaplain is holding confirmation classes each week, and, needless to say, I. am an eager attendant. We had the Bishop of Khartoum out here, who is going about from one division to another confirming those who wish it. Those in our division are to be confirmed 'this month, and I am most anxiously looking forward to it." "The Recent Small Engagement."

"Yes, there is great news from this line, and it :is ~wheti,we have been waiting for for these last few months. We heVen't yet moved ,in:our. particular part of the line, but we are waiting patiently for the time to come, and you may rest assured we shall keep it up, that is, pro- viding these at home will. keep our munitions up to strength, and it is hoped that, our suc- cesses will bring the slackers in and Make them realisp that their seryices will be useful. There was a small engagement here in which our machinie-gun section and bombers took a very active part, and what was asked of them was done first-class. The remainder of the battalion were close at hand behind the firing line, and then went up to the trenches, after dusk had set in, to consolidate the new position, which is part of our duty as pioneer battalion. I am sorry to sax we lost a few more good men

killed and wounded, but we must not let that droop our spirits, as that is only part of what we have to pay for our freedom which the Kultur dealers have had the audacity to try to take from us. But we shall show.them yet what the British Bulldog can do when roused. I haven't the faintest doubt that before many more months are past thc Kultur dealers will beadown on their knees 'begging for the mercy they refused to the helpless ,women and chil- dren and old men, when they.first entered bravo little BelFinsn, ancl,tivlt they will, one and all, -get their just desserts." _

Fruits Of Victory In Our Grasp.

" No doubt those at home on munitions are working six :1 days to the week, but you must remember that to us it is seven days to the week, and keep at it, too, as the fruits of victory are in our grasp now, and as long as the tools are sent out for us to work with Yen may rest assured that the 'Tommy 'who is out here risking his life day after day will give "1! his best. You can't realise the difference t has occurred since we have been out here. if we sent over one shell they gave us in return about a dozen. But I am very pleased to say that it is the reverse of things at present and we hope to keep it up. Seeing the Country!

"It is a fine job for seeing the country. For instance, when I was wounded on the Nth rS June I travelled 21 miles in a motor ambulaneo and about 250 miles on a train,. and now I have had to travel it back again. If there is still any young men left in Orford I should 'like to recommend the Continent instead of the Isle of Man. It is not a holiday, of course, but every- body has to do something. Well, I am pleased to tell you we are not fighting now—or the majority of us. Anyhow, .we are doing the work. It is dangerous, of course. We have to go to the trenches working. But I• had five months holding trenches, and I think this is much better: I was pleased to receive the- maga zinc, as I look forward to it to see how our church is going on

===== 0002601_19151127_art0036.txt | included=true | included =====
Returned Comrades Look Fine.

"I am glad to say that Willie Ingham, Ben Homer and also Bert Corbett have rejoined us,. and, my word, they do look fine. You said in your letter that from all accounts we look as if we have had our hands full. Well, I can safely say we have, and you can take my word for it that the Huns are fairly catching it just lately. Our shelling is absolutely fine, for our. marksmen drop them in the trenches nine, times out of every ten. I amf glad to hear that our boys- are, doing well in Gallop°li."

An abnormal state of affairs exists in the Scotch Whisky trade owing -to the shortage of labour through recruiting. Messrs. Mackie and Co. Distillers Ltd., intimate that they are unable to execute orders except to old customers. in order to maintain the high standard of' quality of their "White Horse" Whisky. .

MUNITIONS AND DRINK.

"It is the interest of a . trade against the interest of the nation; and the interest of the nation must prevail." Sir,—The above quotation from a most .preg- nant article in the "Manchester Guardian" fittingly summarises the whole position. Some years ago Lord Rosebery told us that. unless "we •controlled the liquor trade, it would con- trol us,": and .there is no mistake about it—the trade is making a-serious and most insidious attempt to dominate the situation: Talk about. the "patriotism" of the Trade? . Where is .it, and of what does it consist? A more blatant claim with less justification was never made.' • It is a fact that there is overwhelming 'evi- dence to prove that the output of munitions was seriously hampered by excessive drinking on-the part of ,men engaged in this vital work, and it is a fact that since certain areas have been scheduled in every one of them there has been , a great improvement. The Trade 0, clares that the restrictions have not been folloN\ed by -less consumption of drink ig —if this is t . e, on what- possible ground does the Trade o lose the :restrictions? , 'lt is also , fact that the number of 'men who have been'reinoved from amongst us by enlist- ment is so great that we have a right to expect from that cause alone a very great diminution in the quantity of drink consumed. . If there is still as • great• a consumption as ever—and the Trade says there is—then there must be "excess- sive" drinking, and that 'on a•very large scale. The power of the Trade was , evidenced in. Manchester this week. • What it has cost to foster the agitation which was disclosed at the inquiry no one outside...the inner circle of the Trade will ever know—you cannot take whole page advertisements in the Press and issue hundreds of thousands of • postcards for noth- ing, but if rumour is to be credited, the liquor interest can well afford to go to any cost to maintain he present drinking facilities, in view of the huge trade which is being done. . How much of the present' agitation againsf restriction is genuine Is it not a fact that the prime movers in the matter all along the line • are the brewers and their satellites? Does any- one imagine for .a.moment that any substantial section of " labour " would have " groused " against the new conditions if it had ,not been prompted and provoked to do so by the Trade? The country *mild have accepted "total pro- hibition" at the beginning of the war—what has brought about the present opposition? The answer is obvious.•

CMZEN

DRINK RESTRICTIONS.

Sir,—ln his latest effusion -Mr. " Citizen " makes no attempt to meet the cake I put for- Ward, -preferring rely upoirpersonalities and suggestions of bias on my part lam content to leave it to the intelligence of your readers to judge as to the impartiality of Mr. "Citizen," and will confine myself to answering his legiti- mate arguments—such as they are. In the first place your correspondent states from his intimate knowledge of large 'employers of labour that "0r-indulgence in drink is in tt. greater or lesser degree the Clafte of nearly every workshop in the country." In declaring this statement to be a ridiculous exaggeration I -am in the fortunate position of being able to back up my contention by- facts, and I do not— as does Mr. "-Citizen "4 ---ask your readers to accept this grave indictment of. the working classes upon the mere personal Opinion of an anonymous correspondent. In March of the I present year the -True Temperance Association sent: out a circular letter to 300 representative manufaCturing firms .asking for_their viewW as to the alleged interference of drink- with indus- trial efficiency. Only 52 replies were received; - and of these, 41 have no complaints to make, and many speak in high praise of their work- men's industry and sobriety. I have a copy of this correspondence before me now, and I shall continue to treasure it as abiding proof that the British workman is not so black ashe is painted —by uncharitable persons of the " Citizen " type. Mr. " Citizen " says that my "real interest in the working man is infinitesimal! that I know nothing about .working class opinion; and from the depth of his own deep knowledge and sym- pathy he "Asserts emphatically that there is no feeling of irritation at the restrictions." I hap- pen to have taken some little interest in labour questions for the last fifteen years, but instead of "asserting emphatically' my personal opinion, I prefer to Point to the publicly-ex- pressed, sentiMents of the workers themselves. Only last Tuesday- the Deckers' Union sent a deputation; headed by Mr. Ben Tillett, to ex- press to the Control Board the resentment felt by the .men at being penalised for the -offences of a 5 per cent. minority, ancl to ask for some modification of the restrictions. This is only one caSe, but it serves to show that Mr. " Citizen's " "emphatic assertions" are not always reliable: Liverpool, Nov. 20th, 1915.

J. E. SIMPSON

===== 0002601_19151127_art0041.txt | included=true | included =====
GOTHENBURG SYSTEM.

Sir,—With reference to the question as to the effect of reducing the ..facilities for obtain- ing ,drink or the consumption of drink, may I state that •in Gothenburg, in Sweden, the per capita consumption of brannvin in 1874 was 22.21 litres, while hi 1913 it was 5.18 litres, a re- duction of about 75 per , cent. This was effected by reducing the number of public- houses, the hours of , business, credit, treating, ke., Ste. These sweeping -reforms were made possible by. the fact that the public-houses for the Wale of this spirit are not run by private individuals for personal profit, but by indi- viduals who have no vested interests in the traffic at all. The reforms were made not by Parliament, but by the managing directors of their own accord. That is what is known as the Gothenburg systenfi. Speakitg, of this system in the House of Lords lately, --Lord Lytton, said : " If,*e turn to' Norway and Sweden—the only countries in which it has been fully applied I claim that in those countries it has been universally accepted as: a better alternative method of controlling the retail liquor- sale

k

• . _ -

than the licensing of private individuals wi; we have in this country to-day, and which existed in Norway and Sweden before the prin- ciple of disinterested management was intro- duced. In Norway and Sweden there are tem- perance societies, the Good Templars, the Rechabites, and other Societies, the members of which believe in the total prohibition of the sale of liquor and look forward to that as the ultimate ideal of any temperance policy. They hold in fact, precisely the same views on the question of temperance as do the Templars and Reehabites in this country, and yet all the members of those Societies in Norway and Sweden, although they would certainly wish to abolish the disinterested management companies because they would like to abolish all facilities for obtaining liquor up-on • licensed premises, although they are opposed to the disintt7l-4, management system in that sense, they are all

agreed in preferring it to the system which pre- vailed beforehand, and you will not find one of those societies who would be in favour of doing away with the system of controlling the trade through disinterested management com- panies and going back to the old system. 1 claim, therefore, that in those countries where it has been tried, whatever may be .the differ- ences of opinion as to its merits as a final measure of temperance :reform by universal con- sent it. is admitted that this system is prefertthle to one of 'private licensing such as we have in this country." (November 25th, 1912, p 1033). That Lord' Lytton's interpretation of the position was correct has since been amply proved by the simple fact that a recent Swedish Royal Commission, made up almost entirely of temperance workers has actually and unani- mously recommended increasing the powers and extending the operations of the companies.— Yours, CIVIS.

MUNITIONS TRIBUNAL.

Worker Who Lost 100 Hours In

Twenty Weeks.

A number of cases were dealt with at the local Munitions Tribunal on Monday afternoon. ,The President (Mr. T. Ridgway) was in the cha,ir, and the assessors were Dr. 'Armstrong (for the employers) and Mr. F. Berry, of Sockton Heath (for the workmen). The first application for a discharge certifi- cate was made by a workman on the grounds that his employers failed to employ him six days a week.

It was stated on his behalf that for four or five weeks he had been told net to work on Mon-

days. In consequence of this he had found another situation, *here he would lae employed directly on munitions. His previous employers had refused to give' him a- certificate: . A representative of the firm stated that the man had been in their employ since July Bth and he had only been unemployed on three Mondays. On each of these Occasions it was not the fault of the firm: The workman in question had, during the last twenty weeks, voluntarily lost 105 hours. A certificate was refused.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0044.txt | included=true | included =====
TRADE UNIONISTS' SUCCESSFUL PLEA

Two men applied for discharge certificates on ,he grounds that other Trade Unionists had uade their position in the workshop " uneom-

fortable." - A Trade Union official said that the two mei were sent to the firm from the Labour Exchange When they were nicely • "sot in the shop." they were asked to join a particular Union. They, were already members of another :Union. They refused to do as requested, and said that would be settled by the Tribunal whether they should be forced to tray° one trade society and join another. They told the manager of the firm what had happened, and that things were being made uncomfortable for them, and he ordered them off the premises. The manager of the firm said that it did net matter to him which society the men belonged to, but he wanted the work done. The firm had a big Government order and the work must go on. If there had been any trouble he knew nothing about it. lle told the two men to get on with their work and the other men said that there was no trouble at the works. Work was at a standstill and the Government were sending soldiers to help the firm. The foreman of the workshop also said that. so far as he was aware, there was no trouble. Certificates of discharge were granted. •

DRINKING AMONG WOMEN.

Saving Soldiers' Wives And

Children.

The Mayor of Southport (Councillor Henry Ball), speaking at a .meeting in Southport on Tuesday, said he had attended a meeting held in' Manchester that day to consider the further restriction of the sale of liquor. Evidence from Lancashire towns and all over the district was submitted, • and the speakers were unanimous on one point, which revealed a very sad state of things. Every Chief Constable and every Mayor who spoke said it was absolutely certain that drinking among women was on the in- crease, especially among soldiers' wives. Vari- ous reasons were advanced for that most de- plorable state of things, • and very strong arguments were put forward for the earlier closing of public-houses in Lancashire. Some speakers said they would go further, and wel- come total prohibition during the period of the war to f3aVe the wives and children of soldiers. And they were worth saving. There was another way of lifting Up the population• and at the, same time by destroying the liquor trade, and this was by personal effort. Heartbreaking.

Archdeacon Madden described the 'drinking among the wivesiof soldiers as heart-breaking. It should be remembered that the women were undergoing a new experience, and were now having money that they had never thought of getting before. They had not been educated, so to speak, to use it properly. It *could be a good thing to endeavour to distract the women from their sordid surroundings and help to lift them up. lie did not know who could do that work better than women. "Do not leeture them," the Archdeacon said, "do not hector them, but give them your kindly help.r'

===== 0002601_19151127_art0047.txt | included=true | included =====
TRAVELLED EIGHT DAYS WITH- OUT SEEING A HUMAN BEING.

Sir Cornthwaite Eason, ex-Premier of West Australia, speaking at the Bovril Australian Estates -meeting about his recent visit to the Commonwealth, said his trek across Northern Australia occupied twenty-one days, travelling from daylight to dark, and for the greater. part of that time he was journeying within the boundaries of the Bovril Australian Estates. "I passed," he continued, "through many of the numerous scattered mobs of cattle that make up the company's herd, the total of which now numbers nearly 150,000 head. One hundred and fifty thousand head of fine healthy cattle de- pasturing on nine and a quarter million acres of land—the largest cattle farm in the British DOminions, owned by a British Company, and destined for British consumption mainly in the form of Bovril.

"The undisturbed existence that these cattle lead is illustrated by the fact that I travelled for eight successive days out of the twenty-one without meeting a single human being."

===== 0002601_19151127_art0049.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, NOVEMBER 27, 1915.

RESTRICTING THE SALE OF LIQUOR.

Should The Manchester Area Be " Proclaimed " ?

===== 0002601_19151127_art0050.txt | included=true | included =====
POSITION OF WARRINGTON.

Considerable interest is being taken in the result of the inquiry held this week in Man- chester by the representatives of the Central Control Board. The Manchester area, which in- cludes Warrington, is shown on the accompany- ing map to be almost surrounded Iv areas already subject to restrictions. On the south the hours in the Cheshire rural area are more restricted than in Warrington. It has always been our opinion that instead of inviting contention by sectional limitations of the sale of drink, a national time-table should have been enforced over the whole country. This would have done away with the sense of unfairness, from 'both points of view, which is caused by one area being brought under restric- tion and an adjoining one left subject to the old regulations, as in the case of the Liverpool and Manchester areas at the present moment. The same reasons for restrictions in areas engaged in Government work apply with scarcely less force to areas engaged in the national service of keep- ing going the nation's activities in manufac- ture, in service, in transport, and in export. For all these the maximum of human efficiency is required no less than in specialised work. The question of restriction ought to be ap- proached solely from the necessities of the country occasioned by the war. It would be inopportune—it would be unfair—to take ad- vantage of the present needs to advance the cause of temperance or abstinence, and perma- nently alter the regulations for the sale of intoxicants. The ordinary reasons for restric- tions are only pertinent, and should only be advanced in as far as they are applicable to the national emergency of the hour.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0051.txt | included=true | included =====
GOOD EFFECT OF RESTRICTION.

The Report of the Central Control Board as to the effects of the amount of drink consumed, the public order kept, and the resultant conditions of life and labour, form a guide as to the de- sirability or otherwise of extending the restric- tions to areas not yet Controlled. Since the Orders of the Board became operative every opportunity has been taken to judge of the practical effect of the restrictions imposed and of the other measures enacted. Speaking broadly, the information obtained points to the following conclusions, which, hovhever, musk necessarily be regarded as provisional, owing to the short duration of the experience. There has been a consideKable diminution in the number of both the prbsecutions and the convictions for drunkenness. Prosecutions in the first eight areas scheduled in England and Wales, on an average of; the four weeks prior to the commencement of the Order in each area amounted to 734 per week. This figure, calcu-

===== 0002601_19151127_art0052.txt | included=true | included =====
NATIONAL FINANCIAL PERIL.

Mr. J. IL Thomas, the Railway Labour M.P., shows that he at least is alive to the national peril. He says:— "I am bound- to say that people to-day are not paying sufficient attention to -the dan- gerous financial position we are in. It is no use. Men are not living as if we were at war. Rich and poor fail to appreciate the fact that to-day we are living on capital, and not on income, and it is for us to realise that if we want to win this war we must all contribute

our part." Would that all, rich and poor alike, were alive to our national financial peril. We can- not afford to spend large sums of money in non- essentials. We are spending unnecessarily in many things apart from intoxicants, but the amount spent in these is the largest item of national waste, that is waste under present conditions. This must be cut down if, as Mr. Thomas says, "we want to win this war." When we consider the sacrifices which so many are making in offering themselves for military service, sacrifices of money, of comfort, and the chance of having to offer .the supreme sacrifice, surely it is up to us, who, from age or other disability, have to remain at home, to do our bit.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0053.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PUBLIC-HOUSES.

Shall Opening Hours Be

Further Lessened ?

CONTROL BOARD CONSIDER MATTER.

It is not yet known whether there will be any further reduction locally in the number of hours during which licensed premises in the town may remain open for the sale of intoxi, cating liquor. But the matter is under con- sideration by the responsible authority, who have had placed before them a great mass of information—marshalled, on the one hand, by those who contend that the present drinking facilities are calculated to interfere with the maximum production of the much-needed munitions of war, and, on the other hand, by those—the representatives of the "Trade " who claim that the present "opening hours " are sufficiently restricted, and that no such degree of disturbance with the output' of war-

RE.VTRICTED

necessities has taken place as would justify further , interference with drinking facilities, arguing further that it would be an injustice to the moderate drinker that he should be deprived of his "creature comforts" at such times as he has been "used to taking them" just because the privilege has been abused by a small minority. A WHOLE DAY SITTING.

The question as to whether there shall be a further curtailment of the hours during which licensed premises in Warrington shall remain open, was discuf3sed at Manchester on Tuesday —by the Central Control Board (Liquor Traffic), within the scope of whose jurisdictions this county borough of ours comes. The pros and cons of the point were thoroughly gone into, the Board's whole day being fully occupied in consulting the views of a • 'large number of authorities, it being about eight o'clock in the evening before their labours were completed. If an order should subsequently be made as to the restriction of drinking facilities in the Man- chester district it will be communicated through the "London Gazette." The repre- sentatives of the Board gave no indication at the close of the inquiry. of the impressions they • would •carry to the cull Board. The public is now generally cognisant of the nature of the restrictive orders which the Board has promulgated in London and other parts vf the country where munitions are being made. The Manchester Area is probably the last large munition-producing district to come under its eye, and in preparation for the visit the forces for and against further restriction had their batteries trained. Such forces, however, were only heard by courtesy at the end of the real inquiry, to whichshad been summoned a "great crowd of witnesses."

THE BOARD AND WARRINGTON'S REPRESENTATIVES.

The delegation from the Board which at- tended at Manchester Town Hall at 10.30 in the morning was presided over by the Chair- man .(Lord d'Abernon) ' and included Sir William Lever, Mr. Philip Snowden, M.P., Mr. John Hodge, M.P., Mr. E. R. Cross and Mr. W. Towle, with Mr John B. Lincoln (assistant secretary). The lig a the investigation was not confined to Arichester and Salford, but extended over Lancashire, Cheshire, Flint and also took in the Glossop Petty Sessional Division and Glossop town. . The County Borough of Warrington was repro-, sented by the Mayor (Alderman Peter Peacock), .the Deputy Town. Clerk (Mr. A. T. Holloway) the Clerk to the 'borough Justices (Mr. G. F. F. White), and the Chief Constable (Mr. Martin , Nicholls).

===== 0002601_19151127_art0054.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PUBLICANS' LEAFLET.

It goes without saying that the temperance folk 'of Warrington were no less interested in the inquiry than were those engaged in the licensed trade, to say nothing of those who made that trade possible—and- profitable, from the £s. d. viewpoint. During the week-end' customers calling at the various licensed houses in the borough were importuned to sign post- cards protesting against any further reduction in the "opening hours" and the town : was liberally circularised by the local branch of the National Trade 'Defence Association. Tinder the caption "Munitions and Drink," the Association's circular set forth :

The Central Control Board are visiting Manchester on Tuesday, 2:3rd November, 1915, to hold an enquiry, at the Manchester Town Hall, as to whether further restrictions

should be placed on the sale and con,umptioli of intoxicating liquors in licensed premises and clubs.

In other areas, where orders have been made, the following restrictions apply:- 1. No person may "treat" another to a drink, except with a meal. 2. The hours of sale and consumption are reduced to two at noon, and three and a half at night. 3. No spirits may be sold for off-consump- tion on Saturday or Sunday. 4. The sale on credit, even by wine and spirit merchants, of intoxicating liquors, is prohibited.

The Board must- be satisfied that such an

Order is necessary to increase the output of munitions. The general opinion in Lanca- shire is that such an Order is totally un- necessary. If you are of the same opinion, you should apply on these premises, for a postcard, and sign your name, address and occupation, protesting against the extension of the Order to Lancashire.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0055.txt | included=true | included =====
THE LICENSED. VICTUALLERS' POINT OF VIEW.

An " Examiner " representative sought—and round—representative views from members >f the Trade as to the need or

otherwise for further curtailment of the hours during which the licensed houses of the borough may keep their doors open for the sale of in- toxicants.

lie first called on a gentleman who presides over a flourishing hostelry near the centre of the town. He was much in sympathy with the object aimed at by the proposed restrictions, "but," he added, "these temperance folk• will never learn. They go on making the setae old mistake over and over again. Men and women do not get drunk on licensed premises—we don't give them a chance of doing that; that is to say the wise 'amongSt us don't—for we realise that we have too much at stake; in fact, our very bread and butter! Ds it stand to reason that I am, with my eyes open, going to throw away a large part of the money and all the time and energy r have put into building up this 'house' just for the sake cf eAllectirg three ha'pence off some befiiddled fool, who would only be a source of nuisance to me and my wife and to every customer who came in? The drunken man is anathema to the publican quite as much as he is to the teetotaller. And as a class, we publicans hate to see women lolling over our bars and' drinking themselves —well, drinking themselves into heaven only knows what! A man does not lose his finer feelings just because he goes to the Brewster' Sessions and pays for a licence. Most .(f us are married men—in fact, nine-tenths of: us, I sx.p- pose—and we know that we should • I<ick i(p ' a terrible fuss if our wives 'carried on' 8s( me of the poor creatures we have to deal with— despite the advice and warnings we often tender them—' carry on.' Most of th.) mon you !we staggering about the streets—and, after all; they are only a tiny proportion—get in the state you see them off licensed premixes, for no landlord in his right senses would knowingly keep them inside his doors until they got to the 'wobbly. walk-and-hic-cough stage.' Ask any publican you like and you will find that he regards the red-nosed stay-away-from-work toper as a great nuisance, rather than a good customer. It is fellows like he who get us and our business into disrepute and penalise the average man who merely has his drink, because he feels it does him good, and then goes about his work like a sensible chap."

===== 0002601_19151127_art0056.txt | included=true | included =====
AN INNKEEPERS' SUGGESTION.

Another representative publican interviewed by the " Examiner " man, was quite ready to admit that there was a deal of excessive drink- ing going on, though he denied that there was anything like so much now as there was before the war, particularly among women. He added that the small, mean public-houses in the back streets are prolific causes of over-drinking and secret-tippling, and is of opinion that some dozen large houses, run on the lines of the well- known wine-lodges to be met with in Manches- ter and Liverpool, would be sufficient for the needs of the town, and would constitute a safe- guard against drunkenness. They would not only cater for those requiring intoxicants, but would serve every other form of light refresh- ment—and even substantial meals—and the customers using them would, so to speak, be so open to the public gaze that that in itself would be a powerful factor in preventing stupid over- indulgence. THE " NO-TR EATI NG " REGULATfON.

Among the restrictions which will be impoSed if the proposed reduction of opening hours, ttc., is decided upon is, as will be noticed, that the time-honoured custom of " treating " is taboo— and, on the whole, the custom is one that even "the most habitual habitue" of the public- house will not be sorry to Part with. A magisterial decision bearing on this section was given by the Cardiff Stipendiary on Wednesday, when the licensee of a hotel and his Wife were summoned for supplying intoxicating liquor to persons to be consumed on the premises with- out the same having been ordered or paid for by the persons so supplied. It was stated in evidence that a man ordered three glasses of beer and one of stout for himself and paid for them with a two-shilling piece. When the drinks were placed on the counter he handed them round to three friends. The stipendiary held that the landlady, by placing the drinks on the counter, did not " supply " them, but that this was done by the purchaser in handing them round. It was pointed out that such ft ruling was tantamount to making the no-treat- ing order null and void, and the stipendiary consented to adjourn his decision for a week. The stipendiary dismissed the summons.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0057.txt | included=true | included =====
WORKS CANTEENS.

It was pointed out in the House of Commons on Wednesday that the object of -the Control Board was not to deprive workers of legitimate and reasonable refreshment, but to, so far as possible, prevent "lost time" by men who are inclined to "turn the rational into the irrational." Working under the Board is a Canteen Committee and this, so Dr. Addison, the Parliamentary Secretary to the Munitions Department, assured the House, is actively en- gaged both in stimulating the provision of can- teens by employers and in arranging directly for their provision. All the projectile ~and national factories' were being provided with canteens.

Aunt Maria's Forgetfulness. Mistress: "Did you have company kit night, Mary?" Mary: "Only my Aunt Maria, mum." Mistress: "When you see her again will you tell her that she left her tobacco pouch on the piano?"

1" Manchester Guardian" Map Department: Copyright.

The shaded portions of the map show the areas for which Orders have already been made. The dotted portion shows the area which will prob ably be included in any Order that affects Manchester.

lated for a period of four weeks for each area subsequent to the commencement of the Orders, has fallen to 417, a decrease of over 40 per cent. The corresponding figures for the first two areas scheduled in Scotland are 695 and 585.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0059.txt | included=true | included =====
IMPROVING THE HOME.

In addition to the fOregoinefigures, to which undue importance should not be attached, there, is abundant evidence that drinking' has dimin- ished in many of the areas. Many witnesses, speaking of their own areas, mention improved public order, a better condition in the streets, and an improved condition of the children; and the Board have received evidence that money which was formerly spent in excessive drinking is now either deposited in savings banks or used to improve the home. On September 24 the Board held a conference of chief constables from areas scheduled in Eng- land and Wales, at which tho unanimous opinion was expressed that better order and behaviour had prevailed generally since the coming into force of the Orders, and that this result was due particularly to the prohibition of " treating " and of credit sales, and. to the restriction of hours. The same'views,have been ,expressed by a number of chief constables in areas scheduled in Scotland. "These initial results augur well for the . future, though it would be rash to assert at( this stage that all the imProvement obtained will be permanent." It is\ evident from the above extract, and also from the character of the evidence admitted at the Mdnchester inquiry, that the Control Board takes a wide view of the conditions which .weigh with it in coming to a conclusion. It considers ; not alone the direct effect of lost time .on munitions, but also the general effect of re- striction which only indirectly but not 'less really, influences the manufacturer of war material. _ .

It should be frankly admitted that the re- strictions are drastic, and that they involve for a considerable number of working class- people limitation of social facilities, for which there is at present no ample substitute. For a num- ber of .our people the public-house has become their Only place for social intercourse and re- freshment of the character they desire. Of _ _ .

course, it should be remembered that these re- strictions di) not close the public-houses, but only limit\ the sale of drink to certain hours. These restrictions have diminished • drinking, and so saved money for necessary needs; have reduced excess; .and saved much time and national expense in dealing with drunkards; and have improved time-keeping in all classes of employment so vital to production at the present hour. The question is whether the national need demands the sacrifices from those who depend for their social needs Upon the public-house, and if so, will not the patriotism of such rise to the opportunity, and cheerfully acquiesce with the results of restrictions, if the Control Board deem it desirable to enforce I restrictions in the Manchester. area. _

IN THE COURTS.

SOLDIER'S FICTITIOUS PASS.

Before the Borough Bench on Monday was a soldier charged with absenting himself from his regiment—the South Lancashires—without leave. It was.stated that when he alighted from a train at Warrington he produced an old rail- way ticket with the date erased, and that the " pass " he offered was a fictitious one. lie was handed over to an escort. At the same Court a man who admitted being a deserter from the Northampton Regiment was similarly dealt with.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0062.txt | included=true | included =====
AN UNDATED PASS.

Soldier Who'Denies That He Is An

Absentee.

When Daniel Muir, a private in the Argyle and Sutherland Highlander 4;, was charged, at Tuesday's Borough Police Court, with being an absentee from .his regiment he cienied the offence, pointing out that no date as to when he was to return was stated in the leave-pass issued to him.

A police officer stated that he found Muir at 6, Robson-street, and asked him to produ.;e his pass. The man at once did so, and witness t-w.w that it was undated. Witness arrested him. Councillor Roberts, who was in the chair, told the prisoner that the fact that the pass gave no date as to when he was to return was in his favour, and said that no doubt the mili- tary authorities would take the point into con- sideration. "The magistrates," he added, "have no option but, to hand you over to a military escort, and I hope you will be able to make a successful defence."

Drunken Man Who Smashed Cell

Window.

. At Tuesday's Borough Police Court. before Councillor H. Roberts and other magistrates.
