Inspector Hodgkiss said that when the man was brought in the Police Station his language was filthy, whilst his conduct in tile cell was very bad. The man was a stranger to the town; he said he had been in the place about three weeks or a month. The police desired to make further inquiries concerning him, and therefore asked for a week's remand. The remand asked for was granted.

FURIOUS MOTORING.

Thirty Or Forty Miles An Hour In Sankey-street

That he drove a motor van down Sankey-street at the rate of forty miles an hour was tne sub- ject matter of a summons heard at Tuesday's _Borough Police Court against George Herbert Armstrong, of 23, Poplar-avenue, Altrincham, In reply to the summons the defendant 1,..c did drive the motor fast—at a speed dangerous to the public. I was-in a very great hurry." P.C. Skinner told the Bench that the offenct, was committed at 9.50 on the morning oi November 18th. The motor was proceeding down Sankey-street in the direction of the Quay Bridge, and was going at quite 40: miles per hour. Witneskput up his hand and shouted to the driver, but no notice was taken. Th( dangerous speed was maintained all the way to the bridge and, although there were a lot of vehicles and people about at the time, the de- fendant did not sound his horn. Subsequently witness saw the defendant outside a shop. De- fendant then told him that he had no spee,' indicator, and he did not know at what rate he had been travelling. Defendant now told the Bench that he might have been travelling at 30 miles per hour. The car would not do more. He produced his licence, which was free from endorsements so far. _ _

Councillor Roberts (who was in the chair), told defendant that his action was highly dangerous, and the Bench had some dOubt in their minds as to whether they ought not to in- flict the maximum penalty, which was £2O. Nowever, seeing that that was apparently his first offence of the kind they had decided to let him off with a penalty of £5, with 2s. 6d. for a witness's fee.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0066.txt | included=true | included =====
THIEF AND RECEIVER.

Bench Act In The Spirit Of The Old

Motto.

A Warrington dealer was fined ot2o at the Borough Pollee Court on Thursday on a charge of receiving certain property, valued at £4 125., well knowing it to have been stolen. Previous to the hearing of this case, however, George Mulhalh of Pocket-street, Warrington, was charged with having, during the past month, stolen from a warehouse in Scotland- road, Warrington, the Property in question, which consisted of 841 b. of curled hair and 18 sacks, and was the property of Richard Hard- ing. Mulhall pleaded guilty, but before he WWI sentenced, Joseph Matthews, metal dealer, 2, Pump-yard, Lythgoes-lane, Warrington, was summoned for having feloniously received the property. He was represented by Mr. T. S. Steel ,and pleaded not guilty. • Candid Admissions.

After a short statement by the Chief Constable, Mulhall was called as a witness

against Matthews. He said that he had been employed by nearly every marine store dealer in the town. About thirty-five years ago he was in business on his own account. For the last three months or so he had been employed by Mr. Harding at the warehouse. He started taking things from the warehouse about five weeks ago,, and sold them to Matthews. He received payment on each occasion. The sum was generally about ss. or 65., but on the last occasion it was either 7s. or Bs. It was night when he went to see Matthews. The lattet never asked any questions. He (Mulhall) understood that he received about 4d: a lb. for the hair. Matthews had seen him working for Mr. Harding. Richard Harding, paper-bag merchant, of Buttermarket-street, said that he valued the hair at Bd. per lb. The sacks were worth about 2s. each. In answer to Mr. Steel, the witness said that Bd. a lb. would be the selling price of the hair. He would expect to buy 'the hair cheaper. Detective-Sergeant Reddington spoke to find- ing the, goods on Matthews's premises.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0067.txt | included=true | included =====
The Defence.

Defending, Air. Steel said that there was a very great difference between receiving stolen, property; and receiving stolen property "well knowing it to have been stolen." 'Matthews had known MuMall for a number of years, and knew him to be a very respectable man. When Mulhall first brought some hair, he said it.was some that his wife had collected. Matthews bought it quite bona-fide, and paid approxim- ately the market price for it. There was no corroboration of Mulhall's statement, and the Bench must satisfy themselves that Matthews knew the goods to have been stolen. With regard to the 18 bags, Matthews did not examine them very carefully, and thought that they were soda bags or sugar bags.

In the witness-box Matthews said that he paid about 4s. 6d. a dozen for the bags, which was the price for soda bags or sugar bags. He paid 4d. a lb. for the curled hair and 10d. for the long horse hair, which figures would be about the market price. Answering the Chief Constable, Matthews said that he did not know that it was illegal to purchase the sacks. He admitted that he was not a registered marine store dealer. He did not keep books. During a year he purchased hundreds of lots of various things. He could not produce any written statement as to what he paid Mulhall. Elizabeth Matthews (wife of the defendant) and Margaret Griffiths gave evidence as to Mul- hall bringing some of the hair round one after- noon. _ _

===== 0002601_19151127_art0068.txt | included=true | included =====
The Receiver's Record.

After reading a long list Of charges that had been made against Matthews, the Chief Con- stable said that in 1913 a series of offences were found out, but as the police had a desire that he should help, rather than hinder, them in their work, they refrained from -prosecuting him and gave him a severe caution, on the dis- tinct understanding that he gave up his busi- ness. _

Mulhall was fined 40s. and Matthews „C2O. Both flues were paid.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0069.txt | included=true | included =====
SUFFERING CHILDREN.

Neoleel ful Parents Sent To Prison.

William Wood and Elizabeth Wood, residing at 1, Stanley-street, Warrington, were sum- moned at the Borough Police Court on Thurs- day for neglecting their children. Mr. Arthur Browne, who prosecuted on be- half of the N.S.P.C.C., said that this was a case of giving way to drink, esEecially on the part of the woman. One of the children, aged four and a half months: had been seen by a doctor, who ordered it to be taken to the Work- house. The husband was not so much to blame as the wife.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0070.txt | included=true | included =====
Left Baby Without Food for Five Hours.

Inspector Wainwright, of the N.S.P.C.C., said that the defendants had five children. They occupied furnished rooms in Stanley-street, and paid. 9s. 4d. a week rent. On September 21st he visited the house and saw a baby, belonging to the ,couple, seriously ill. It went into con- vulsions whilst he was there. On November 7th he again went to see Mrs. Wood. He was told that on the previous Saturday she had left the baby without food from 11 a.m. to 4 30 p.m., and that she was drunk at 7.30. The bedroom was in a filthy condition. Dr. Peacocke ordered that the child should be sent to the Workhouse. It should have weighed 111 b. Soz., but only weighed 71b. 4oz. The Inspector added that the male defendant seemed afraid of his wife. Dr. Peacocke said that the conditions under which the children lived caused them to suffer unnecessarily. Evidence was also given by Dr. Jagger (resi- dent medical officer at the Workhouse) and 11 rs . McKay (with whom the defendants lodged).

Mrs. Wood was sentenced to three months' imprisonment and her husband to one month.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0071.txt | included=true | included =====
WOULD NOT AVORK.

Yet Says "It Is A Grand Thing For

A Man."

At the Borough Police Court on Thursday, Edward Williams; 18, Holland-street, Warring- ton, was summoned for neglecting his children in a manner likely to cause them unnecessary suffering. He pleaded not guilty. His wife applied for a separation order. Prosecuting on behalf of the N.S.P.C.C., Mr. Arthur Browne said that this was a peculiar case; the defendant would not work! He had given up his place at Crosfield's for no reason at all. The Society's object in bringing the de- fendant before the Bench was to make him realise that he had to look after his children. Mrs. Williams said that she and the defend- ant had been married 18 years. There were five children. The defendant had worked at Crosfield's for 25 years, but during the last six Weeks had done nothing. The children .and her- self had been short of, food. She left him be. cause he would not work. Asked to make an offer to do something for his wife and children, Williams said "I am always willing to do a bit. Work is a grand thing for a man." Thought He Would Have "A Bit of a Rest!" The Magistrates' Clerk: Why have you not worked• this last six weeks ?—Williams: I thought I would have a bit of a rest. He added that he would find work as soon as possible. Inspector Wainwright, of the N.S.P.C.C., said that the defendant had told him that he wanted a "bit of a rest," and had taken up the same attitude on three other occasions. Once he said his rest was "only a flash in the pan." The defendant said that he did not mind find- ing a "fresh shop." The Chairman (Mr. J. Rymer Young): That is very considerate of you. The case was adjourned for a week to see if the defendant endeavoured to find work.

THE EXAMINER,

SATURDAY, NOVEMBER 27, 1915

===== 0002601_19151127_art0081.txt | included=true | included =====
FEWER DRINKING FACILITIES?

We report and review at length the fact that during the present week there has been a conference at Manchester to decide whether the Manchester area, shall ho proclaimed an area to be placed under certain restrictions as to the sale of drink. If the area is to be " controlled " Warrington will be controlled, for we come, for these purposes, within the sphere of influence of Cottonopolis. A tre- mendous effort was made by "The Trade,". as is always the case when the interests .of ,the drink traffic are in any way challenged, to avert any further ,inroads upon trade privileges. There were efforts made in the opposite direction by the habitual opponents of drink. But neither the interests of "The Trade," nor the advocacy of temperance cOunts in this matter. The,only question is the salvation of the country; and the asser- tion of Mr. Lloyd George is as true now as it was when he made it—that alcohol is a more dangerous enemy to us than German submar- ines. We need munitions, and if drink stands in the way of the free supply of even a small percentage -of thee() munitions, drink inter- data must stand aside. In these matters it is

well to consider the opinion of the detached atitsider. There has been, as we have said, great display of power on thel side of the ?think traffic; but the London 'correspondent of the "Manchester Guardian," viewing the situation with,a cold-bloodedne.ss born of dis- tance, says : 4

There is really very little TOOIII for specula ton as to what the order will be. It is as nearly certain as anything can be that in the Manchester area, as in all other areas, includ- ing London, it will be virtually Sunday hours fur every day, with no treating, no credit, and no long pull.

The esridenee taken by the Liquor Control inquiry Committee this week will be digested L y them and submitted to the Restrictions committee of the Control Board, and then the fall Board will proceed to judgment. To reach this point will take from two to four weeks, so that the Order, if made, cannot be made immediately, as some people thought.

7:PC

_ WHAT OF GREECE? A good many harsh things have been said of Greece, but some allowance must be made fair • Sovereign who sees_ his little country

naemaced on one side by the gentle German, and receiving, on the other, the attention of

such naval Powers as Great Britain, France, and Italy. In the long run fortune must favour the 'side on which the great naval array, to which we have referred, belongs, and the Greek people may be presumed to know it. But besides the dilemma in which King Constantine finds himself, there is his domestic and Court bias in favour of Ger- many. He has dallied betwooft the two stools an unconscionable length of time, and betrayed Servia in the meantime, so that he must not complain if he meets with the fall that is said to await invariably upon those who so daily. Our latest information is that the King and his Government have come down on the side of the fence favourable to the In fact, on Wednesday, an official statement was issued in Athens stating that that day the Greek Government handed to the Quadruple Entente Ministers its reply to their collective Note. "The reply," we were told, "is couched in very friendly terms, and gives satisfaction to the Entente -demands, and all the guarantees considered easential." In confirmation of the fact that satisfaction had been obtained, the world was also in- formed that "the British Government has authorised the departure of Greek steamers loaded with corn and other goods, which have been held up at Malta. The transmission of cables via Malta is also authorised." It is assumed that such authorisation would not hav€ been givm had not the Allies been able to accept, and to trust, the Greek assurance. By German wireless we learn that the "Frank- furter Zeitung " reports from Constantinople that, according to an Athens report, " Kitchener's mission has been in vain. At every moment an ultimatum and the recall of the Entente Ambassadors are expectpd."

HOUSE RENTS IN WAR TIME.

The Rents Bill, introduced in the House of Commons on Thursday, is a good measure in the present emergency—good in the sense that it is necessary—though it may outrage economic law and threaten all kinds of evils if persisted in. _ But the measure will dis- appear when its temporary necessity dis- appears. The Bill, if it becomes an Act, will prevent landlords from raising rents below a certain sum and in certain areas. It is in the differentiation of districts that tho Bill will find the greatest volume of criticism. The powers of the Bill will be enforced by Orders in Council. The areas dealt with are ' the administrative county of London, bor- oughs or urban districts with populations ex- ceeding 100,000, or areas where, owing to the influx of population attributable to the war, there is as insufficiency of housing accommo- 'dation. Primarily, the Government seek to prevent landlords from exploiting their ten- ants where war work has made tenantfrplenti- ful and houses scarce, and therefore valuable, and in very large towns. There are however, other places in the land somewhat smaller than London, and the districts with over 100,000 inhabitants, that contain landlords who have not been above passing on their war taxes to their tenants to be_paid for them in the shape of increased rents. As-a matter of fact, a tenant in a small district may stand in greater need of protection, for he may have no extra war work to help him to pay his extra war rent. The limitation of the rents to be guarded to £3O a year in Lon- don and £2l in the provinces is not open to the same hostile criticism, though there are many middle class tenants who can ill afford to have their standing charges increased at the moment. The pre-war rent will be taken as the standard, and any increase in rent above the standard since the war will be de- clared irrecoverable upon the issue of the Order in Council. The Bill also applies to mortgages on property, no higher rate to be charged than before the war, except where there has been structural alteration. In such a case a maximum of 6 per cent. may be charged on the total outlay. There are re- strictions against foreclosure. These are only temporary restrictions brought about by methods of ,personal greed—happily not very. widely spread—which should be unknown at the present time.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, NOVEMBER 27, 1915

LEPROSY CANARD.

Abominable Gossip Causes Pain

To Parents.

LIKE THE " RUSSIAN " STORY.

During this week an abominable lie has been spread in Warrington. It has taken various shapes but, in the main, it has stated that one of the daughters of Mr. Wm. Hy. Platt, 127, Church-street had contracted leprosy. It was stated (1) that the young lady was dead and had been buried privately, and (2) that she had been taken away to some foreign island. It was also stated that her sweetheart, who was a soldier, had sent her a set of furs from the Front, from which she had contracted the disease;'

(2) That the furs came from Turkey; (3 That they came from France; (4) That the young man had returned from Egypt and brought the disease with him. The names of the doctors who were supposed to have attended to the patient were given, and awful details were added in keeping with the disease that was supposed to be involved. Yesterday we made careful inquiry with a view to laying this most cruel of all the cruel rumours the war has provided for the edifica- tion of the Warrington public. _

We consulted the doe-tors whose names had been so unjustifiably used. One said that he had heard the rumour-which he likened to the rumour about the presence of Russian soldiers in England at /the beginning of the war. He emphatically denied having had such a patient and he added he did not want one. The other doctor said the story, though not impossible, was highly improbable, because leprosy takes years to develop. Last evening we consulted Mr. and Mrs. Platt. Mr. Platt, who is an employe 'at Messrs. Armitage and Rigby's, was highly in- dignant that such a story should have been spread about the mills. Mrs./Platt was much upset, because people persisted in pointing out the house, and she had had many callers, in- cluding the Rector. "My daughter," she said to our representa- tive, "is a bonny girl,sand in the best of health. She has not seen the doctors mentioned, though she has seen another one for indigestion. She is at- work as usual, and her employer is as annoyed as anyone at whit is being said. A few minutes ago. she answered the door to a person who came to inquire about her." "Has she had a set of furs?" we asked. "She has," said Mrs. Platt, coming as near a smile as her palpable trouble would allow her to come, "but she has had them for four years. Her sweetheart is not a soldier, and has there- fore not been abroad."

"Would you like the story contradicted?" we asked.

"r should," said both Mr. and Mrs. Platt at once.

• Yesterday afternoon Mrs. Platt wrote a letter to Mrs. Stansfield, Fairfield School, asking her kindly to tell the children, if they heard any- thing of the story to say it was untrue.

SUNDAY SCHOOL PROBLEMS.

LOCAL AMUSEMENTS.

"THE CINEMA STAR."

Big Musical Comedy $` Hit" At The Royal Court.

"The Cinema Star" took musical-comedy loving London by storm, and this week it has captivated Warrington theatregoers. And there can be small wonder that it should have done, so, for ,it is a vastly amusing production, everything which goes to make it up—" book,' lyrics and music—being of the smart and alluring order, whilst the mounting and the dressing are on the lavish scale. Few merrier musical melanges have come out of the Metropo- lis, and its phenomenal success has been due to the fact that allied to music that ,haunts the ear is a coherent plot—something to marvel at inn Modern piece of the kind !—'-plenty of snappy dialogue liberally interlaided with real shafts of humour and many originally-conceived situa- tions.

In a word, "The Cinema Star" is the ideal show for those in search of a really "merry and bright" evening, for the fun never flags from the first beat -of the conductor's baton to the final descent of the act drop, nor does the music for a single instant grow unmelodious or un- attractive.

Mr. Edgar Stanmore has charge of the out- standing part—that of Clutterbuck—and by his treatment thereof he proves himself to be a comedian of great resource and cleverness. He is seldom absent from the stage during the piece, and all the time he is on he keeps his audiences in jovial mood. Miss Lilian Gilbert appears as Louise, "the cinema star," and gives convinc- ing evidence of the fact that she is an artiste of no mean ability, possessing an attractive presence and an equally attractive voice. Miss Leslie Graham sings winsomely and dances daintily as Phyllis, and Miss Doris I'assmore is excellent as Cissie, the irresponsible film actress, whose penchant for making "the boys" en- deavour to satisfy her insatiable craving for tasty luncheon and recherche dinners creates much amusement. Mr. Edmund S. Phelps gets a lot of fun out of the part of Billy, the stutter- ing film-producer, and Mr. Horace Lingard is really clever and tremendously droll as Freddy, an actor of the old school. The rest of the cast is capably filled, and the chorus are well up to their work, whilst Mr. Frederick Rosso takes charge of the orchestra "The Cinema Star" is essentially one of those musical plays that should not be missed. Garvice Play Next Week.

There are two distinct features of the enter- tainment to be submitted to the patrons of the Royal Court Theatre next week. First is a welcome return visit of that popular actor, Mr. Sam Livesey, and secondly the play presented, "A Heritage of Hate," is from the pen of Mr. Charles Garvice, who is widely known amongst lovers of fiction. This is the first work of Mr. Garvice's that has undergone the process of dramatisation, but those who see "A Heritage of Hate," will certainly welcome a continuance of plays adapted from the popular author's works. It is a story full of real, live interest in which love and affection are much in evi- dence. The play enables one to note the ver- satility of Mr. Livesey,. who in the past has been identified with such robust parts as Rayne in "The King of Crime," and John Strong in "The Village Blacksmith." In "A Heritage of Hate," however, he is seen in an entirely new vein, and it is safe to predict a. most hearty welcome for the charming produc- tion, which drew crowded and enthusiastic audiences to the Aldwych Theatre, ,London, during its run there.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0087.txt | included=true | included =====
Expert Answers Queries At 'BILL" OF ALL-ROUND EXCEL- Tea-Table Conference. LENCE AT HIPPODROME.

"Problems of the Modern Sunday School" was the subject of an after-tea conference of the Wargington Sunday School Union, at the Wycliffe Memcrrial Building on Friday evening. About 120 Sunday school teachers were present, and questions asked by them were answered by Miss Emily Huntley, extension lecturer of the Sunday School Union. Opening the conference, Mr. Sohn Upson said that as Sunday school workers they were all conscious of their inipeifections. They; all knew of the ideal conditions of a Sunday school in

which the greatest possible good Would result from an organisation which was perfect, from a discipline which was admirable, and from methods which were of the very best and most modern. But they were up against failure in so many directions that at times they were some- what despondent. Such conferences as that were admirable in their purpose, and he be- lieved that they would be practical in their out- come.

Interesting The Old In The New. Answering a question as to what she con- sidered the best incentive to get the old type of Sunday school teacher interested in modern methods, Miss Huntley said that this was a psychological problem. She expressed her be- lief that the old type of teacher would never adopt—modern methods without sympathy, and added that meetings of Sunday School Union's were a great help in securing more modern methods.

Asked if ladies should teach boys under 12 years of age, Miss Huntley said that she could not make a rule about such a matter. Let them get a live, bright teacher; one who was interested in the things that boys of such an age were interested in, and then it did not matter whether the teacher was male or female. Miss Huntley also emphasised the need of using tho reading powers of boys in the junior schools. She laid down the doctrine that God did not make a mistake when He made children to be interested in things before they were in- terested in truths.

Referring to the necessity ,of having some link between Sunday and Sunday, Miss Huntley said that they must recognise that there was much in the world that attracted the children and chiefly because there was no religious training in the majority of homes, it was neces- sary for the Church to provide that link. Importance Of Missionary Instruction. She further dwelt on the importance of missionary instruction in all departments of the Sunday school. Touching upon the question of morning schools, she said that it was possible for the morning school to supplement the afternoon school in an extraordinary way. Asked if there should be a standard of educa- tional, attainment, for Sunday school teachers, Miss Huntley said that there was no excuse for teachers who did not make an effort to increase their knowledge of the work of teaching chil- dren about Biblical history and geography and missionary work. She would expect teachers to study at least three books every winter. At night a lecture-conference on "Lessons and Lesson-making" took place. The Mayor (Alderman Peter Peacock), who was in the 'chair, said he knew of no nobler work than that connected with Sunday schools and kindred organisations. The world required that this work should go on now more than ever it did.

Miss Huntley ,dealt with , her :subject at length. A lesson, she said, was like a tree. It must have. roots and movements and then spread out its branahes.. It must be whole and possess a unity. She also dealt with the choice of lessons and gave several valuable hints in the art of lesson-making.

A very good show. That is the general opinion expressed by habitues ,of the popular Macnaghten house this week, even in spite of the non-appearance through illness of Sammy Shields, the inimitable Scotch comedian. The first turn on the programme worthy of special note is Ella Moore, the "chocolate dandy coon," who renders her songs in ,very pleasing style. Arthur White and Co. are the premier at- traction in their " screaming absurdity," " The

Detective," in which Mr. White takes the pan of an amateur Sherlock Holmes. He is a comedian of the best ,type. His facial expres- sions, especially 'when he is interviewed by a lady client, being especially funny. Ably sup- ported by Wilcox Cadogan (his private secre- tary) and Clairette Keane, the " star " turn it, one of the best we have witnessed for some time. Another fine item is provided by the cele- brated Bernard Ash Quartet, which includes Miss May Hessian (a pupil of Sir Charles Santley) as soprano vocalist, Miss Maud Bell as 'cellist, Mr. Bernard Ash, himself, as sob violinist, and Mr. 'Victor Jellen, as the accom- panist. This pleasing quartet have during the week earned hearty and appreciative applause, especially for their rendering of the "Miserere" music from II Trovatore." The two sisters Junes give a representation of a Mexican rebel dance—which is quite out of the ordinary =besides contributing vocal items and other dances. Otto Viola provides a.sensational turn in his own particular way. Billed as a clam, he 'is also an athlete, for his adroitness whilst poised on three tables, two barrels and a chair literally makes One ,gasp. The daring enter- tainer earns ranch merited applause. Nellie Babbie, ably supported by a baritone, also con- tributes a worthy tarn to the evening's enjqy- ment, and the" Gaumont Graphic" eoncludes a programme which is quite up to the usual high standard expected of this variety house.

FIRE IN AN OFFICE.

Old Achdemv Has A Narrow Escape.

Shortly after six o'clock on Wednesday even- ing an employe of Messrs. Richmond and Co., Ltd., discovered that a fire , had broken out amongst a collection of old office books in the 'basement of. the offices in Academy-street. An alarm was immediately raised, and a number of employes got to work with buckets of water. They found it most difficult to get at the seal of the fire osvine to the dense volumes of smoke which were issuing forth, and they had to pro- tect themselves from, at any rate, partial suffocation by fastening handkerchiefs around their mouths. They had just succeeded in ex- tinguishing the, flames when the local fire brigade arrived on the scene, under the charge of Superintendent F. H. Turner., A chemical extinguisher was soon ,put into use upon the smouldering material, which was then quickly overturned and removed. A 'quantity of books, &c., were destroyed, and a portion of the ceiling was scorched. The building, which is part of the old Academy f historic fame, had it very narrow escape. The cause of the outbreak is thought to have beenr a defettiye flue.

SPLENDID PROGRAMMES AT STAR KINEMA.

Another series of good films were shown.4t the Star Kinema in the fere part of the week, the chief of the items being "Rags," a film in four parts, which featured the famous Mary Pick- ford, premiere among tinema artistes. The second episode of the "Exploits of Elaine" was quite as thrilling as the first, which is say- ing a great deal. This series forms one of the finest detective stories that has ever been filmed and each week its interest increases. " Pathes' Gazette" was quite topical, all the "latest news" being shown in picture form. Two sterling comic films were " Von "Pirpitz's Riding Place" and "What a Face" both of them screamers.

The week-end programme is every whit as good as that which delighted excellent audiences on the first three 'nights, fully maintaining the enviable reputation of the " Star " manage- ment for giving their ever-growing clientele that which is of the " up-to-datest " and best only.

FOUND DEAD ip ICE- BOUND PIT.

Yesterday, at Lostock, Northwich, Coroner Yates investigated the mysterious death of William Graham (44), of 235, Liverpool-road, Warrington. Deceased was found dead in an ie,p-bound pit on Wednesday. his head being above the water. The previous day he sought for work at Lostock. The police suggested that while crossing the field he accidentally fell into the pit and died from exhaustion, Deceased's brother said he wandered away from home on Saturday night. A verdict was returned of found dead, probably through cold and ex- haustion. •

===== 0002601_19151127_art0093.txt | included=true | included =====
TEACHER-SOLDIERS,

School Staff's Grand Response

To Call To Arms.

WHERE WARRINGTON LEADS.

. The patriotic part which Warrington school teachers are playing during the war was the sub- ject pf high commendation at the monthly meet- ing of the Education Committee, which was held at the Town Hall on Monday evening, Councillor Henry Roberts being in the chair. The minutes of the School Management Sub- committee set forth that it had been reported to them by a sub-committee of themselves that that body had met thirty-three head and assist- ant masters—two. more were unavoidably absent —of military age, and, after discussing the posi- tion of the latter, with regard to their enlist- ment in His Majesty's Forces, it was finally de- cided that the teachers should immediately -con- sider the matter and notify the 'Director .of Education (Mr. J. Moore -Murray) of their in- tentions. " The'Director," the minute in ques- tion went on to say, "stated that, of the 35 masters concerned, 34 had forwarded communi- cations in accordance with the foregoing deci- sion. Of these, 29 expressed their willingness to enlist under Lord Derby's scheme, 3 intimated that they had already been rejected, and 2 gave special reasons for not enlisting. It was de- cided, in view of the importance of the matter, to adjourn consideration of the matter to a -special meeting."

===== 0002601_19151127_art0094.txt | included=true | included =====
THE OFFER TO THE TEACHERS.

It was further resolved that leave of absence be granted to the undermentioned teachers dur- ing the period of their military service, and that their nominees be paid such SUMS as, with the total Army pay and separation allowances, if any, received from the Government, will make up an amount equal to the teachers' salaries for the time being: Mr. William H. Houghton (Hamilton-street Boys' School), and Mr. Andrew Malcolm (Heathside Boys' School). At the special meeting, the report continued, the sub- committee proceeded to the further considera- tion of the enlistment of head and assistant masters, and it was resolved that permission be given to all eligible head and assistant masters to enlist in the Reserve under Lord Derby's scheme, and that, in the event of their enlist- ment prior to the 11th December, 1915, they be entitled, if called up for service, to the full provisions of the Town Council's resolution of the Ist September, 1914.

TEACHER WHO GAINED A COMMISSION.

In moving the adoption of the minutes quoted above, Canon Stevenson stated that when the war broke out there were on the staffs of the schools 32 married men of military age, one of whom was a Territorial 'and offered himself for service at the beginning of the campaign. Since then ten of the remaining 31 had enlisted. Of the 26 unmarried men of military age, 15 had enlisted since the outbreak o the war. or the 21 married men who had not enlisted, twenty announced their intention of doing so, and of the eleven single men nine were going to offer them- selves. The two who did not intend to do so had, in the opinion of the sub-committee, very excellent and justifiable reasons for their course of action. The result of the appeal to the teachers was, he thought, one of which they might all feel very proud. Indeed, he believed that a larger percentage of teachers had en- listed from Warrington than from any other town of which the sub-committee had cogniz- ance. (Hear, hear.) It would, he was sure, interest the committee to know that one of their teachers—Mr. S. J. Price, of St. Mary's School— who enlisted in the 4th South Lancashire Regi- ment in September, 1914, when the first call for volunteers came, and who received promo- tion almost at once, had again, on November 6th, received promotion, having been gazetted quartermaster and lion, lieutenant in the 2nd- 4th South Lancashires. (Applause.) This was the first case of a Corporation employe being promoted from the ranks, and, as an Education Committee, they should feel very proud that such promotion was that of a gentleman who had been directly under their control. (Hear, hear.) Alderman Smethurst seconded the adoption of the minutes, and the committee passed them.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0095.txt | included=true | included =====
EXAMPLE BETTER THAN PRECEPT.

Alderman Bennett, in secohding, thought the figures which had been announced deserved just a passing comment. He was sure every member of the committee felt extremely proud that so many members of the staffs of the various schools had so nobly responded to the call of duty. That pride would, he knew, be shared by the whole of the inhabitants of the town. (Hear, hear.) They had very often been told that example was better than precept, and the teachers of the town had set a striking example of patriotism; not only before their scholars, but also before their fellow townsmen. A good deal had been said recently as to the teaching of patriotism in the schools. Surely the teachers of the town could not have inculcated the lesson better than by offering themselves as they had done,! The Mayor (Alderman Peter Peacock) said that he was very pleased to find that the teachers had responded so well under the difficult circum- stances which beset them, and if he might add a word it would be to the effect that he hoped the Corporation employes generally might' offer themselves in the same proportion. Then the Council might consider what they had to spare, and the Education CommittWs idea of making up allowances to the single men and married men might be followed—a course which would be so great an encouragement—and a proof that: so far as Warrington was concerned, neither Council nor Education Committee were lacking in their generosity in supporting the great move- ment for filling pp the ranks. He hoped that many firms in the town would follow the ex-. ample set them, for if they did so it would not only he an advantage to the town, but a particu- lar advantage to those who joined the Forces.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0096.txt | included=true | included =====
FINE RALLY OF THE SINGLE MEN.

Mr. J. Upson said that prior to Lord Derby's scheme being propounded 51 per cent. of the eligible teachers in the borough had enlisted. On Saturday he heard a great deal of satisfac- tion expressed by Oldham representatives that 25 per cent. of their teachers had enlisted. whilst another education authority were more than gratified because 30 per cent. of their teachers had joined up. With the exception of two, whose circumstances were such that they could not be expected to enlist, all the single teachers in Warrington had either enlisted or were to be attested under Lord Derby's reserve scheme. The whole of the married teachers— with three exceptions; and even they had pro- fessed their willingness, under conditions--had offered themselves; in fact, the response to the national appeal on the part of the Warrington teachers had been magnificent. (Hear, hear.) He had heard it whispered that there was little merit in the response made by the teachers, because there; had been pressure used to induce them to act as they had done. "I want to say here," continued Mr. Upson, "as the spokesman of the teachers, that from no quarter, nor from any teacher—and I have made exhaustive in- quiries—has there been the slightest suspicion of ,any,presgure being put on. (Hear, hear.) They have done what they have done voluntarily and readily, and no force has been 'exercised in any quarter to induce them to do what they have done. They have answered the country's call willingly and without pressure from any quar- ter." (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19151127_art0097.txt | included=true | included =====
A CANARD "SCOTCHED."

Mr. E. Plinston said he was glad, to hear the remarks that had been made by Mr. Upson, because it would appear that it had been be- lieved, or understood, by some people that pres- sure had been brought to bear, and that many of the teachers had been pretty well straightly told that if they did not offer their services there would be disabilities. "I am," said kr:.Plin- ston, "very pleased to hear and to accept the statement of Mr. Upson, for he is a gentleman on whose word I can rely." Councillor Pemberton thought the teachers of the town had evidenced a spirit of patriotism that might well be copied by others in the town. The Chairman cordially pgreed with every word which had been spoken about the patriotism of the teachers. Both the teaching staff and the official staff were equally to be admired for the manner in which they had come forward at the present juncture; indeed, so far as every class of the community was concerned, lie did not think there was any town in England that had a better record than had that old town of theirs in the matter of the re- sponse to the call of the country. .(Hear, hear.) The minutes *ere adopted.

Getting_ Colder.

The easterly winds ivhich prevailed last week brought colder weather with them, the mean temperature recorded in the borough being 82 degrees, or 10 degrees colder than the average temperature in the previous week. On the 16th inst. the temperature fell to 20 degrees. The 14th was the warmest day recently, the glass touching 40 degrees.

Mrs. Mayne, wife of the Commanding Officer of the 20th (Service) Battalion of the Lancs. Fusiliers (Colonel Mayne, D. 5.0.) is collecting comforts for the men of her husband's battalion,

===== 0002601_19151127_art0104.txt | included=true | included =====
Landlady's Sudden Death.

With painful suddenness the death occurred on Tuesday afternoon of Mrs. Martha'Bonnell, the wife of Mr. Fred Bonnell, the licensee of the Cattle Market Inn, Market-street. The deceased lady had been under the care of Dr. Anderson for some time and he was able to certify that her demise was due to heart failure; hence no inquest was necessary. The late Mrs. Bonnell was in her 59th year, and on Tuesday morning she appeared to be in her usual health and went about her customary household and business duties. She leaves three sons and six daughters to mourn her.

Working Men and District Nurses. The annual meeting of the Working Men's Committee, which yearly renders such valuable assistance to the funds of the Warriington Dis- trict Nursing Association, will be held in the Town Hall at 3 o'clock this afternoon. The financial statement to be presented to the meet- ing shows that the total remitted by the treasurer of the committee to the treasurer of the Association during the twelve months ended June 30th was £245 10s. 2d., as against £297 Bs. 10d. in the previous twelve months, when, it has to be remembered, there was no war. Be- tween £6OO and £7OO is requited for the annual upkeep of the Association, nearly the whole of Which has to be raised by voluntary efforts. Regular subscriptions of one penny monthly from all the working men and women in the district would realise about a half of the amount required.

- Education Committee Changes. At their meeting on Monday evening the members of the Warrington Education Com- mittee welcomed a new member and expressed regret at losing the services of an old one. The new member is the Rev. Father T. A. Hind, who succeeds the Rev. Father Neville Wilson as Rector of St. Mary's Roman Catholic Church, Buttermarket-street, and also as the representative of the Catholic Schools on the Committee.

The Chairman (Councillor H. Roberts) ex- tended the Committee's welcome, remarking that Father, Hind was a member of one of the oldest Warrington families, and the name of his father—and indeed the whole of his family —was held in respect in the town. They fur- ther welcomed him as the successor to one of the most useful and consistent clergymen they had, ever hdd amongst them. (Hear, hear.) The departing member was Mr. James Jar- man, whose term of office has expired.—The Chairman said the Committee regretted the severance, for Mr. Jarman had been an ex- tremely useful member of the Committee, hav- ing been most assiduous in his attendance at all their meetings. He would carry with him their best wishes for his future. (Hear, hear.) —The Finance Committee have passed a resolu- tion placing on record their unanimous appre- ciation of Mr. Jarman's services during the last three years. It was notified that the Revs. T. A. Hind and G. C. Jackson had been appointed foundation managers of• St. Alban's, St. Mary's and St. Benedict's Schools, in place of the Rev. Neville Wilson and the late Res. T.O. Swarbreek res- pectively. - -

Milk To Cost More.

The Warrington and District Milk Dealers' Association announce that "owing to the far- mers demanding a further advance," they are compelled to raise the retail price of milk to 41d. per quart, as from to-morrow.

The three-day sale of work held in connection with Emmanuel Church. Bold-street, last week, resulted in the highly creditable total of £127 48. id. being raised for church purposes. The takings on the last day amounted to £39 48. id.

Rev. H. B. Firth Recovering.

Last week the Rev. IL B. Firth, the Vicar of St. James', Latchford, was operated upon for appendicitis. We learn that the operation was successfully performed and that the reverend gentleman is now making good progress to- wards recovery.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====
Fewer Births; More Deaths.

During the week ended November 20th, 39 births were recorded in Warrington and 29 deaths, the comparative figures for last year being 48 and 25 respectively. Seven of the deaths notified last week were those of babies under one year old and nine those of persons over 65. On an estimated population of, 75,532 the death-rate for the week was 19.9, as against 17.3 in the corresponding period of last year, and the birth-rate 26.8, as compared with 33.3.

School Leaving-Age.

The Warrington Educatron -Committee on Monday evening endorsed the following resolu- tion passed by the School Management Sub- committee :—" This committee, having heard with much concern that proposals are being made for the lowering of the age at which chil- dren may be exempted from school, proposals which, if carried out, will be gravely inimical, more especially at this time, to the vital in- terests of this country, desires respectfully to urge upon the Home Secretary and the Presi-. dent of the Board of Education, the pressing necessity for the retention of the present law in respect of the exemption of children from attendance at school.'' Conies of the resolution will he forwarded to the Home Secretary, the President of the Board of Education, and Mr. Harold Smith, M.P. ,

Warrington Photographic Society. " Cheshire ' was the title of a lecture given to the members of the Warrington Photo- graphic Society by Mr. C. Wileockson, their hon. treasurer, on Tuesday evening. The chair was taken by the President (Mr. A. Davis). The Lecturer took those present, by the aid of some fine lantern slides, over the picturesque, if somewhat flat country, showing Eaton Hall, Delamere Forest, the Weaver Valley. White- gate, Beeston Castle, Port Sunlight, and. nearer home, High Legh, Grappenhall, and the Ackers, with its swans. Pleasing little pictures taken on the high and by-roads to these places were shown, as were also several groups of the , members of the Photographic Society and • Cycling Club taken during official visits, show- • , ing members of both who are now serving with the colours in various parts of the war zone. fik. At the eloSe* Mr. Plinston and Mr. Rowlands expressed 'the thanks of the meeting to Wilcockson. " Buffs' " Motor Ambulance. A noticeable feature of the Mayor's proces- sion on Mayor's Sunday was the R.A.0.8. Motor Ambulance. It was not, however, generally known that it hail been used during the pr.-. ceding night to convey 162 of our wounded soldiers, and that without any rest the follow- ing Southport brothers brought it on, in ful- filment of a promise made to Knight Holden, in order to take its place in the procession:— The Right Hon. "Sir" Wilfred B. Rawson, ROIL, K. 0.5., P.P.0.P., P.G. Sec., P.G.Ch. (England), accompanied by "Sir". Thomas Richards, K.0.M.,' K.G.11., P.G.P.; " Sir " Peter RiNnmer, K.0.M.; "Sir" Frederick Taylor, K.0.M., and Bro. Stafford. The car is 20 named "Vulcan," and was built by the Vulcan Motor Co., Ltd., Southport, the same firm hav- ing supplied to the Red Cross over 200. It carries four stretchers, attendant and driver, and will accommodate eight wounded; full com- plement 10 passengers. The cost of the car was ~e 450. the whole amount having been raised by the Brethren of the Lancashire, Cheshire and 1.0. M. Province. Clearly the Buffaloes are living up to their watchwords—" Justice, Truth and Philanthropy." A more satisfactory method of doing one's bit than endeavouring to relieve the sufferings of our wounded war- riors could not be conceived. Old *Warrington Cloekmakers.

Old Warrington—Alderman Bennett in- formed the local Class Teachers' Association in the course of an interesting paper which he recently read to them—was quite renowned for its distinguished watchmakers. Here resided John Harrison, the inventor of the compensat- ing pendulum, who won, in 1767, the sum of £20,000 from the Government for nding corrector mode of ascertaining longitude. A little earlier there lived in the town John Kay, another watch and clockmaker, who, in con- junction with Richard Arkwright—who resided in Warrington for some time—invented the fly shuttle. Warrington too, gave shelter to Peter Litherland, the inventor of the patent lever- watch. But these are not the only horological inventors that Warrington can boast, for, on the threshold of the Civil War, there saw the light in it a famous Benedictine Monk called Edward Booth. or Barlow, as he dubbed himself, in honour of his godfather, a Benedictine Monk of that name who died, Lancaster, for his religion. This Booth read Euclid with as much avidity as most people read a good story, and he was the first to make the pendulum watch and clock, as well as the repeating watch, and left behind him several learned treatises on meteorology and other sub- jects.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0116.txt | included=true | included =====
LAST CHANCE OF THE VOLUNTARY SYSTEM.

Men Offer In Greater Numbers, But Many Still Hold Back.

The men of military age and fitness in the Town appear to be at last fully awakening toAthe fact that Lord 'Derby's recruiting scheme is u serious business, and that Unless they avail themselves of the privilege which it affords them " coming in" as volunteers, they, in all probability, will have to rally to the Colours as .conscripts—in which case they will not only be deprived of . the honour Which attaches to voluntary service, but may also, it has been broadly hinted, suffer other actual disabilities. There has been a decided fillip, so the can- vassers report, during the past week, and -fatuous excuses have been fewer and farther between, though there still remains plenty of misunderstanding to clear• up. For obvious reasons no definite figures' are yet available, but it may safely be said That whilst the number of men who have signified their readiness to join up is decidedly encouraging—as compared with the total for the previous week—there is tll room for improvement—an improvement which must be forthcoming if the British Army is to remain an army of free men and not to become one of forced men.

word of warning. "Lots of men," he said, "have got it into their heads that mice they have stated their case ,here, they are finished with, but it is not so. The final arbitrament rests with the Tribunal, which is part and parcel of- the scheme; indeed, it is a most im- portant part. But it is not likely that the Tribunal is going to waste its time over, the =Jen who has not first 'shown willingness."

===== 0002601_19151127_art0117.txt | included=true | included =====
WHAT VERDICT ?

ot un S'stein On us Last Trial.

MANY COMING, BUT MORE MUST,

There are signs that the eligible men of War- rington are appreciating more and more the 3ieeds, aye! and the seriousness of the situation, and as a consequence there is something akin to rejoicing in'the local Recruiting Office, which is at 3, Horsemarket-street—wher6 daily Messrs. Ernest Sanders.and Herbert Haves—the secretaries of the local committee—ancra staff •of helpers—some or them khaki-clad—organise and superintend the task of discovering those who ought to be serving their King and coun- try, but, up to the present, have not announced their intention of doing so. During the last few days the, response to the pleas and argu- ments of the canvassers has been decidedly en- couraging, but still there is ,room for improve- ment. The following extract from the daily Press; perhaps, crystallises the position: Lord Derby, addressing members of the 'London Stock `Exchange on Wednesday, said that men were coming in tinder the recruiting scheme in large numbers, but they mist come in in very much larger .numbers during the next three weeks if -the position of the voluntary system was to be made absolutely unassailable.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0118.txt | included=true | included =====
INDISPENSABLE APPEALS ONLY FROM ENLISTED MEN.

- An imi)ortant communication has come to 'hand from headquarters during the week, and it is 'to the effect' that no claim for exemption— on the grounds of indispensability shall be 'brought before the, tribunal, with whom the final arbitrament rests, unless- such claim is -raised by a man who has been attested and who has. been placed in a grade under Section B '(Army Reserve).., Consequently it behoves every man wito calls at 3, Horiemarket-street, to give his reasons to be first attested at one of the recruiting offices in the town and placed among the reserves'. His case will then come before the Advisory Committee, who will have the assistanve of a representative from the War .office. ' When his case is considered, the indus- try to which he belongs will be asked to send :a representative to urge their point of view. It -will then be left to the Tribunal, acting on the .advice of the Advisory Committee through its War Office representative to decide as to whether any eligible man's servii!es to his cniployer are 'indispensable or not. The point is made clear in a written answer published in the report of Wednesday's Par- liamentary debate, in which answer Mr.. Ten- nant informed Colonel Alan Sykes that it is 'essential that every man of eligible. age, em- ployer or employe alike, who considers himself Indispensable to his particular trade or busi- ness should be duly attested before being allowed to appeal to the local tribunal to adjudicate upon, his case for exemption or deferment to a later class. The' latest date for so enlisting is December 11.

'PROOF OF INDISPENSABILITY WANTED. Before a man can be classed us indispensable good reason will have to be shown; such, for instance, as that the husiness in which he is employed cannot be properly carried on it he is called up for actual military service with his group; or that the employer has made every effort temporarily to replace the man, and that he has given reasonable facilities for enlistment , to every man of suitable age whom he might have in his employ. Too much stress cannot be laid upon the ser- vice that men of eligible age, who feel that they are not called upon to go, will be rendering if they will only call in at the Recruiting Office and state their reasons for refraining from offer- ing themselves. It would ease the work of the •canvassers immensely and expedite. the comple- tion of the canvass—already somewhat behind, though canvassers have come forward in larger .numbers during the past few days, with the re- -snit that a good deal of leeway is being made up. Many men of the prescribed age have 'already saved themselves and the recruiting -agents, &c., trouble by calling in at the office •and advancing their reasons why they should not immediately "join up," and in 90 per cent. 'if such cases it has been found that -the reasons advanced are worthy of consideration. This hint applies to "budged" men, as well as to the unbadged."

===== 0002601_19151127_art0119.txt | included=true | included =====
A RUSH OF MARRIED MEN.

Since Lord Derby, in the parlance, has some- what "cleared the air," there has, been a rush of married men to offer themselves, but the can- vassers are still all too frequently met, when they call upon an eligible who happens to be a Benedict, with the answer: "I will go when the single men have gone!" And that in spite of the fact that the position has been made as clear as daylight, viz., that if it comes to com- pulsion it will be a case of single men first! If ,only people would take the trouble even to -glance at their daily papers and get .a bang of -what is afoot, the canVassers and the office staff would be saved a lot of time and energy—time 'and energy which they could vastly more pro- 'fltably employ.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0120.txt | included=true | included =====
WHERE ARE THEY?

Summarised, the report for the week, so far as Warrington is concerned, is to the effect that the number of potential-recruits is considerably on the increase. "nut," added one of the offi- ,cials in charge of the work, "we are still wait- _ing for many who have, in the past, advocated • the voluntary system at street corners and else- -where, to show their faith in that system by coming forward and helping to ~,,eserve it, for this is the last chance of voluntaryism, and if this chance is allowed to go a-begging, there will be nothing else for it but methods of com- pulsion." There is a feeling abroad that time and money are being wasted in attempting to persuade men to "join up," and that the war would be finished very much more quicklii and that that time and money could be -devoted in other important directions, if the word "must" were substituted for the appeal- ing "will"!

Some men, for reasons special to themselves. may, before joining the •forces, require time to .enable them to release themselves from their -civil obligations. Such men may apply to the .competent authority for a longer time to be :allowed to elapse before they "join up" than would be allowed were they called up with their -group. Reasons that may be regarded as suffi- cient are that such men will require time to make the necessary arrangements with regard to the conduct of their businesses, or that time will be required to adjust their financial or -domestic arrangements. Each case of this kind will bp decided on its merits,

===== 0002601_19151127_art0122.txt | included=true | included =====
THE MANUFACTURERS' STAND- POINT.

A Justifiable Grumble:

The Warrington manufacturer, who is en- gaged upon war work, has some cause to feel aggrieved even in these days when grievances ought to be buried. To be hustled by a Govern- ment Department for goods urgently needed for the Front, and to be deprived of the men neces- sary to meet that. hustle with anything like success, constitute conditions that most people will admit justify the grumble. In the early days of the war, before the Gov- ernment made their big organised demand•upon the resources of the works of the country, a good percentage of our workers enlisted. When. the order for material and more material was given, it was found that the number of men left was insufficient to give the deliveries re- quired. In an odd quiet moment this week an "Ex- aminer" representative interviewed the head of a large local manufactory of war material. "With recruiting agents still worrying for :more men, and with the best will in the world to help the country," he was told, "the manu- facturer of munitions colt only see further trouble and delay ahead.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0123.txt | included=true | included =====
Worrying.

"A• very grave fact is: that in most munition factories, a considerable part of the labour is that of experienced and skilled men whom it would be impossible io replace—with anything like the same degree of efficiency—for a matter of months, and in some cases years. That means, of course, that the materials of manu- facture are likely to be less suitable in quality all round. • _

"It is felt .by manufacturers that if some recruiting officers appreciated these facts they would divert their efforts to businesses and trades, particularly in the Warrington district, which are not necesSary for the supply of war materials. These businesses, however, are on much smaller lines than those engaged on munition ntanufacture and, consequently, are not so easily noticeable, They are possibly less permeated by patriotism than places where large numbers of hard working men are gathered to- gether, as in the big local iron works and similar establishments.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0124.txt | included=true | included =====
Regular and Territorial Recruiting.

"It should be noted," continued our inform- ant, "that there are - two main recruiting agencies in the 'district, one for the Regular Army and one for the Territorials. The head of the Regular agency gives every considera- tion to the factories engaged in munitions by retui,ning their men who may apply to enlist. The head of the Territorial recruiting agency, while apparently equally considerate, is pre- sumably handicapped• by superior orders. It is therefore necessary for firms wishing to keep their men to go to a great deal of trouble to prevent them being taken for the Territorial

branch. "These matters were discussed at a recent meeting of Warrington manufacturers, but it may be that they did not quite clearly under- stand the ,difference between firms dealing with ordinary trade and firms dealing direct with war material. If matters get more strained in re- gard to recruiting, a line of distinction should be more definitely drawn in the operations of the recruiting officers."

New Conditions.

These conditions existed three weeks ago. "The present situation," said the manufac- turer, " is that the recruiting agents are civPian canvassers. They should also.discriminate with regard to men employed. A well-known works manager who is over the military age, mar-:e4, and, of course,thas huge re.-; onsibilities n i•tm- nection with his Government work, has twice approached by an enthusiastic recruiter; and absurd as his case may seem, it is by no means an exceptional case."

===== 0002601_19151127_art0125.txt | included=true | included =====
RECRUITING OFFICE EXPLA NA-

TION.

Al) Unenlisted Men Have To Be Canvassed.

With reference to the manufacturers' grumble referred to above, an official attached to the local Recruiting Office in florsemarket- street, pointed out to an " Examiner " man that it was the duty of the canvassers to go to every man, whether he, is indispensable to the employer or not. No man will be made a soldier if his employer can satisfy the proper authority that snch man is indispensable to his business. Each employer will have the case of his employes considered in a bunch, and the Advisory COmmittee, which is being set up, will meet that employer at his works and will give him every opportunity of proving that this. man Or that man of military age cannot be spared. "it is," added ,our informant, "because the , ' starring ' business has been so badly done and that men who ought to have been 'starred ' have not been 'starred,' that the .present can- vassing is absolutely necessary. This canvass has got to be completed whether the employer likes it. or not. The badged ' men are to be, and ought to be, canvassed, and they may be attested for the Reserve on condition that they return to their several employments. They can- not then leave, without coming before the Tribunal and their employers being consulted." The latest. instructions from "those above" set out that, in no case ,are men wearing the official war badge to be approached in the pub- lic streets by' canvassers, but canvassers "will, of course, call at the house of every person for whom there is a blue card." •' "This," our representative was assured, "includes every Iknown person, no matter what the employers may say."

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAYS NOVEMBER 27, 1915.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0130.txt | included=true | included =====
THE WAR NO PICNIC!

Captain Fairclough's Appeal To Single

Men.

At a recruiting meeting at Market Gate on Saturday evening, Captain Fairclough declared that he did not wish to speak on behalf of conscription, if the war could be won on the voluntary system. If that could be done, it would be "a fine feather in our caps." The war was no picnic, and the finest of the manhood that the nation could produce was wanted for the Army. He appealed to every -single young man to consider the matter and to show his willingness to carry on the glorious traditions of the country.

WHY GREECE DALLIES!

Referring to the position in the Balkans, Alderman Bennett gave it as his opinion that Greece was waiting to see which way the cat jumped. Unfortunately, owing to the hesitation of our statesmen—and little hesitations were in- separable from politics—it seemed as though Serbia was to be left in the lurch, but he be- lieved that it was the intention of every Eng- lishman to see that Belgium, France and Serbia were restored to freedom. England had won her great Empire by fair fight and exploration. The reason that men came from all quarters of the globe—from Australia, New Zealand, Canada and other places to help her—was that they were fully conscious of the fact that under the British flag they could develop and be free, happy and prosperous. (Applause.)

RECREATION FUNDS.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0133.txt | included=true | included =====
Pressing Needs Of Our Terri- torials In Training.

To the Editor of " The Examiner."

Sir,—May I ask for the assistance of your valuable paper to call attention again to the urgent need of funds and goods in kind to pro- vide recreation for the Wjrrington soldiers stationed at Blackpool?

The officers and men are very grateful for the assistance given on our last appeal, but the need still exists.

At the 'present,time there are about five hun- dred and fifty men here, all from Warrington and district, and recruits and invalided men are reporting every week.

_ The -pressing need is to obtain equipment to enable the Battalion to enter creditably for the Divisional Competitions in boxing, cross- country, running and football, which are to be held in December.

At the same time, gifts of any kind suitablc for recreation will be most acceptable, and should be addressed `to the President of the Sports Committee. Yours faithfully, *, F. TYMMS (2nd Lieutenant), President Regimental Sports Committee. 3/4141 South Lancashire Regiment, Blackpool, 24th November, 1915.

Well-known Tobacco Firm's Christmas Offer.

The Christmas parcels traffic this year promises to be heavier than usual. Hundreds of thousands of parcels will be sent to the Navy, to France, Belgium, Turkey and Serbia, and in view of this fact, it is desirable that orders should be placed as early as possible. There is one thing that nearly every soldieriat the Front likes almost above everything else, and, that is a cigarette. It often occdrs in the trenches that when the men are "hard up" for a smoke they make their own cigarettes out of tea leaves and bits of newspaper. Nothing, there- fore, will be more appreciated by " Tommy " this Cliristmas than a gift of cigarettes. The British-American Tobacco Co., Ltd., are offer- ing unique facilities to those who desire to send gifts to the men. They are in a position t.l fit a soldier up with 280 " Woodbine " cigarettes for 35., 280 "Gold Flake" cigarettes for 55., 1,900 " Woodbine " cigarettes for 88., or 500 " Player's Navy Cut" cigarettes for Bs. 6d. If Ithe cigarettes are to be sent to a prisoner .of war in 'Germany, the fee will be is, less as there is no postage to pay. A Christmas card will be enclosed in each parcel. Orders may be given to any tobacconist or direct to the British-American 'Tobacco Co.. Ltd., Ez. Dept.. Weetminster

PRIVATE X. POOLE. Died of Wounds.

vine. The news was conveyed to his mother, who resides at 22, Milner,street, on Thursday night by means of a telegram, sent from the Records Office at Shrewsbury, and reading as follows :

"I deeply regret to inform you that C 2, Stationary Hospital, Abbeville, reports the death of 14324 Private J. Poole, of the 9th Battalion, Cheshire Regiment, on November 25th, from a gunshot wound to the skull."

===== 0002601_19151127_art0138.txt | included=true | included =====
Twice Operated On,

Since the day Private Poole was wounded, Nurse J. Todd, at the hospital, has been in constant touch with Mrs. Poole. A short while ago the nurse wrote that he had under- gone a second operation, and that his condition was slightly better. He had a relapse, how- ever, and she wrote on November 21st,. as follows: _

"Your dear son's condition is no better. There is no fresh complication, so far as we know, and there is no improvement." • A Keen Cyclist.

• Private Poole, who was 22 years of age,' had been in the Army about fourteen months. As a boy he attended the Ileathside School, and previous to the war was employed by Messrs. Crosfield and Sons, Ltd. He was a keen wheeler and was a member of the St. Luke's Cycling Club, and of the Ancient Order of Shepherds.

The following convalescent soldiers have arrived this week at the Thelwall Heys Mili- tary Convalescent Home from the Lord Derby Military Hospital :—Lance-Corporal Caton, Ist Essex; Private Niven, Highland Light Infantry; Driver Thomas Murray, R.F.A.; Private Quin- ney, Royal Warwick; Private 11. ===== 0002601_19151127_art0142.txt | included=true | included =====
TWO ENJOYABLE MNCERTS FOR WHITECROSS PATIENTS.

The soldiers under treatment in the White- cross Hospital were entertained on Saturday by Miss Marie Houghton, assisted by the Misses D. Read, Olive Dale and-May Cook, and Messrs. Charles _Lambert and G. Walker. Every item was vociferously encored by a most enthusiaf- tic audience

The patients and the inmates of the White- cross Military Hospital and the institution to which it is attached were provided with a grand' concert on Thursday evening. The dining hall was well filled when the popular band of the 2nd South Lancashire Regiment, under the able conductorship of Mr. R.. E. Cahill, opened the evening's entertainment with a sprited military march. From then on to nine p.m. Miss Marie Houghton. Mr. Charles Lambert and the band kept the audience in a good humour with songs, recitations and neketions. Niiss Houghton sang several songs, in her mud finished and dainty manner, accompanied by the band, and received a hearty reception. Mr. Lambert was very entertaining with his hum- orous recitations and humorous songs. Mr. Cahill played popular soldiers' chorus songs, the wounded lads joining in right heartily. A most enjoyable evening was spent by everyone, and at the close of the concert Mr. Loxtrant heartily thanked Miss Houghton, Mr. Cahill and Mr. Lambert for their kindness and for the pleasure they had given to all who were , present. Mr. J. Beddard, who was responsible , for the arrangements both for this concert and the preceding one, was thanked for his ser- vices.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0143.txt | included=true | included =====
"MR. ATKINS," ARTIST,.

Prizes For Wounded Christmas Card Painter's.

A few weeks ago the public of Warrington were shown what the wounded soldiers at Had- don Court Hospital could do in the way of entertainments; and on Tuesday afternoon ample evidence was given of their abilities in another direction. Some time ago a competi- tion was organised amongst the patients, and prizes were offered for the best hand-painted Christmas cards. The competition was divided into three sections, viz., original designs, copied designs and cards with regimental crests. A good number of cards were sent in and these were exhibited on Tuesday afternoon. Exhibits Said By Auction.

After the exhibition the cards, with a num- ber of pictures (painted by Sergeant Bryan at the hospital) and articles of needlework, were offered for sale, the "auctioneer" being Private Johnson.

An encouraging number of buyers were pre- sent and good prices were secured. More than one Christmas card realised 28. 6d.

The Prize-winners.

The cards were judged by Mrs. Charlton Parr and Miss Broadbent, the prizes being allocated as follows:

Original Designs.-1, Private Whorlow; 2, Private Gowan; 3, Private McLeod; 4, Private Williams; 5, Private Jowsey. Copied Designs.-1, Corporal Mapeston; 2, Corporal Molyneux; 3, Rifleman Oakes; 4, Pri- vate Cox; 5, Private Palmer; 6, Private How- arth; 7, Corporal Tipper. ' Cards With Regimental Crests.-1, Sergeant Major Northcote; special Ist, Corporal Moly- neux• 2, Private Bramwell; 3, Private Wild; 4, Private Green. A special prize was also given to Private Murray, who has only one arm, for the in- genuity he had 'displayed in his workmanship..

CHRISTMAS CHEER.

Parcels For South Lancashire Prisoners Of War.

The second dispatch, consisting of 120 Christ- mas parcels to another section of the men of the South Lancashire Regiment who are prisoners of war in various German camps, was made on Wednesday, and, thanks to the kindness of the, teachers and ptpils of the School Cookery Centres, cakes, made by the pupils, were onelosed in the parcels. There are still about •MO parcels to be dispatched, and funds are urgently needed to keep the imprisoned men reminded that, though absent in exile, they are not forgotten by the people of the Depot town of the regiment.

Poetic Tribute To V.C.'s Gallantry.

Piper Daniel Laidlaw. V.C.. of the K. 0.5.8. —the idol of the Lord Derby Hospital at Win- wick—has since the publication of the details of the gallant act which won him the coveted Cross, been the recipient of letters from all parts of the kingdom—some. of course, written by admiring relatives and friends, hut the great majority by absolute strangers, who, neverthe- less, admire his, wonderful bravery and presence of mind in "piping the hove to the charge '• ond keening on piping until he was wounded in the ankle and forced to hobble back to the trench. _

One of the tributes is in verse. It Wfif; T/Pnnell by an old friend of the Piper's—Mr. T. W. Little, of Ilexhain7-and runs as follows:--

Row brave and calm while' mounted high, Conspicuous to every eye, PlaYing his pipes with lusty zeal, And how it roll'd and made appeal To the Xing's Own Scottish Borderers there, And made them rush to do and dare.

swift to the fight they dash'd along, Braced grandly by the piping song "Blue Bonnets over the Border." Well On the laddies rash'd pelt melt, And won the hill they were striving for, Just as a shell ray ankle tore.

And "The standard on the fteights of Mar" Ilearten'd the laddies and took them far. Didn't I play my best that day? Didn't I skirl and skirl away? Limping along, feeling no pain— And I'm going back to sound the strain. T. W. LITTLE.

BANDSMAN SWIFT TRANSFERRED.

Fine Cornet Player Who Was Wounded At Neuve Chapelle.

Bandsman Horace Swift, who until the be- ginning of this week has been a cornet player in the 2nd South Lancashire Band, has been trans- ferred to the band of the newly-formed Welsh Guards. Bandsman Swift wawa member of the South Lancashire Regiment for about twelve and a half years. He was wounded at Nenve Chapelle, and since his return has been at the Depot at Orford-lane, Warrington. He left Warrington on Tuesday.

WAR-WORKER PRAISES ZIAI-DUK.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0152.txt | included=true | included =====
Saved From Disablement By Famou,

Healer.

A Leeds man, now on Government work, would have been permanently incapacitated years Ago had it not been for Zam-Buk. It was a striking narrative Mr. C. H. Hamilton told a reporter at his home, 11, .Firnley7street, Meanwood-road, Leeds.

"When at the tannery," said Mr. Hamilton, z" I wear clogs. About 10 years ago, the hard edge of ray clog grazed my left ankle. The place got very swollen and grew rapidly, worse. Large 'blebs' formed. These burst, and a lot of matter oozed out. With the raw flesh ex- posed I could only, limp painfully about. I tried no end of ointments, salves, and suchlike, but the lower part of my leg became one mass of unsightly, highly-inflamed sores. The irrita- tion was so bad that I could have torn the skin to the bone. At last I had to give up work. "Although I- kept on with doctor's dressings and hst- fomentations, the inflammation in my leg did not go down: The swelling spread and the buining pain nearly drove me mad. "For weeks L was laid np, vainly, hoping that rest would help the sores to heal. My whole system was affected; I got as weak as a baby. Sleepless nights, and the dread that I should never be well again, added to my misery. Month after month I got worse. "By a happy chance, a friend in the next ,street told me "to try Zam-Buk-. I dressed my leg with the balm and at once felt easier. The swelling above my knee went • down, and the whole limb looked and felt better. Of, course, I kept on with Zam-Buk which dried up the discharge and cleansed the ulcers. Then the raw places were healed by Zam-Buk in a per- fectly 'natural and thorough manner. Zam-Buk literally' put me on my feet again. For years I have been back at work, and despite long hours have never had the slightest trouble with my leg since Zam-Buk so splendidly healed it."

Don't trust to ointments and salves without any medicinal merit. While you are expeii- menting with such coarse and useless things, the disease only gets firmer. Get Zam-Buk,l which has more sterling medicinal merit than any other known preparation for the skin. Prices: 1/.lf plus 11d. war tax for small, or 2/9 plus 3d. war-tax for large size.
