FOUR FATALITIES.

Daughter's Sad Discovery !n The Early Morning.

Mr. S. Brighouse, the Warrington Coroner,' held four inquests on Saturday morning- at the Borough Police ..Court, and in each case a verdict of "Accidental death" was returned. The first inquest was on the body of Janet Rowe, aged 68—widow of Jacob Rowe. a ship's steward—who resided at 211, Knasford-road; Warrington, and died' at 8.25 a.m. on November 17th.

Mrs. Mary Grose, a daughter of the deceased, said that it was her mother's usual custom to get up early in the morning, call the husband of witness, and go back to bed. On the 17th the witness did not hear the 'deceased do this, and on going downstairs she^ found her mother un- conscious. A doctor walr sent for and it was found that she was suffering from a fracture at the base of her skull. She died the same morn- ing.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0154.txt | included=true | included =====
Boy Burned To Death.

The second inquest was on the-body of Joseph Caddick, aged 8 years and 5 months, the son of Thomas Caddick, an ironworker, of 20, Melville-street, Warrington. Mrs. Caddick stated that on ,the 11th instan• she came downstairs, leaving the deceased with three other children in bed. At about 9.10 a.m. she went to a shop, and on her return she was met by Joseph, who was running towards her with his flannel shirt on fire. The flames were put out. He was taken to the Infirmary and died on the 17th. It was surmised that Joseph was standing near the fireguard and a spark from the fire caught his hirt. Death Follows Fracture.

The third inquiry was into the circumstances attending .the death of Martha Wicks, aged 85 years, the widow of George Wicks, who resided at 9, Powell-street. Miss- Wicks, a daughter of the deceased, said that on October 12th her mother slipped on the kitchen floor and complained of a great pain in her right thigh. She was examined by a doctor. who found • that the thigh was broken. The deceased died on November: 16th.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0155.txt | included=true | included =====
Aged Invalid's Terrible Fate.

The other inquest was on Mrs. Mary Hannah Greenhalgh, aged 69, of 10, Rodney-street, who as reported in our last issue, died as the result of burns.

The evidence showed that seven years ago Mrs. Greenhalgh had a stroke. and had been a helpless invalid ever since. It was her (Its- tora`to sit by the fireside in an armchair. On , Friday, November 19th, her daughter Mary went out of the house to do some shopping, leaving her mother alone in the house. Open- ing the street door, upon her return, she founi the house full of smoke. She rushed into the house, only to find that her mother was dead. The charred clothes fell from the body as it was touched.

Self-esteem is a good thing, but lots of people have more of it than they are entitled to.

By Another Name.

First Seaman: " I say, Bill, wot's this card?"

Second Seaman: "That's the saloon passen- gers' menu, of course." "But wot does it mean?"

"Oh, it's a list of all the things they have for dinner—soup and fislrialid rvegetables and meat. That's the difference between the saloon and the forecastle; they get them all separately and call it a menu. In the fore- castle we get them altogether and call it Irish

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY NOVEMBER 27, 1915.

not had .someone in this terrible strife. There is a church in the village which has been badly shelled, and also a good Many bUildings. Shell holes abound everywhere, and nearly every day one hears of someone being laid to rest. Many of us when in the trenches hear voices calling for the stretcher-bearers which makes you think whether it is your turn next. At the end of the day it makes one sure that there is a Higher Power watching over you. "It makes us all different, the old times have changed, and one only prays that it may last on after the war. If it does England will be a new place. When we are out of the trenches we go, so many at a time to the baths for a het bath and change of clothing, and I can assure you that we are thankful for the opportunity. After that we are put on fatigue work, filling sand- bags. We do all these things with a good heart, for after all war is war, and when it is all over we hope to be able to say that we have done our share for King and country. If the single men at home would only shoulder their responsi- bility they would not hesitate one moment about ioining. but would come like Englishmen and help their brothers at" the Front." Private Clare attended the Oughtrington Parish Chureb. and is well-known , and respected in the im- mediate vicinity .to where he resided.

Illness of Mr. Owen Davis.—The residents in the district will regret to hear of the •illness of Mr. Owen Davis., who resides at High Legh. The sick Man is highly 'esteemed ,and respected and is an ardent worker in connection with the Working Men's 'Committee.

Skating On The Dom.—The popular winter pastime of skating was much enjoyed, by a large number of the local inhabitants On Sun- day, for the severe-trost of the •week,end.made 'skating on the Dam possible. Thtre were many •onlookers lining the, banks who -would not venture on -the ice, however, who evidently were .preoccupied with the thoughts of the recent skating. disaster in Cheshire.

Corporal F. Moss Home on Furlough.—Cor- poral Fred Moss, of Broomedge, paid a visit to his home on Saturday whilst on a short leave from the firing, line. He is a member of the local " TerriW,", and was drafted out to the Front in Febrithry. He is' now attached to the niachine-gun section, and has witnessed some thrilling and weird experiences whilst serving the Colours.

He returned to the Front on Thursday and was accorded an enthusiastic send off by his many ardent admirers.

„;-Sergeant S. W. Lewis at: .Chiseldon Military Hospital—Many of our readers, will be sorry to hear that Sergeant S. W. Lewis, of the Ist Cheshire Regiment, is lying seriously ill at the Military Hospital, Chiseldon Camp, near,Swin- don, Wilts. /He is suffering from shock, result- ing from burns. It will be remembered -ERA' Sergeant Lewis answered his country's call at the commencement of the war, taking part in many stirring engagements, including the Battles of Mons and the Aisne. He was in tho firing-line a little over six weeks, when he was invalided home through illness. Sergeant Lewis, who, we hope, will speedily recover, resides at Rush Green, and, previous to en- listing, was employed by Mr. F. E. Evans, chemist, The Cross, Lymm. Motor Skids Through Hedge.—An alarming motor accident occurred on Tuesday night on the Booth's Hill road, in the immediate vicinity of Deckficlds Bridge. A heavy motor wagon belonging to Messrs. Faulder, chocolate manu- facturers, was proceeding towards Altrincham, when, owing to the fog, the driver failed to observe a large, piece of scrap-iron which had apparently fallen from a' passing cart, with•the , result that the mator crashed into it, causing it to skid from the road, through the hedge, and into a ploughed field. The motor was ex- tensively damaged about the ‘-be'nnet and had to be towed by a passing steam wagon for re- pairs. Happily the driver and his assistant escaped injury. Private John Leigh.—Private John Leigh, of the 6th' Battalion South Lancs. 'Regiment, has written home to his wife, who resides at -16, Moston-street, Newfields, that he is at present lying ill in No. 15 Ward, St. Paul's 'Military Hospital, Malta. In a communication dated November 15th, he stated: "I have been very ill in the trenches, so they sent' me. to the hos- pital ship, which .eventually landed at Malta last night. I. have got yellow jaundice, but am a little better now, although still weak and thin. We are' comfOrtable here, and are well lo6ked after, so I think I shall soon be all . right. Private Leigh enlisted upon the out- break of hostilities. - He was employed at MiSFirs William Robertson's Ltd. Tube Works, Latch ford; and attended the Lymm Parish, Church. He toolea great interest in local foothall, pla3- ing several times for the village club tesidrs regimental teams. Recruiting Campaign.—Recruiting has been very busy throughout the present week, several people coming up for medical examination. At a committee meeting held on Tuesday night at, the Council Offices, Mr. A. Watkin presiding, Mr. Barker (secretary) reported the result of the canvass up to date, which was highly sat- ,(1-7. E. Evans, the recruiting officer, also gave the total number•of men who had presented themselves for. medical inspec- tion and also the number grouped-tinder Lord Derby's scheme. At the close of the proceed- ings •Mr. Watkin moved-Ahat a letter of sym- pathy be sent top. thelanaily-of, the late Mr. J. R. Newton, who had been •a respected and ad- mired member of that committee for some time. Paying a glowing and eulogistic tribute to the decea.sed'e memory, Mr. Watkin pointed out that not only had the late Mr. Newton inter- ested himself' in the Lymm Division of the Recruiting Committee, but also in other public branches of work.—The proposition was carried.

Primitive Methodist Church Anniversary.-- The anniversary services connected with the Primitive Methodist Church were held on Sun- day and Monday. The Rev. Principal H. J. Pickett was announced to preach on the Sun- day, but indisposition prevented him coming, to the great disappointment of many. Mr. Holtby, a student of the Primitive. Methodist College, came in his stead, preaching to good congrega- tions morning and evening. On Monday a pub- lic tea was held, when about a hundred sat down at the tables. The evening meeting. which was well attended, was opened by the Revs. R. W. Ferguson and W. Daw. Mr. G. L. Welford, in a racy speech, introduced the Chairman, Mr. C. S. Parkin (vice-president of the Primitive Methodist Conference), whose address was an inspiration to those who work in village churches. At the conclusion of his remarks the Chairman called upon Principal Pickett to de- liver his popular lecture, "Oliver Cromwell.". The lecturer was humorous, interesting and edifying. The Rev. R. W. Ferguson thanked all, who had taken any part, however small, in making the anniversary a success.

SERGEANT J. H. M9STON.

Arrives Safely In France After , A Rough Passage.

Mr. Henry Moston, of the Anchor Inn, has this week received the news that his son, Sergi J. H. Moston, has arrived back in the firing line fully prepared to do his share for the honour and glory of the country. He makes the remani. that it was very bad crossing the Channel, the wind blowing a hurricane which delayed the steamer four hours. Whilst it lasted they dare not attempt to effect a landing. However, the popular Sergeant concludes with the words that he is none the worse for the experience.

SAPPER JOSEPH CLARE.

Ih.seribes How He Was Wounded By

Shell-Fragment.

Mr. David Daniels, of Booth's Hill, on Tues- day received the following interesting letter from Sapper Joseph Clare, who is, at present, an inmate of the 2nd Western Hospital, Lei- cester-road, Higher Broughton, Manchester "I know you will be pleased to hear from me and .of .how I RM getting...on. Well, my wound in the lip is about healed up, but I am not ,able to open.my,mouth much, as it has affected my jaw. However, it might have been worse; for four men, who were behind me in action, Were killed and about a dozen wounded, some with legs and arms off. I tell you it was a very exciting moment. We hcard the gun go off! Then we could see a trench mortar- shell in the air coming right :over, but we could not judge where it was going to drop. There was a scuffle amongst us—some running one way, and others another—to try and obtain shelter, ,b,!.it it was no use. We had to have it!

===== 0002601_19151127_art0166.txt | included=true | included =====
Did Not Know He Was Hit At First.

"I was the furthest man in front that was hit. Being excited, I did not know I was hit for the moment, for I went back to where some of the men were buried, with a view to help- ing them out. My face began to pain me, so I went to .the stretcher-bearers to have my wound dressed. Eventually I was moved to hospital in an .ambulance car. Little did I think a piece of trench. mortar had lodged in my cheek until next morning." Sapper Clare is the sop of Mrs. M. E. (flare, of Booth's Hill, and was, prior to being wounded, attached to the 3rd • Eeetim of the Royal Engineers. •, • , .• .

===== 0002601_19151127_art0167.txt | included=true | included =====
THE "GAY CADETS" IN QUERIELAND.

Entertainment Thai Set Up a Local

Record.

What an enthusiastic number or worthy workers there are at present' for the British Red Cross Society! 'Even in our small village there- are many ever Willing to assist this noble and time-honoured • 'Btitish organisation. true we only. occupy a small place in the.sun, but we Can and do.helplo keep the old flag fly- ing in 'just a way peculiarly our own. The Working Men's Committee, a gathering .always. to the fore ,where the interests of others are concerned, with foresight and initiative were the means of giving to the inhabitants an entertainment on Wed.uefday night which in its unexpectedness has never been surpassed for many, many months. We, who know net the horrors of war, must do our share towards those of our more unfortun- ate brethren, hence the goodly and appreciative gathering to witness the entertainment provided by the distinguished artistes named the Gay Cadets. One enjoys a good entertainment, and this one was no ex- ception to the rule, if one might judge from the unstinted applause at the end of the perform- ance.

Sometimes the thought arises in our mind, .when we visit an entertainment, as to whether pr. not .the male artistes might be doing more -good .fighting for their. King and- country, and in Many,CaieSlt is a correct one, but upon this occasion we were set -at rest and,leaned back in our chairs contented and 'satisfied, for on perusing the programme (although many of us had to use spectacles), the information was vouchsafed us that no man eligible -for the Army was concerned in the production of the Gay Cadets in Querieland. In consequence, it was a case of "nothing doing" for the ardent and energetic local recruiting officer and his confreres.

Truly was it a fine programme set before us, for included in it were monologues, comic songs, duets, comedy sketches and other pleas- ing fare. The whole of the artistes acquitted themselves in a fashion worthy of professionals. Such well-known artistes as Mr. George Stubbs (producer), Miss Dorothy L. Burgess, Miss Lily Wills, and• Messrs. James L. Stewart and Denby Wilson appeared, as well as ex-Gunner James Plant, whom we may record was discharged from the R.G.A. on the 30th June last owing to defective eyesight. , The programme was a long one,, and in conse- quence the many appeals for encores, with one or two exceptions, were fruitless and unavail- ing. The gentlemen sang in a manner entirely worthy of their reputation, whilst the ladies (charming and good looking) distinguished themselves for clarity of voice and clearness of enunciation in capital style. Included in the company were several clever humorists, *horn we venture to suggest will in the near future he heard of in higher circles of entertainment unless we are very much mistaken. One song, entitled "I want to meet the Kaiser," brought the house down, as did "Tommy, lad." sung effectively by Mr. Denby Wilson, and as we listened to the words we, .for just a little while, let our thoughts stray to far-away Flanders, where our own lads are fighting. In our pleasures we do not forget, but in a few moments there was gaiety again for us in a burlesque duo, entitled "Dandy Swells," written by Mr. George Stubbs and rendered in fine style by the.author himself and ex-Gunner Plant.

Miss Dorothy L. Burgess gave us many pleas- ing, items.. which included songs entitled "My dear soul" and Alittle bit of -heaven." This charming and ae,gomplished young lady also took part in several duets, the principal of which were entitled "The Voyagers" and "I do kind of feel I'm in love." Miss Lily Wills capi- tally rendered a monologue entitled "Reggie," Which produced whole-hearted .applause, as did Mr. James L. Stewart in one entitled "Alphonse." Many of the company gave several concerted efforts, which went to fur- nish one of the best entertainments we have as yet witnessed in the district. - To sum up, it was a thoroughly enjoyable evening, and 'everybody concerned worked con- scientiously and artistically. We hope to again have the pleasure of hearing this fine company of entertainers, who came spontaneously to the Village to assist. on behalf- of the British Red Cores Society. The proceedings terminated at 10.15. but not before Mr. H. E. Odber, on behalf of the Working Men's Committee, had thanked one and all for their kind presence and patronage. To ,Mr. A. H. Hudson (treasurer), of The Nook, Lymm, credit and praise is due for his worthy efforts in introducing this brilliant array of entertainers.

HILL CLIFFE BAPTIST CHAPEL.

Is It The Oldest Meeting House In The Country?

In the course of an absorbingly interesting lecture on old Warrington, its Academy and its worthies which he gave recently, Alderma ii Bennett made reference to a former citizen of note, wise old John Macgowan, the author of "Dialogues of Devils" and other allegories which, in some respects, will bear comparison with those of John Bunyan, "the immortal dreamer."

===== 0002601_19151127_art0171.txt | included=true | included =====
Baker, Preacher and Pamphleteer.

Several books of Macgowart's, said the Alder- man, made more or less of a sensation and in one of them, which he humorously called "The Shaver," he castigated Oxford University for daring to expel six young men "for holding -Methodistical tenets and for praying, reading and expounding the Scriptures in a private house." Old John Macgowt,a was a baker by trade, but he found time for more than the baking of bread and the writing of pamphlets, for every Sunday he walked out across the com- mon to the little Baptist Chapel at Hill Cliffe, where rumour tells that Cromwell worshipped whilst a trooper preached, and which is said by -some to be the oldest Nonconformist meet- ing place -in England. For Macgowan was not only a baker, an author, and a brilliant dis- putant, but also a Baptist pastor, and a very able one to 'boot. Vhe Italian poet, Alfieri, lived at Hill Cliffe in those days. He fell in love with Sir John .Ligonier's wife, fought a duel with Sir John and went to live at Bello Fields, in the -pleasant hollow just behind the wood,. as the result of it. He was greatly charmed with the view of the Mersey valley, which he caught from his sylvan eyr,ie, and a delighttul, pathway by the firs wa's "named after him Alfieris' Walk. Where Smollett Found a Character.

The house in which Alfieri lived was built by quaint old Admiral Hoare, the original of Com- modore Trunnion in Smollett's "Peregrine Pickle." The Admiral planned his abode on the model of a ship; had his cabins in the quaint interior, and his .quarter-deolc upon the lawn outside; addressed his visitors in nautical par- lance and timed his meals to the accustomed "bells." Richard Wilson, the celebrated landscape painter, was enamoured of Hill Chile. residing there at intervals in. semi- poverty, and boldly claiming that the panorama visible from the old burial ground was not ex- celled in all the length and breadth of Merrie England.

A PLUCKY RESCUE.

Act i ng-Police-Sergeant's Gallantry Recognised.

• The bravery of Acting-Police-Sergeant Wilcoxson was awarded at the Runcorn Petty. Sessions on Monday, when he was presented with the Royal Humane Society's certificate. Giving details, Superintendent Owen said that at 12.10 p.m. on September 10th, Wilcoxson was on duty in Manchester-road, Broadheath, when he heard a shout. Looking over a wall he saw a woman up to her neck in the canal. He ran to the tow-path and, though fully clothed, jumped into the water. • He swam to the middle, and, securing the woman by the hand, brought her to the side. Plenty of people were about, and both the Sergeant and the woman were got out of the water quite safely. At this point the canal was 20 yards wide and Wt. deep in the middle and 6ft. deep at the sides. The facts were reported to the Royal Humane Society, who had granted the gallant rescuer their framed certificate.

On receiving the certificate. Acting-Sergeant Wilcoxson expressed his thanks and said that he would prize the document highly. He added that he had only done his duty by rescuing the woman.

Homo From The Front To Wed.—On the eve of his return to the Front, where he has seen service with the 2nd Royal Irish Regiment, Sergeant Joseph Clare .was on Wednesday married to Miss Elsie Gerrard ilorridge, daugh• ter of the late Mr. and Mrs. norridge, and now resident, at the Post Office, Padgate. Sergeant Clare's home address is 508, Collyhurst-road. Harpurhey, Manchester. The wedding took place at the Padgate Parish Church, the cere- mony being conducted by the Rev. J. Hurst. The bride was given away by her brother-in- law, Mr. Frank Burrell, and she was attired in a travelling costume of navy blue and wore a black velvet hat embellished with a white ostrich feather and orange• blossoms. The bridegroom's present to her was a gold wrist! watch. She was attended by one bridesmaid— Miss May Clare, sister of the bridegroom. Mr. John Weston acted as best man, and after the ceremony a number of friends of the parties were entertained at the Post Office. Owing to Ithe bridegroom's departure for the Front once more there was no honeymoon.

alive and kicking, and I hope you are all the same in dear old England. I am trying to let everybody have a line or two in turn, and you cannot sit down here and write a letter when you want to. Artilleryman Who Objected To Becoming Infantryman.

Two novel protests were made by absentees at the Borough Police Court on Thursday. One of the men was a member of\ the Royal Horse Artillery, attached to an infantry regi- ment. He admitted being absent, saying that since he joined the Army last August, his mind had been turned against soldiering. He did not think it was right that he should be trans- ferred to an infantry regiment when he had joined a horse regiment! The Chief Constable: If they all did that we should lose half the British Army. Defendant was ordered to await an escort. Says He Only Signed On For A Year.

The other, absentee pleaded that his term of .serrice in the Army had expirecl before the war started, but on August 10th, 1914, he volunteered for twelve months' service as a special in- structor. This 12 months' service expired on 3,ugust 9th, when he was shot in the knee at the Dardanelles. He was 'under no obligation to go out to the Dardanelles. He had done hie duty to the Government, and he wanted know why the Government didn't do its duty to him.

The Chief Constable: Better write to the Raiser!

He was ordered to await an escort.

- PROTESTING ABSENTEES.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0186.txt | included=true | included =====
A Long Spell in the Trenches.

"We landed here on September 29th, and they took us straight into the trenches in the front firing line. I have been here ever since, although our company have been out for a rest. I am servant to the battalion machine-gun officer, and we never leave the firing line so long as the battalion is in the trenches. I am just about getting used to it now. It is 35 days and nights since I had my clothes off, as we sleep in our uniform and with our full equip- ment on and rifle, with bayonet fixed ready at hand for action. I cannot say where we are, except that it is somewhere on the Ciallire! Peninsula. I had a narrow escape the other day. A shell nearly dropped in our kitchen, and put our milk and jam tins "out of mess," as it riddled them with shrapnel. The flying fragments missed me, but an officer and another servant were wounded alongside of me. I was making tea at the time, and the shell covered me with all kinds of stuff, but when I shook myself I found I was all right, thank God !"

CHRISTMAS MAILS FOR THE EXPEDITIONARY FORCE.

War Office Regulations.

The Post Office issues the following notice regarding Christmas mails for the British Ex- peditionary Force in France and Flanders The War Office notifies that in military in- terests it is necessary to limit the amount of parcel traffic for ,the troops during the Christ- mas season. The great bulk of mail matter dealt with at normal times is already a very severe tax on the transport service. The amount which the roads will carry is limited, and ammunition, food, and stores must natur- ally have the preference over mails.. Any con- siderable increase in the volume of mail traffic taust inevitably lead to serious delay. The public are therefore requested, as well in their own interests as in regard to military exigencies, to limit the use of the parcel post strictly to articles Of real utility. Fruit, perish- able articles of all descriptions, bottles, pud- ding basins, and the like are prohibited and will not be accepted for transmission.

The maximum- weight for a single parcel will be reduced to 71b. as from December Ist. All parcels must be strongly and securely packed in covers of canvas, linen, or other strong material. Parcels which do not comply with these requirements are unlikely to reach their destination safely, and if observed in the course of post will be returned to the senders. The name and address of the sender must be written on the outside, and parcels which do not comply with this condition will be refused. If it is desired to secure delivery on or before Christmas Day, letters should be posted not later than December 17th and parcels not later than December 13th.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0189.txt | included=true | included =====
PARCELS FOR PRISONERS OF WAR.

The postal authorities announce that the time taken in the transmission of parcels for prisoners df war in Germany and Holland is necessarily .considerable, and, in view of- congestion. which is likely to occur at the Christmas season, parcels should be posted in good time. It should be clearly understood that food- stuffs of a perishable nature must not be sent in parcels for prisoners of war interned abroad.

All letters and parcels for the troops must be fully and completely addressed. Failure to give the proper address will result in delay or non-delivery.

GOOD OLD LANCASHIRE!

A Reference To The County Cheered

In Scotland.

Many Lancashire men, reading the report of Lord Derby's speech at Edinburgh, must have been particularly interested in the fact that someone cried "Good old Lancashire!" upon a reference to "my own county" made by the super-recruiting sergeant. (By the way, the Lancashire for "super" is " cock-nodger.") Lord Derby replied, "Yes—good old Lanca- shire!" and the Scottish audience cheered. The Scotch are so strong on history that most of them would remember that a Stanley figured at Flodden, if only on the authority of "On, Stanley, on !" in "Marmion," but they would hardly know how great a part Lancashire played at Flodden. There is an old ballad which gives the county all , the credit— Lancashire like lyons laden them about, _ All had been lost by our lord had not those leddes Incidentally this rude couplet traces the phrase "Lancashire lads" back a few hundreds of years. Middleton has a stained-glass window in its church, placed there soon after the battle, which silhouettes fifteen Middleton bowmen who fought under Stanley. Their names are nearly all extant to-day, and some hearing them are enrolled in Scottish regiments and wear the kilt.—" Manchester Guardian" Mis- cellany.

CAUSES OF APPENDICITIS.

Appendicitis is chiefly a disease of youth. it is met with more frequently among males be- tween the ages of ten and thirty years. The later in life the less liability one has of con- tracting appendicitis. It commonly occurs in those who are apparently in good health and strikes them down very quickly. What was commonly called a few years ago "inflamma- tion of the bowel" is now known to have been appendicitis. It often follows constipation and catarrh of the bowels or colitis.

Investigation of late years into the causes of appendicitis have led many surgeons to be- lieve that one chief cause is the diet of the people. The comparative immunity from appendicitis of the 'races vho subsist upon a. purely vegetable diet is worthy of consideration. This fact alone implies that -::ho frequency of the disease in some communities nes tr, do raps doubt with meat eating. Dr. D. P. D: Wilkie in his article en appendi- citis as reported in the "British Medical Journal" of December, 1914, has tb3 following to say on the relation of diet to appendicitis:— " The remarkable frequency of a ppendicular disease in its acute and fatal form among Western nations and its comparative rarity among Eastern peoples has attracted ecnsider- able attention. Lucas Charnpionniere, in an analysis of 22,000 patients among Roumanian peasants, found only one base of appendicitis. In the cities of Roumania, on the other hand, he found one case of appendicitis among every 221 patients. The Roumanian peasants subsist on a diet almost wholly vegetarian, whereas the cities an animal diet is the rule.—" Good Health."

WAR LOAN INVESTMENT.

The 18th annual report and statement of accounts of the Warrington District Nursing Association, proves that in spite of the war, this splendid and philanthropic Association is in a healthy condition, though the balance at the end of the year was slightly less than at the beginning. The total income for the year, in- cluding a balance brought forward of £9B 17s. Bd., was £869 9s. RI. Expenditure, including £lBB 17s. 3d. invested in the War toan, amounted to £776 100. 21. This left a balance in hand of £B2 19.5. 2d.

FINANCIAL POSITION WELL MAINTAINED.

The report of Councillor 11. Roberts, hon. treasurer, is as follows:— "I have pleasure in submitting my accounts for the past year. In spite of. a year of war— the greatest and the saddest our Country has ever known—our financial position has been well maintained. We are deeply indebted to the ladies and gentlemen whose strenuous efforts combined to make the Old English Fai;- so great a success, and from the results of which we have received, through our friend Mr. Collin, the handsome donation- of £lOO. A legacy of £5O, left to us by the late Alfred Baker, has been received from the trustees through their solicitor; Mr. J. W. Knowles. There is a gratifying increase in the amount of the Church offertories, but in this directicui I hope I may be forgiven for suggesting that there is yet room for considerable improvement. Tt is difficult to imagine any movement which has stronger claims than ours upon the practical sympathy of members of the Christian faith. We have again received very substantial sup- port from the Working Men's Committee, whose untiring. efforts • bespeak the intense interest which i felt by them in the beneficent work of the Association. Having in mind the extra- ordinary demands which the war continues to make, I am especially grateful to all those friends who have contributed to our funds, and I venture to hope that we may be able to con- tinue with unabated. force the work of our in- stitution, which through the ministration of our nursing staff has brought comfort to so many who otherwise must have _suffered a great deal of avoidable and therefore unnecessary pain. Death has -deprived us during the Past year of the benevolent support of the late Mr. W. Long. of Thelwall Heys, who has been a constant and generous subscriber 'to our fund since the Association was established; of the kindly and practical interest of the Rev. Father Swarbreck, of St. Benedict's. and of Mr. James Smith and Mr. E. S. A. Robson. Unfortunately—in every case owing to the war—a few of our oldest sub- scribers have discontinued their contributions, and others have reduced the sums •formerly given."

===== 0002601_19151127_art0195.txt | included=true | included =====
15,256 VISITS IN 12 MONTHS.

In their report the compittee state that Miss Milne (superintendent) and the nursing staff have merited the warm appreciation of the com- mittee and the gratitude of the patients and their friends for the devotion to duty and sym- pathy with suffering which is so st,riking a characteristic of their daily work. During the year 15,256 visits were paid to 664 cases. Amongst the patients were four discharged soldiers—broken in the service of our King and country—and 150 wives and children of men serving in his 'Majesty's Forces. The committee regretted that the removal of the Rev. Father Wilson deprived them of the support of a most estimable colleague, who took a warm interest in the Association. They gratefully acknow- ledge the financial support accorded to them during the year, and desired also to express to Dr. Langdale their thanks for his professional attendanee upon the nursing staff.

EASTERN LIFE.

The Land Where Fashion Never

Changes.

ORIENTAL MUSIC.

In the sixth of a series of lectures on Eastern Life, Mr. A. Mounfield described the scenes and incidents of an ancient market place. The first part of the lecture was devoted to dress and its significance. The lecturer said that dress. had always been and was to the present day a sure indication of rank in the East, and not only of rank, but of race and religion. In the streets of Damascus; Jerusalem or Cairo to-day it woubl be possible to identify Jew or Mohammedan and rich and poor by the garments they wore. "Soft clothing," as in Bible (lays, marked the aristo- crat, and the humble man of the desert wore camel's hair. Nor had there been any change of fashion. An interesting instance was revealed in the statement that Jesus wore a seamless robe. This was the native dress of Galilee, and differed from the dress of Jerusalem. To this day the people of Galilee wore seamless gar- ments. The garments, of course, were loosely fitting robes thrown over the body and secured by a girdle. • They were not made to measure, and could be worn by anyone. Thus the giving of "changes of raiment ". was a usual thing; there was no consideration of whether the gar- ments would fit. In the stories of Joseph, Stun- son and Gehazi there wa's the mention of giving "changes of raiment," which meant the giving of so many robes. Rich men invested their money in numerous and costly garments, and at feasts decked their guests in them. • When Jesus gave the warning against laying up earthly , treasure which the moth might destroy, he had . in mind the elaborate wardrobes of the rich. The well-to-do classes wore large and flowing robes, the labouring classes \ wore them 'smaller so as to give more freedom to the limbs. The "long clothing." was that, of the indolent and the do-nothings, hence the complaint against the 'Scribes that they loved "long clothing." . Joseph's Coat Of Many Colours.

Among the Arab tribes, Mr. Mounfield ex- plained, the long robe marks the chief, and when a father presents a flowing robe to one of his sons it indicates that he has chosen him to be his successor as head of the family. The chieftainship does not follow always to the eldest son, but may be given by the father to the one who seems best fitted to sustain it. It is in this that we -find the explanation of the jealousy of Joseph's brothers. Jacob gave to Joseph what is wrongly called in the Bible "a coat of many colours." • The Hebrew word means " expansive " or "large" The ex- planation is that Jacob picked out Joseph as his successor, and events show that heimade a wise choice, for Joseph was the noblest of all his sons, but as might have been expected, the older brothers wore indignant and jealous, and took a terrible revenge upon their father and brother.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0199.txt | included=true | included =====
A Capacious Pocket.

The loose robe was held to the body by a girdle, and when the Oriental prepared himself for work he threw back the loose portions of the garment over his shoulders, thus leaving his arms free. This is the meaning of the figure of speech "The Lord lath made bare his arm." The loose space above the belt formed a large and useful pocket.: The statement that good measure would be poured " into your bosom" described the method of carrying grain after it had been purchased; and the shepherd carried the lambs "in his bosom." Unlike Western women, the Eastern women wore their plainest clothing in the streets and decked themsehies in their best at home. The men-folk in the East show their appreciation of this by always spending their evenings at home. In the street the women usually cover the head with a veil. This is a sleet two yards long by a yard wide.' In such a veil :it will be remembered that Ruth carried six measures of barley. Eastern veils must not be confused with Western veils.

In sending out the disciples, Jesus gave the instruction that they were not to carry two coats. The carrying of a second coat would indicate that a man was travelling for pleasure, or going to social function, the second coat being intended for use in the house when evening came. Jesus wished his disciples-to appear as. working men out upon a mission, which did not suggest pleasure. He also bade them lo "salute. no man by the way." .Eastern salutations were elaborate greetings in which much time was wasted. The disciples were told to spend no time Upon useless and unreal talk.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0200.txt | included=true | included =====
The Tax Gatherer.

In describing the Eastern market place and its usual features, the lecturer described the gateway and the tax assessor who sat there. Taxes were placed on everything that entered or left the city. The tax assessor was hateful not only because the taxes went to Rome, but because of the methods of the tax collectors: The taxes were " farmed " as we say in these (lays, .that is, the districts were let to indi- viduals, who made out of them as much as they could, and used extortion. Zaccheus would would be especially hated, because he was the "chief" tax gatherer ofjericho, a superintendent of the district. Matthew was an underling who taxed the produce that entered or left Caper- nauni.

Music And Dancing. Describing the relaxations of the people who gathered in the market place in leisure hours, Mr. Mounfield explained their musical diver- sions. They sang and danced, but their music was unlike. our own. The harp was tuned not in tones, but in quarter tones, and a piece of bone was drawn from end to end to make a whirring sound. There was no tune or melody as we know it. They sang or recited in . a wailing voice 'to the accompaniment of the buzzing strings of the harp. In the words of the Psalms they 'played "..With a loud noise," and that was their idea of good music. Dancing was also a common diversion, but we must be careful not to compare it with our own. In the East men and women never dance together. They dance and sing to music, and express with their limbs gladness and even sorrow. Their dancing consisted of movements of the body which expressed ecstasy or other emotions. This helps us to understand the words of Psalm 149 "Let them praise His name. with the dance: let them sing praises unto Him with the timbrel and harp." They sang and danced both their happi- ness and their sorrow..

A lecture on "Shepherd Life in the East " will be given on December 3rd, in the Friars' Green Schoolroom.

CAROL CONCERTS IN AID OF TIIE

BLIND.

The National institute for the Blind is organising a League called the National Carol League, the members of which will sing carols in aid of its widespread work among the blind, including the training and after-care of our soldiers and sailors who have lost their sight. at the war.

Branches of the League have been established in different parts of the country, and a large number of small groups of singers have been formed to visit private houses, composed of local residents who have volunteered to help the League in this way. In the formation of these groups the League has been greatly helped by many Churches and Musical Soc- ieties, and by many ladies and gentlemen who are lovers of music and sympathisers with the blind.

The League is making preparations to hold a special Carol Week throughout the country from Saturday, December 18th, to Sunday, December 26th, aml everyone is asked to do something for Carol Week in their district. The National Institute for the Blind would be particularly glad to hear from anyone who could organise a concert, or other entertain- ment, at which carols might he sung, or who could organise one or two groups of singers either as an addition to their local branch, should h branch already have been established, or as a first ,step should their district be at present unrepresented.

- Help is also invited in making the Sunday following Christmas Day a special Carol Sun- day. Al any churches have premised to give an organ recital or to hold a special carol service on this day, Sunday, December 26th, giving their collection to the League, and a sugges- tion has been made that everyone should follow the example thus set and sing carols in their own home on this Sunday, makiiw a family collection in aid of the Institute.

Everyone willing to help should communicate with Arr. C. Arthur Pearson, the Pre3iderit 01 the. National Institute .for the.81ind,.224, Great Portland-strpet,. London, W.

WOMAN'S WORLD.

CHAT and GOSSIP.

By MARJORIE.

The City of Treasures. It is nothing less than a tragedy that Venice should be a naval base in view of the fact that it is a city of glorious churches and palaces, the like of which can be seen in no other city in the world. It is possible that Venice might escape attacks from the air, even by the modern Huns, were it not for her. arsenal. She is also unfortunately situated in the front of the battle line, and only one hour's distance from the enemy's airship base. It is scarcely likely that the opportunity of working irreparable mischief will be lost by the Teutonic barbarians, who are incapable of appreciating the sacred duty of protecting the • priceless treasures stored in Venice. The Italians, 'however, have done everything in their power to ensure the safety of their rarest works, and most beautiful build- ings. Layers of sandbags protect the Sausovino bronzes on the Campanile, and the most elaborate care has been taken for the protection of the interior of San Marco. The four horses of gilded bronze that once adorned the won- derful gardens of Corinth, have been placed for safety in the archway of the Doge's Palace. They are over two thousand years old, and dur- ing that time have made many journeys from and to the east and west.

Wicker Work and Balket-making. The manufacture c)f- wicker baskets was once a flourishing industry in this country. • It pro- vided work for thousands. of women and chil- dren, and was a recognised craft in the City of London. The planting and cultivation of osiers, and basket-making, were the mainstay of many flourishing villages, but like so many countryside industries, it died down with the age of machinery and cheap importations.. The Worshipful Company of Basketmakers is • en- deavouring to revive the making of basketware by disabled soldiers and Sailors, and plenty of useful employment could be found for skilled workers. The Army alone requires an immense number of -baskets for the transport of shells and munitions. The Post Office also uses baskets to a very largo extent, and there is an increased demand for them for the carriage of fruit and garden produce generally.

HOME NOTES.

Profitable Recipes And Useful Suggestions.

Cockroaches.

If pulverised borax is plentifully scattered where cockroaches frequent it will destroy them.

Grease.

Grease spots can be removed from clothing by making a paste of fuller's earth and tur- pentine. This should be rubbed upon the fab- ric until the turpentine has evaporated, and a white powder remains. The latter can be brushed off and the grease spots should be gone. Teeth. •

A good wash for the teeth: Dissolve two ounces of oorax in three pints of water'; before quite cold add one teaspoonful of tincture of myrrh and one tablespoonful of spirits of camphor; bottle for use. One wineglass of the solution, added to one-half -pint of tepid water, is sufficient for each application. This solution, applied daily, preserves the teeth and shoutri prevent tartarous adhesion.

The Complexion. To clear a sallow skin one must not drink tea or coffee and avoid all highly-spiced food and never eat pork or greasy dishes of any kind: Eat plenty of ripe fruit, particularly oranges. As a medicine take one teaspoonful of phosphate of *soda in a glassful of hot 'water every morning one hour before breakfast; repeat the dose at night one hour before retiring. Con-, tinue to take the soda for three or four weeks, or as long as required. A Good Cold Cream.

Three-fourths ounce of white wax, four ounces of oil of sweet almonds, one-half ounce of rose water, one-half ounce of glycerine, one- half-drachm of borax, five to eight drops of oil of rose water; melt together with moderate heat, in a granite dish, the sperm and wax, add oil of sweet almonds, do not overheat, dissolve borax in glycerine and rose water, and add to oil and wax as it begins to cool, then whip thoroughly in an egg-heater until it begins to cream, then add oil of rose.

Some girls are taken at their face valtie and some at their pa value.

Cause and Effect.

It has frequently been noticed that husbands known to be henpecked make their way in the world, while, as a rule, domestic male tyrants are left far behind in the race for prosperity.

FEMININE TENACITY OF LIFE.

Difference of Mortality Rates Between Men and

Women.

It is a well-known- scientific fact that the term of life of women is slightly longer than that of men. The difference of the mortality rates during the first few years of life, however, is striking. During the first year the mortality among males is decidedly greater than among females.

Although more boys are born than girls, the proportions are reduced to almost even terms at the end of the first year by the.excessiVe male mortality. Even during the first four years the mortality among males exceeds that among females, notwithstanding the fact that there are practically no distinctions made in the man- agement of the• two sexes. Both are subject, to the same conditions, are dressed virtually the same and receive the same food.

•At the age of about five years the comparative death rate among girls begins to increase. This has been attributed to the fact that boys of this age are more in the open air. The mortality in both sexes diminishes from this time until the 12th year, when it attains its lowest point, it then steadily rises, being larger in each suc- cessive year. Between the 12th and 16th years the death rate among girls increases more rapidly than among boys, but after the 16th year, for several years, the rate of increase is more rapid on the male side. The explanations that have been offered for these peculiarities are not wholly satisfactory, but one fact is blear—that during early years females possess a greater tenacity of life than do males.

A POPULAR COMPETITION.

MY DEAR CHILDREN,-

Of course, I knew that when I asked you to write me about your favourite games I should be. "snowed under "—as our American cousins say—with letters, but even in my most hopeful moments I did not think I should have to read throughltalf so many attempts as I now find awaiting me. My word, I must get those " comfy " slippers on, draw my easy chair up to the fire—for it is bitterly cold outside; just Christmassy in fact !,--and make an attack on the big pile of essays, before me. The sight of so many attempts is a real " heart-gladdener " —again I borrow a phrase from the. United States—and I must drop our old friend. Uncle Ned a. lino to let him know that the large family of nephews and nieces, in whom he took so much interest, are still as keen as ever, even if they aie not keener, op the "Corner." By the way, Uncle Ned writes asking me to remem- ber him to " all 'the hundreds of kiddies in the good. old 'Corner.'" lb is now a- full-blown soldier 'ef the King—serving in one of the most important parts of our great Army; in fact, he is in that corps which has been called "the eyes- and brains of the Army." In huge modern wars, like the one that is now raging, a great deal more is needed to win battles besides strength and courage and straight shooting on the part of the soldier. It is no use sending " Tommy " into the trenches, unless, behind him, you have officials who see to it that he is fed and clothed and armed; that he .is sup- ported by big guns; that he is sent to the right part Of the battlefield and to the best possible and safest position, and that.Prrangements are -made to care for him and nurse -him back to health should he be wounded—and it is in one of the great departments of the Army that looks after some of these details, as well as many others, that Uncle Ned is "doing his bit," for his country. I know you will join with me in saying " Good luck " to himl • Christmas, as*l reminded you last week, is now drawing near, so I don't think I could find a better subject for ottr next essay Competitirm than " The Christmas Present I 'should Like!" Let me have your attempts not later than Tuesday next.

UNCLE JACK.

SOME OF THE BEST ESSAYS.

Let me now dip into my post-bag awl see the games which my nephews and nieces mcst favour.

Nephewld Whittle. of 123. Cartwright- street. writes:—

"My favourite game is • draughts; because while interesting, it is also a game of great

MIMNI

mental skill, and teaches us the virtue of patience. It requires careful study and thought, which are essential for the development of the mind—a thing which is very helpful to us through life. The game of draughts reminds me of this great war. I describe the board as the battlefield and- the players as two great opposing Generals 'skilfully moving their men into the best possible positions to gain a victory with as little loss as possible." A very good little paper, Harold. I, too, am ;owl of draughts. Next to chess I think it is the finest indoor game ever discovered. Like chess, too, it is a very old game and-it used, I believe, to be known as chequers. Niece Maud Hickey, of 35, King-street, favours rollicking outdoor game, called "Leave. Home" —" because it keeps the players warm in winter and because there is plenty of fun in it."

The Voluntary Aid Detachment.

Quietly and unostentatiously the work of this organisation has been carried on for over a year. Scarcely had the first British soldiexs left this country before its hospitals, to the number of about a thousand, had sprung up all over the country. Their equipment was as perfect us it was then possible to make it, and it has since been improved in every detail. Women who are not trained Munition or industrial workers, or are unable to give the whole day to war ser- vice could hardly do better than join the Voluntary Aid Detachment of the Red Cross Society. There is at all the hospitals a good deal of cleaning and ordinary housework to be done, and those who can give no other help might undertake such work, although it may be quite new to them.

Indoor Jackets. Coats of all kinds are enjoying a wide popu- larity. Sleeveless boleros of simple design are as charming as they are various in shape and decoration. In velvet or velveteen they are at their best, and Ipay be relied upon to bring a last year's corsage or dress quite up-to-date. In addition they give warmth and comfort, and are so useful as well as attractive, that a pur- chase could not, even in these days be con- sidered an extravagance. The simpler shapes present no difficulties to the hthne dressmaker, and being sleeveless make no great demand on the brief leisure hours Of the busy woman. The indoor golf coats or jackets are also having an unprecedented vogue. Whilst not as smart as the little boleros, they are desirable garments, and being Made with long - basques, are warmer • for winter wear. They are fashioned .of many materials,—velvet, soft satin, China silk, crepe de chine, but faille appears to -be the most popular. Fur and marabout, or bands of a different material and contrasting colour, with various kinds of stitchery, are all used for decorating these use- ful and often handsome coats.

The Vogue of the Coat-Frock. The redingote, or coat-frock, is an economical fashion, and is carrying all before it. ,By its aid the wearing of a thin frock is possible in winter, and thus possibly the cost of a new dress may be saved. Many of this season's coats are distinctly Russian in origin, but the redin- gote comes from Poland. It is in common with so many of this winter's modes, .a most comfort- able garment, covering up the dress entirely,

or reaching the hem within a few inches. It is full or pleated in the skirt, but the upper part and sleeves fit almost tightly. Russian influences may be noted in eoats, blouses, tunics and frocks, which fasten at the side. Checks are used largely for wrap coats, and travelling coats of serge and heavy materials. The squares are in many cases quite large, and the trimming is of course fur of some kind or its imitations. A travelling coat in chess board squares ar- ranged diamond-wise, was most effective in brown and white. It recalled the wraps of long ago, for it had a circular cape, tight-fitting sleeves underneath, thus making everything de- sirable in the way of warmth and comfort.

The New Millinery. Milliners are cleverly exploiting brocade, com- bined with all kinds of trimmings. For instance a hat will have a brocade crown, a velvet brim, and be very simply trimmed with fur or plum- age. The small hat has returned to favour with the winter gales, for it is both practical and comfortable in rough weather. The larger hat is still in evidence for smart functions. Some of the new models have bizarre trimmings, freakish in the extreme, but these are designed only for the few as the majority of women in- sist on moderation and simplicity in millinery as in clothes. Generally speaking this winter's headgear is both picturesque and becoming; the greater number of hats being made of velvet which has a softening effect on the features. Hats with fur crowns to match the peltry worn with " them will be popular. KoHusky sable and brown taffetas form an ex- cellent combination. Bands and flowers, or beads trim hats of all descriptions. The beads are very tiny, and closely sewn together, some- times of steel, but often gay in colour, the flowers taking Futurist forms. The Use of Perfumes. •

Every nation has its special taste in perfumes, and from time immemorial men as well as women have made use of scents and essences. Some of them in use to-day are made from recipes known to the Ancient Egyptians; and the Crusaders extracted from the Arabs the secrets of making many of the heavy laugurous Eastern scents. The modern taste is for scent distilled from the flowers which have an ex- hilarating freshness. Musk is a dead perfume, but it was a favourite scent in Prance at the time of Napoleon. The Greeks and 'Romans favoured essences, and preferred the odour of the violet to any other- flower.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0209.txt | included=true | included =====
A New War Game.

Niece Louie Minshull, of 29, Wakefield-street, has found a new and topical game. "As you have asked us to write about our favourite game, I will just tell you what mine is," she says. "It is playing Red Cross Nurses, I think it is such a• nice game to play, while this war is on. I would like to be a Red Cross nurse if I were old enough, because it is such good and noble work. All Red Cross nurses at the Front are in danger of their lives, and have ,to be very brave . when they go out to tend to the wounded. The death of Nurse Cavell ought to set an example to other women, and it just shows what some English women will do for their country." "My favonrite game is skipping," writes Louie Wilson, of 27, Golborne-street. "All that is needed when playing this game is a rope, which is eas)ly procured. All the muscles of the body are set working when you are play- ing this game; the arms for turning the rope and the feet for jumping. All athletes skip for the sake of the health and breathing. It is a good game on a winter's morning."

HINTS ON HEALTII.

Useful Information In Popular Language.

Bachelors are declared to be more liable to indigestion than married men. One reason .9g- gested is that bachelors so often eat alone. Good company and cheerful conversation aid diges- tion.

An Extraordinary Habit. " I look forward to the time when people will leave off the extraordinary habit of taking medicine when they are sick.—Sir Frederick Treves.

Our Common Enemy. Clearly we cannot all shoulder a rifle and march along to the • battle- field, but we can at least serve our country well and faithfully by striving at home with that common and devastating enemy—drink. Thus every woman can fight for home and country. This is no counsel of perfection. It is the plain duty of all.—Marie Blanche, in March "Good Health."

Cause- and Effect.

District Visitor: "And how are you to-day, Mrs. Jones?"

The Patient: "Not all all badly, thank you, ma'am. The doctor is doing his' best. I've told him there will be nobody to pay him, unless I get well."—" Cardiff Western Mail." Worry.

The measure of all time and duration is spelled out in one sentence. " Yesterday—to- day—and for ever "; but we are not able to change the past, nor are we responsible for the future. We have only one problem to solve, and it is expressed in one word, "to-day." Live one day at a time and when the sun sets let the account be settled. Don't take your trouble to bed with you. Stack it outside of your bed- room door. Canon Beadon, who lived to be over a hundred, said to a friend that the secret of long life, in his own case, was that he had never thought, of anything unpleasant aften ten o'clock at night.—Rev. James L. Gordon.

HOW A DOG HELPED TO SAVE MANY LIVES.

One Autumn, years and years ago, when emigrants were pouring into our oldest colony— Newfoundland—the emigrant ship the Despatch, was caught in a gale and struck a rock near Cape Ra2,'. There was no. wonderful " wireless " in those days to summon help, but a fisherman named. (leorge Harvey, whose lonely home was on a rocky island about three miles off, beard the ship's signals of ,distress, and he deter- mined to do what he could. He launched his boat and took with him his boy of twelve years, his girl of seventeen, and his dog. The little boat with its crew of one man and two children struggled valiantly through the raging sea, but it could not get close enough to the wreck to be of any use. But now came the dog's turn! At his master's bidding he jumped overboard and swam off in the direction of the ship. The waves beat him back, but, nothing daunted, he struck out again. At last he got within reach, and the sailors threw him a rope which, half dead with exhaustion, be brought back safely to his master. And in the end, by the united efforts of that fisherman, his boy, his girl, and his dog, all the hundred and gixty-three people on board the Despatch were saved.

THE INTERIM DIVIDEND.

At the 220th quarterly meeting of the War- rington E.I. Co-operative Society Ltd.—held in the Co-operative Hall on Monday evening—the Secretary (Mr. J. Jarman) read ti statement respecting the action which the committee had decided to take regarding employes who had enlisted, or who might enlist, and the allow- ances that were proposed to be made. •

===== 0002601_19151127_art0220.txt | included=true | included =====
PRACTICALLY 50 EMPLOYES WITH THE

COLOURS.

The statement was as follows:

In August, 1914, before the war, we had 218 male employes, including lads, and men over military age. Up to the moment 49 have en- listed in all departments. We had on Novem- ber 13th, 106 men of military age (69 in shops, &c., and 37 in the productive and carting de- partments, bakery, boot repairing departme,nts, &c.) We estimate that to carry on the business it is essential that 63, out of the 106 should re- main, as they cannot be -replaced by women. The positions are those of branch grocery and butchery managers, departmental managers, carters (absolutOly necessary, even if deliveries are dropped, to take goods to the shops), bakers, boot repairers, and sufficient male shop assist- ants to provide one, beside the manager, in smaller shops, and possibly two in the larger.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0221.txt | included=true | included =====
Promise To Married Men.

"We have undertaken in the ease of all married men to make up the wages to the aver- age earnings, including Government allowances. This has applied to all who have enlisted up to now. In the event of any branch managers or heads of departments enlisting, we have under- taken to make up the wages, so long as the pay- ment from the Society does not exceed '2.h. to any one person per week. In addition we have undertaken that all men who have enlisted from the regular staff, or who may enlist under the present voluntary system, shall be found a position—the one left if possible; but, at least, one equal in wages to what they would have received had they remained in our employ. The cases of single men who have dependents are being dealt with, on application, on their merits. No guarantee has been given or can be given if anything in the nature of compulsory military service is instituted—either as to re-instatement or payment of allowances—as then the u hole position will have to be reconsidered. Women Temporarily Replace Men.

"Females are being introduced into several of the larger shops as temporary assistants dur- ing the war only. They are being paid the Co- operative Union Congress scale of wages and war bonus in addition; above this Scale every one will be dealt with according to ability. We ask the assistance of our members (1) by shop. ping early in the day and as far as possible avoiding the week-end; (2) by carrying away their own goods and not requiring either bread or groceries sent, and whenever possible to bring. a basket and save cost and time of parceliiqg. Owing to the very late hour to which the assistants are kept on Friday evenings, at the branches, the committee have decided to close at 8 o'clock instead of 9, as at present, and, at 8 o'clock on the Saturday at the Central, instead of 9."

Some little discussion followed the reading of the statement; questions being asked As to the. action the committee had taken in certain in- stances, and Mr. Wadsworth suggested that the committee should consider me possibility of making an allowance, say, of five shillings per week, to every single man who had enlisted, and where there were no dependents that i his cm might be put aside by the ;ociety until the num returns. The suggestion seemed to meet with general favour, and on behalf of the committee, very careful consideration of it was promised. The meeting was presidod over by Mr. .T. Upson. who was supported by the whole of the committee with the exception of Mr. Jones. There were about 2SO members present. Messrs, Hibbert, Knight, Kilduff and Randel were elected AR scrutirfeers and returning officers.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0222.txt | included=true | included =====

Mr. 'Upson briefly introduced the report of the committee, which showed that the sales for the thirteen weeks ended October 19th, realised £104,825, an increase of £18,926 over the corre- sponding period. The membership had in- creased by 350, and now stood at 15.196. The individual holdings of share capital averaged £7 lls, per member.

The report was accepted practically without dissent.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0229.txt | included=true | included =====
GENERAL BOOTH'S WIDOWS'

SCHEME.

Proposed Guardianship for Dependents

Of The nillen.

General Booth has just announced another important Salvation Army undertaking. Re- cognising the difficulties and hardships that will surround many of the widows and chil- dren of men who have fallen in the war, arising from various causes connected with their bereavement, more particularly from the absence of the masculine element in the economy of the family, the leader of the Sal- vation Army has devised a scheme of some magnitude for the establishing of a paternal guardianship to be placed at the disposal of dependents—widows, children, and aged par- ents—who, cannot obtain this in the ordinary course.

A Central Council, termed "The Widows' Counsellors," has been instituted at the Head- quarters of the Salvation Army in London to construct the details of this scheme, which will embrace the United Kingdom, the inten- tion being to make use of the Army's highly efficient organisation to the fullest extent in this connection. The main idea of the guard- ianship would be to maintain a fatherly interest in each member of the family con- cerned, assisting the widow in the management of her affairs when she desired, guiding her in her dealings with the State administrative bodies, securing for her supplementary finan- cial aid if need be, and helping her to obtain suitable education for her children on lines adapted to the careers intended for them, the consideration of which would also be one of the most important functions of the -guardian- ship. Local counsellors appointed in each dis- trict 'would form the intimate link, between the widow and the influence enlisted on her behalf, while the whole of these executive agencies would be governed by the central authority in London presided Over by the -leaders of the Army.

The Salvation Army has already had consid- erable experience in promoting the welfare of widows and their' families in connection with its emigration and colonisation work The efforts of this branch of the Imperial Mission of the Army have been attended with the most satisfactory results, 95 per cent. of the families transferred to the Dominions having fully realised the most sanguine expectations enter- tained for their success. The natural inclina- tion for social service possessed by many of its officers and developed by experience into a ripe efficiency is another valuable auxiliary at the command of the Army in connection with the work of the Widows' Counsellors.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0230.txt | included=true | included =====

JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST.

Anthrax.

The fatal case at a local tannery reminded me of experiences in the Bradford district many years ago, when what was commonly known as woolsorters' disease occurred for a while with alarming frequency. When I came within-the area of the cotton trade there was an occasional instance at a picker maker's. I attended inquests _on two men in the employ of one firm, these having contracted anthrax through handling. foreign hides. The em- ployers complained that the men would not tako the trouble to put on the gloves which were ready to their hands, and the habitual use of which was well calculated to render wearers immune from the dreadful disease in question. Order, Order.

The Bridge of Sighs over which, according to an extract from his Parish Magazine in last week's issue, a prominent local vicar labours somewhat painfully, somehow set me thinking of an incident at a monthly meeting at one of the old Local Boards of 30 to 40 years ago. The members had uniler con- sideration the estimates for a new bridge over the river, which was not at all wide at that point. "Far too much," exclaimed one; " why, I could spit across there." " Mr. W-," interposed the Chairman, "you're out of order." " Aye, I know that, ' Mr. Chairman," was the immediate response, "if I wasn't out of order I could spit twice as far!"

The Soldiers' Home. The account of the special treat at the local Soldiers' Home reminded me of a significant confession lately made to me by a wounded " Tommy " from Gallipoli. Said he, "Lots of our fellows—most of 'em, in fact—are very fond of singing, and the others are always glad to hear one with a good voice. Well, no; not quite always. Now and again one would start Home, sweet. home.' That was more than they could stand. Oh, chuck summat at him.' 'He's crying for his mammy,' and other expressions of the sort came from all aides, and the singer was obliged either to change his tune or shut up altogether. 'Home, sweet home!' It was unbearable in such circumstances." "

===== 0002601_19151127_art0239.txt | included=true | included =====
The Unspeakable Fitzgerald.

After the case had been disposed of at Man- chester Assizes, I got into conversation with one, of the victims of the scoundrel just son- tenced to eight years' penal servitude for de- frauding women in heartless fashion, as re- ported in last week's "Examiner." As a matter of fact the lady accosted me and re- ealled how we had met in a district in which I formerly lived. She complained that,. although on the suggestion of the Court the Press representatives had agreed not to publish the names of witnesses who had been caught in the adventurer's toils, one had given hers, so that her friends and neighbours had become possessed of the whole story. At all events she had behaved very foolishly. She had an- swered one of the rascal's matrimonial adver- tisements, had met him clandestinely, and had finally handed to him her savings—all without telling-her parents or consulting some experi- enced friend. Such considerations tend to modify the symplithy one naturally reels for one who has, been grossly deceived kby the thoroughpaced villain who has now got his deserts.

No " Swank.." Of all the modest young heroes I ever en- countered commend me to one whose acquaint- ance I accidentally made in the train last Sat- urday. He sat quietly in a corner seat listen- ing with evident amusement to a, loud-voiced civilian who for some time monopolised the conversation. The talkative one was very positive respecting certain doings at the Dar- danelles, claiming to have received a good deal of informatibn at first hand and pretend- ing to reveal much that the newspapers would not be allowed to print. Eventually, address- ing the khaki-clad warrior directly, he said, "If you eyer get out there, you'll know some- thing." "But," returned the young soldier, now speaking for the first time, "I know already; I've just come from the very position you refer to." "Is that so?" pursued the already somewhat crestfallen speaker. "Well, I shouldn't have thought it." "Yes," con- tinued the soldier, "I was in the engage- ments you have been talking about, and you may take my word for it that you have been seriously misinformed." The fellow did not receive this at all kindly; indeed, he told the good-natured " Tommy " bluntly that he did not believe he had been in the region in qtkes- tion. "Well'," exclaimed the young soldier, "if you must have it, I was recommended for ,the V.C." "But you didn't get it, eh?" sneerecl the other. " No, I didn't," was the reply. " Ah, just as I thought," commented the knowing one, with a comprehensive glance of triumph. " No," repeated the soldier, " I did not get the V.C., but I got the D.C.31." and, throwing open his greatcoat, he displayed the decoration. It was a neatly-administered and well-merited rebuke, and the too cocksure gentleman subsided into silence and endeav- oured to hide his confusion behind his evening paper.

The Poor Gal. As I read the local paragraph headed "Why the gas is poor," I thought of a singular mis- hap supposed to have been caused by the ex- istence of similar conditions in a district in I which I formerly resided. I had come across an old colleague from those parts, and he ex- plained to me how the gas suffered seriously in quality precisely as suggested in these col- umns last week. The Government demand for toluene, said he, had resulted in some other subtle residual escaping into the supply pipes, and this in turn necessitated vigorous application of the forcepump here, there, and everywhere. "In fact," he went on, in his breezy way, "two chaps from the Corporation gas works do nothing else at present but lug this thingummy from one place to another for the .purpose of clearing the gaspipes for com- plaining householders. And they don't half do it, either, as you may guess when I tell you of something that happened round our way last baking day. Those fellows got their apparatus to work in one house, and, having put their loaves in the ovens, the neighbours on each side slipped out at the back for a bit of gossip. The Corporation men—most ex- traordinary thing—positively worked so hard • at the lnimp—very unusual, as you will agree —that they forced the blooming gas out of the ovens in both the other houses, this resulting in a couple of rather violent explosions, and the loaves being blown all over the place 1" Dialect Puzzles Natives.

What Sergt;Widocks wrote as to this in his letter to Councillor Henshall reminded me of an experience of my own in Landon 30 years ago. I was gtaying with Yorkshire relatives in Southamptonrstreet, off the Peckham-road (I wonder how many of my readers on their visits to the Metropolis have, eier crossed the river and explored the southern area), and we arranged to spend an afternoon at the Crystal Palace. We made the journey in one of the old third-class carriages, the partitions of which reached only half-way to the roof. These Continued in use on the Metropolitan Railway long after they had been superseded in the country. After a while, suspecting that some of our Cockney fellow travellers in the next 'compartment were listening to our conversation, one of the Yorkshiremen in our party said, "Let's tip 'em a bit o' th' owd dia- lect." Whereupon, raising our voices, we began to talk in the broadest of the dialects of the county of broad acres, and it was amusing to note the effect . upon the eavesdroppers. One after another they mounted their seats and stole furtive glances over the top to see what the barbarians using such 6 language looked like. •

THE EXAMINER, S'ATURDAY, NOVEMBER 27, 1915.

A SOULING.

A Curious November Custom Which Has Survived Since • Pre-Reformation Times.

OUNFIELD.

And all that dwells within your gates, We wish you ten times more. Soul, soul, &c.

The lanes are very dirty, My shoes are very thin, I have a little pocket To put a penny in. Soul, soul, &c.

The " souling " festival has been observed with many uncouth practices in several Che- shire towns and villages. At Tarporley and Northwich, and, we believe, at Lymm, the sdulers carried an imitation horse's head, which closed its jaws with vigorous snaps. In other places they gavel with blackened faces, a reminder of the black mourning dress of pre-Reformation times.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0241.txt | included=true | included =====
STAFFORDSHIRE HAS THE BEST VERSION.

The very best version of a " souling " song has lingered in Staffordshire. It retains the chief feature of the original hymn in the re- quest it makes for ' cakes and fruit, because the soulers have "all been praying for the soul departed." This expresses so well the purpose and idea of " souling " that it May be taken as the parent version from which the others have sprung: Soul day, soul day, We be come a-souling ; Pray, good people, remember the poor, And give us all a soul-cake. Soul day, soul day, soul, One for Peter, two for Paul, Three for Him who made us all. An .apple, a pear, a plum, or a cherry, Or any good thing to make us merry. Soul day, soul day, We have all been praying For the soul departed; So pray, good people, give us a cake,' For we are all poor people, Well known to you before; So give us a cake, for charity's sake, And our blessing we'll leave at your door. Soul! Soul! for an apple or two, If you have no apples, pears will do; If pears are scarce, then cakes from your pan, Give us our souling, and we'll be gone.

Altered versions of the souling song are to be found almost everywhere. There is, for instance:

If you haven't a penny, a ha'penny will do, If you haven't a ha'penny, a "God bless 'you." which has been perverted in the Warrington version: .If you haven't a ha'penny—God bless you.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0242.txt | included=true | included =====
A LANCASHIRE SURVIVAL.

It is rather remarkable that " souling " only seems to survive in the counties of Ches- ter, Salop and Stafford, and places' that lie clog to their borders. Possibly it is practised here and there without its being understood, but there are few references to it in books of folk-lord. The only allusion we have been able to find in the pages of volumes which deal with Lancashire is one from Harland's "Lancashire- Folk Lore "

There is a singular custom still kept up at Great- Marton, in the Fyldek district, to this day. In some places it is called "soul-caking," but tlTre it is named "psalm-caking "—from their reciting Psalms, for which they receive cakes. The custom is changing its character also—for in place of collecting cakes from house to house, as in the old time, they now beg for money. The term "Psalm" is evi- dently a corruption of the old , word " Sal " 'for soul; the Mass or Requiem for the dead was called " Sal-mass " as late as the reign .of Henry

The suggestion of this passage seems to be that the people. of the Flyde mistook the mean- ing of soul-caking, and made it Psalm-caking, setting the children the task of learning Psalms before giving them the cakes. John Aubtey, writing about 168 Q, said, con- cerning Shropshire : In Salop on the day of All Souls there is sett on the Board a high heap of soule-cakes lyeing one upon another like the picture of ' the Sew-bread in the old Bibles. They are about .the ,bignesse of 2d. cakes, and nly all the visitors that day take's one; and there is an old Rhythm or saying: A soule-cake, a soule-cake, Have mercy on all Christian souls

For a soule-cake.

This " souling " habit seems to have lin- gered in Shropshire until a very recent period,, but only in the form of gifts .to school chil- dren.. Spiced and sugared cakes were given on November Ist centuries after their mean- ing was forgotten—a very interesting instance of ,the tenacity of custom. 'Snatches of the Souling Song may be de- tected in many begging rhymes. In War- rington the ditty was transferred from All Souls' Day to the Guy Fawkes festival of November sth, and " a cob oT coal" took the place of apples and pears in the polite request for gifts.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0243.txt | included=true | included =====
WORTH PRESERVING.

We havli set forth here all the facts we have been able to glean concerning sonling songs, but our readers may he able to help us.

Possibly other versions may linger in the memory of old Cheshire or Warrington folk. If so, it would be good to preserve them, for " souling " will soon be a thing of the past.
