KAISER'S :NIGHTMARE.

Russia's Preparations For The Spring

. Campaign. The military correspondent of "The Times' says :

===== 0002601_19151127_art0245.txt | included=true | included =====
Russia's Situation.

The failure ,of. Germany to overrhelm Russia in the great offensive which began last May is likely to be as • much a turning point in the history of this campaign as was Napoleon's failure in 1812 during the lase Great War. Thanks to the heroic resistance of Ru,ssian troops under every conceivable disadvantage, but thanks also to several bad mistakes on the part of Falkenhayn, the Russiat armies have not been overthrown, nor have the vitals of Russia been penetrated. The armies, not hav- ing been overthrown, will fight on, while the, country, not having been conquered, will pro- duce fresh armies. Although German at- tempts to secure the Dvina line in the north and Rovno in the 'south are not yet completely suspended, it 'Seenis likely that our Russian Allies may have all the winter in front of them in which to recuperate. ' The whole of Russia is occupied night and day in turning out the arms and munitions which failed her earlier in the war and prevented her from developing her vast latent strength. Of all the nightmares which mu 4 • disturb the Kaiser's rest nothing more disturbing can murder sleep than the clang of the hammers throughout the Russian Empire, and the ever-increasing flow of warlike material into Russian arsenals from Allied and neutral States. Armed' at last, these grey heroes who have been waiting in their millions for the wherewithal to fight, will troop to the military frontiers in the spring, when the cm- paign will recommenek, and more furiously than ever. Who but a fool, on the Allied side, can think of peace?

DOWN THE VOLGA.

With The Russian Army On Europe's Longest River.

By HAROLD MAYBURY. Author of " A Russian's Impressions."

Mr. Maybury, the author of this article, will deliver a lecture on Russia and its people " at the whist drive to be held at Atkinson's Cafe on Thursday, December 2nd. As readers of " The Examiner " know, Mr. Maybury is quite familiar with this great sub- ject, and has the gift for putting his know- ledge in the most attractive fcrm.

Travelling down the Volga river is not a very profitable or educational experience, for the course of this mighty waterway is through, for the most part, marsh-land and swamps, where grouse and capercailzie have their abode. Like the people who itharbit its banks, it is slow of movement yet withal sure and invincible. The Volga river in ordinary times is devoid of more than the usual river traffic, but the present European crisis has been the means of added energy on the part of the seamen of the Czar's dominions. There is, a Sad lack of railway facilities in Russia, so the Volga is playing its due part in the movement of troops to the different theatres of war. . The vessels which ply along • this broad waterway are all of the American type, much the same as one sees trading along the Hudson river. To the Russian soldier they are veritable floating palaces, yet, in spite of his luxurious surroundings, Ivan ;s discontent and ill at ease. He does not feel at home; in fact, he would much prefer that his surroundings were not so clean and tidy, for the Russian soldier certainly does not believe in the axiom " Cleanliness is next to Godliness." In civilian life he will with cheerfulness journey miles to the Holy Church, there to pray before the sacred " Ikons " or pictures of the Madonna and to light a coloured candle on the altar, but he would not cleanse himself to visit the sacred edifice.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0248.txt | included=true | included =====
A BOAT SCENE.

And so it was with the soldiprs on the boat this early autumn morn. , They were unkempt and untidy, a motley throng .partaking of bowls of " stchie " and glasses of " kvas," without the nece'ssary preliminary ablutions. They sat at the tables shouting coarse jests and jokes at their countrymen, to the accompanying sounds of the boat's whistle and of the chugging of the vessel's engines. Every now and then the boat would stop at a badly constructed jetty to pick, up a few more units of the Russian Army. There could be seen the resin-tappers and charcoal burners who eke out a miserable existence in the lonely pine forests close by, together with the peasant women, plain .faced and broad of jowl, eyes set wide apart, with garments of mb.ny hues (probably of Lancashire manufacture) and kerchiefs round their heads, wishing their sons God-speed. The soldiers had finished their morning meal and had come on deck to welcome their new brothers. From the shore there arose a strange chant, which was sunewith infinite pity and tenderness ljy those left behind. Weird and melancholy, and sang with fervour, it reminded one .of "The Barcarolle "; then .slowly it died down and seemed to float away in the distance to lose itself in the steppes of this vast tract of country. It was a Russian hymn. Unlike the custom of our own land, on the ,departure of soldiers for war, • Russians do not make merry with patriotic or martial airs or comic songs, but instead they sing the Poetic, haunting and melancholy hymns which linger in one's mind after one has once hoard them. The Russian soldier is a .lovable iellow, generous to a fault, romantic and full of sentiment, with laughter one moment, quickly followed by tears the next. Ile only has a vague idea of what the war is all about, but uppermost in his mind is the thought that his Emperor called; and, there- fore, he takes it as a matter of course. Some- times he has a gnawing feeling•in his stomach for vodka, which he quickly dispels by " guzz- ling " enormous .quantities of " kvas," a ginger- beerish beverage made from half-soaked black bread. When he has had enough of this .he takes to drinking- tea, rich and amber hued, without milk of times, with slices of lemon in it. All Russian soldiers carry a kettle and a packet of tea, for they think nothing of imbibing anything up to twenty cups of tea per day and even more, after a hard day's march. Snow has now began to fall. Many of the peasant soldiers have donned their sheepskin coats and fur hats, jam-pot in shape. From the bulwarks of the boats can be seen the pine trees and firs taking new aspedts. they also have put on their mantles of gleaming white and 4R the soldiers see the large flakes falling they know that winter, with its dread hand, is nigh. Each day the peasant soldiers go through, a short drill, usually just before their mid-day meal, and so the days pass on.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0249.txt | included=true | included =====
LOVE OF MUSIC.

When night has fallen with its velvety folds o'er the scene and the moon is high in the heavens, then is there merriment on board. " Balalaikas " and mouth organs are brought out and the air is filled with music. Ivan sings heartily. He had a good voice, rich in tone and exquisite, brought about by a life in the open- air under God's sky. The songs he sings are mystic, usually with a touch of sadness in them. When the hour grows late the music gradually dies down, and he seeks repose, but not before he has made the shin of the Cross in front of the many, sacred pictures hung on the walls. To Western ideas it may seem childish, .but it is this childishness, so pensive and wistful, that endears the Russian to those who come in con- tact kvith him. He does not like to sleep in a berth, so be just lies down in any odd corner and sleeps untroubled until the morning breaks.

• When he awakes he wishes the "good Father," as he terms the ship's captain, "Good morning," and then proceeds to kiss his rifle prior to cleaning it, for he realises it has to be a staunch friend in future days. So the time passes on until the end of the journey is reached, which in this case is Odessa. When lie sets foot on land he looks round bewildered and in wonder, for, many of these sons of the soil have never before been in a great city.

he, just gathers up his \ kit and follows, like sheep, his conimander in the direction of the military barracks. He is at a loss as to how the tramcars move along without horses, and stares at the automobile, which to him seems so strange. He would dearly like to tarry at the big shops along the beautiful Deribaskaja, for there he sees much that interests him, or maybe he is thinking of wife and home. He wants to spend his few " kopeckv'" on some cheap article to take home when the war is over, see- ing that he cannot now buy "vodka."

===== 0002601_19151127_art0250.txt | included=true | included =====
CONTENT TO WAIT.

At his destination he promptly adapts him- self to his surroundings and to the other regi- mental soldiers there he wants to give away all his money, for the genial company makes him contented and happy. Life in the busy city has intoxicated him. He has been told of the doings of his CoUntrymen in Galicia at the beginning of the war, and he wants to be drafted quickly ro the firing line. . To him life is .held cheaply, for when he hears of the tolls of battle he merely exclaims " Nichevo "—God will pro- vide.

lie retires early whilst in barracks and sleeps soundly, for full well does he know there are no German cruisers prowling outside the Magnifi- cent harbour at Odessa at night., ready to create wanton destruction and havoc in the city. Oh, no, Ivan does not sleep with one eye open, for he knows he is safe. Then when the dawn breaks he slowly bestirs himself and looks longingly in the direction of, the Balkan States, where he knows eventually stern work awaits him. SO he watches and waits until the right time. ,

Perhaps some people talk incessantly in order to disguise the fact that they hare nothing to say.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0251.txt | included=true | included =====
CRUEL RUMOUR.

Protest From Gallipoli Against

False Home News.

LIFE AT THE FRONT.

After a silence of several months' duration, Corporal Frank Kay, of the A.S.C., a member of " The Examiner" reporting staff, addresses to us an interesting letter from the Dar- danelles.

Corporal Kay _writes "Once again I have the opportunity to write you a letter. "Through the Dardanelles."

"Of course I was exceedingly- glad to hear from home that we are through the Dar- danelles,' although one would have thought that, guided by the wise policy of first come first served,' those responsible would have enlightened us who are so near before, or at least as soon as, those at home. But no, the first news I got was' in a letter from someone in England. Even then, on reading my inform- ant's letter further, I gleaned the. fact that the news was to be kept secret Because they will not let us know officially as they say there .is no time for rejoicing whilst our poor soldiers are fighting so hard and suffering , so much.' But, alas for the fickleness of man, I really couldn't kee:p '.the secret,' no, not even to spare the feelings of the soldiers who are fighting so hard. I ran the ' risk' of causing premature rejoicing and dashed straight off with the good news' to all the boys. I don't know who I shall shock, but I must tell the' truth, there was great rejoicing, if boisterous laughter comes under that head- ing, and I think it does. -Tubby '—a really decent sort—' put .the lid on' by proudly re- marking Good old England, business as usual,' the company sang with great gusto "Comrades, don't believe him,' and dispersed for once in a way having foiled Mr. Rumour of his chance to make U 8 either anxious, pleased or sorrowful because we knew the truth. But such would not be the case in England, where no one was in a position to back up any doubts as to the veracity of the statement. By the majority of people it would be taken as the truth. The good news would he accepted eagerly and passed, on from mouth to mouth„ over the 'phone and by telegram—if the evolution of. a common or garden rumour can be taken as an example—it would not lose any of its weight in its trans- mission. Becoming intoxicated with the extra- ordinary news the people would naturally want to rejoice, but no, they must not; for why? What a most absurd reason! Surely. the attain- ment of an object which has cost so much was a just cause for the heartiest rejoicing. The real reason why, of course, is easily supplied, there was no truth in the rumour. There would have been nothing wrong but everything right in rejoicing.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0252.txt | included=true | included =====
Mischievous and Cruel.

"The mischief was in the rumour. I say ' mischief ' because it was a most mischievous and cruel rumour. Why do I say that? Be- cause I have in my mind's eye the picture of a little, middle-aged, sombrely clad widow—her husband died for his country somewhere in France '—seated by the fire quietly and calmly thinking of her eldest son in the Dardanelles. They are mixed thoughts, for her expression alternates between one of gladness and One of sadness. Yes, he was a fine lad, Was Jim,' she murmurs aloud; but why the past tense as if Jim were her's no more? Poor woman, she has not heard from him for some time, and,' she says it -herself, lie used to write every week.' Her reverie is disturbed by a loud impatient knocking at the door, and without waiting to be bidden, a neighbour—whose husband is also out here '—opens the door and rushes in with the news They're through the Dardanelles, so now it won't be long before we see our men.' Through the Dardanelles' repeats • the little, middle-aged women, then Jim will be home before long.' In the excitement of the moment, elated by the extraordinary ,toyfulness of the news, she forgot about the letter that had been delayed and even later, when it troubled her mind again she became very optimistic because of the 'good news." Why, she thought, 'the lad has been too busy; they must have all been too busy to write. I shall hear from him before long, and he will let me know all the news.' Did those two women rejoice? Well, you who have people out here,' how did you feel when you heard the good news'? or you who have men in Prance, didn't you think, with gladness, 'that will relieve 'men to go to France and so finish that part of the war where dear is very much quicker'? I should be sorry if those who heard the. news were not glad, but I am sure they were. It was not their fault. Well, my two women did. They re- ioieed together, and later on, separately, each in her awn heart offering up a prayer of thank- fulness.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0253.txt | included=true | included =====
Thoughts of "Jim."

'Well. she's no worse off than she was before,' one might say; 'she has benefited, for j space of time she was excessively happy and would not otherwise have been.' 1 liken that excessive hyppiness to excessive drinking, and call the results the same, misery deeper than ever.. ` Jim must have gone ,under. Suggested Useful Rumour.

"Before I leave you let me advise you as to your next. Start the ball rolling with the rumour ' All the men of military age and phy- sically fit, not employed on "real war work," are in the Army,' and give some strength to the story by enlisting yourself. Let that he your last, it will certainly be your best, and we will perhaps forgive you. "And to you who wait and watch: Treat rumours with contempt. You will get to know all officially as soon as ever it is expedient and wise to let you know. Be optimistic always, for things are going on quite all right, as you can see by ' the official news.'

From the quay side '—quite a credit to our engineers—the goods are conveyed to the depot in transport waggons. Our depot is quite close to the shore, 'so we have map, good use of the excellent bathing facilities afforded. Lately, however', it has gone terribly cold, so bathing has been out of the question. It never rains, but it pours.' Although that may not be always true in England, it has proved true out here oit the five occasions when , we have had wet weather. The first downfall we had flooded all the tents, and sleeping on the floor, as usual, was out of the question. Now we've all got beds. What art 3 they like? Well, I've got five biscuit boxes covered with my waterproof ground sheet. Then conies the mattress of one blanket and then another blanket, and my overcoat on top. For a pillow I use my trousers and tunic, so there you are. It's the best we can do, and I can

===== 0002601_19151127_art0255.txt | included=true | included =====
BY ARTHUR

Thirty years ago it was common in War- rington to hear a refrain sung by children from door to door: One for Peter, two for Paul, Three for Him that made us all.

It was sung in anticipation of November sth, and after some shocking doggerel con- cluded with a request: ,

If you please, m'am,

A Cob Of good coal

Many besides myself must have heard it without the least suspicion that it contained anything of interest. So devoid Of sense or consecutive ideas is it (we will not quote the Warrington version) that it comes as a sur- prise to discover that a similar song is warbled by the children throughout Cheshire and Shropshire, and is found here and there in Staffordshire and Lancashire also.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0256.txt | included=true | included =====
A PRE-REFORMATION MELODY.

The first .clue to its meaning comes from a study of the music. It is unattractive to the ordinary ear, but Dr. Joseph Bridge, who heard it often in the streets of Chester, pro- nounces it to be of first-rate interest. In his opinion, it is of ‘a period previous to the Reformation, and is of the exact style of the church music of the Catholic Church of four to five centuries ago.

Here is something attractive with which to make a" beginning—a church song at least four centuries old, sung by generations of children until almost every vestige of sense has vanished from the words and only stupid doggerel remains. Yet, however debased the wording, there remains in all the versions a reference to " soul "or " sole." Having some knowledge of the past to work upon, this one word is a sufficient clue. The song is a survival of the old habit of going " Soul- ing " on November Ist.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0257.txt | included=true | included =====
FOR SOULS IN PURGATORY.

In the Church calender All Saints' Day falls on November Ist. The day following is All Souls' Day. The festival of souls was estab- lished in the tenth century, and through the five centuries which followed it was customary to offer special prayers and masses for souls in purgatory. On the eve of All Souls, that is, the evening before November 2nd, the faithful used to parade the streets dressed in mourning. They rang a bell at each street corner and called upon everyone to pray for the souls in purgatory and to give something in money to pay for masses on their behalf.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0258.txt | included=true | included =====
SOUL CAKES.

It was supposed that during that night souls were released from purgatory and re- visited the scenes of their former life. In some countries the people spent the entire night in the churches, leaving their homes to be peopled by the restless spirits of the de- parted. Then arose the habit- of leaving the tables spread with cakes and other food, in order that the ghostly visitors might find good cheer. Beggars and thieves, such of them as were willing to risk the presence of the dis- embodied ones, had often a very rich time at the expense Of pious ones who had gone to church.. After the Reformation a different turn was given to the events of All Souls' Eve. The old habit of going round in black and with a clanging bell was kept up, but the" soulers," as they were Called, asked for food and drink for themselves. The cakes, which were made for the ghosts? were still baked, but were given to the visitors. Songs were prepared, which invited the householders to bring out the good things they had cooked for the fes- tival, and it is these songs that survive in the wretched little ditties to which reference has been made.

Here and there good versions' of these ancient songs may be discovered. A reading of the following from Shropshire will reveal the original idea at once: Soul! Soul! for a soul-cake, Pray, good missus ! a soul-cake, An apple, a pear, a plum, or a cherry, Or any good thing to make us merry— One for Peter, two for Paul. And three for Him who made us all. Up with the ladder and down with the can, Give us our souling and we'll he gone.

" Up with the ladder" meant place it to the apple loft, and " down with the can " suggests the ale cellar.

• A CHESHIRE SAMPLE. ' A Cheshire smiling song has four short verses and a refrain : Souling night has come at last, And we are sonling here; And all that we are souling for Is apples and good cheer. REFRA/N : SOW, soul, for an apple or two, If you've no apples, pears will do; If you've no pears, a good jug of beer Will last us all till this time next year;

God bless the master of this house, God bless the mistress, too; And all the little children that Around the table go. Soul, soul, &c.

Likewise your men and maidens, Your cattle and your store,

IRISH LEADER

Says Send The Pessimists To

The Front.

Mr. John Redmond, describing Isi experi- ences at the Front at a London zecruitiag meet- ing, :bore witness to the spirit of, high confidence which animated all ranks of the British Army, the splendid organisation for which the adminis- trative staff was responsible, and the brotherly feeling of the two sections of Irish soldiers. There are, he said, ,no pessimists at the Front, From the Commander-in-Chief down through all ranks of the Army there is but one 'feeling of absolute confidence in the result of this war. Germany Beaten In The West.

I take leave to say from my experiences there that there is a universal feeling of resentment against those people in this country who are spreading the spirit of pessimism. (Cheers.) It has been said to me over and over again: "Why is pot the all-is-lost brigade sent to the Front?" The one remedy for any man who is depressed and despOndent is to go and meet the troops at the Front. The real truth of the matter is this. So far as the western front is concerned Germany is beaten. (Cheers.) Every day and hour she is getting weaker on that front and we are getting stronger.. For every shell the Germans throw to-day We throw five, and anybody who would attempt .to spread pessimism in the English, French or Belgian lines to-day would meet with a very uncomfortable reception. The second broad impression I have brought back is the amazing charaCter of the organisa- tion. No one can. understand the organisation of that Army of a million men or more who has not .seen something of it. 'We wonder how the four or five millions a day is spent. A visit tO the Front is a great education—a great revela- tion. I thought I had an intelligent knowledge, speaking • generally, of .military affairs, but I found I was, in a state of profound ignorance. Anything more amazing than the spectacle of the English Army cannot be conceived. I drove hour after hour through miles and miles of motor-lorries carrying Munitions, food stores of all sorts, going froM the base to the Front, backwards and forwards, like clockwork; The -organisation Is perfect in every possible respect,

===== 0002601_19151127_art0260.txt | included=true | included =====
NAMES OF RIVERS, MOUNTAINS AND PLACES.

How They Confirm, Correct And lflustrate Early English History.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0261.txt | included=true | included =====
BY C. E.

Our ordinary English language—l mean words in common use--is composed, perhaps, in the proportion of seven-eighths, of words of Teutonic origin, mostly Saxon. - Only a few ol the remaining words are Celtic. These are mostly conserved in the names of rivers and mountains. Such names are not confined to the parts of the country to which the older inhabitants were driven by the Teutonic races, such as the North-West of. Scotland, Isle of Man, Wales: Ireland, and Cornwall, but apply to the whole of our island once peopled by the Celts. The names of our principal rivers and most of the mountain terms in Britain and Ireland are of Celtic origin. On the other hand' names of places are, in the main, Saxon, or a combination of Celtic and Saxon, such as King's Lynn. This is only natural. Rivers and mountains existed before the places on their banks.; and they change scarce at all.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0262.txt | included=true | included =====
RIVERS.

- The Celtic name for running. water was Avon or Afon. We have about seven rivers called Avon in England, an Afon in Wales, and an Afton" in. Scotland. We have also in Wales the Taff; the Tav3,-, and others contain- ing the same root. Then we have many com- binations of an adjective or descriptive form. For example, Avon-Banna, in Wexford, the ban meaning white Water. We have also the Ban, without the Avon, in the river Ban, in WexfOrd, and the little Stream in Scotland which gives the name to the battle of Bannock- burn. 'Then the Celtic Gawr, meaning rough; appears in Garry, Yarr, Yarrow, and on the Continent Garonne, and many more. The word Lynn is Celtic, and signifies a deep pool; King's Lynn, Lynton, Killin, Roslin. From the smile language we .have the_ word Cam, meaning , crooked, the Cam at Cambridge means a crooked river, while Morecambe in- dicates a crooked bay. Firth and its cognate form of Ford means an inlet from the sea. We have the Scotch Firths, and in Ireland many fords, as Wexford, Carlingford, etc. Deptford moans a deep ford. The Norwegian Fiords, are related. _

_ Naturally in Wales and in the Highlands of Scotland we find the names all of Celtic origin. We give a few of the common ones. The word Aber denotes the confluence of waters, say, where the rivers join the sea. Aberdare, Abergele, AberyAtwith, Abordovey. The word Inver, used by the Celts driven northwards, means the same as Aber, Inver- ness, Invergowrie. The word Ty, so often used as a part name for a dwelling-plaqe, means a cottage, Ty-Croes. Bod,, equally common, means a house. The word Kul in the Highlands has the same meaning, Killin. Nant, as in Nant-y-glyn, Nantclwyd, means a valley with a pool in it. Llan originally meant an enclosure, and in time became ap- plied to a sacred enclosure wherever there was a church or the abode of a saint; for ex- ample, Llandudno was the abode of St. Tudno.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0263.txt | included=true | included =====
MOUNTAINS.

Coming now to the mountains, we have. amongst the southern Celts the word Pen, meanidg a head or highland, corresponding to the term used by the northern Celts, Ben. We have the Pen in Appenine, Pennine Range, Pentland Hills, Pennigant, Pendleton, Penrith, Penmaenmawr, Penrhos, and in Scot- land the Ben, .Ben Nevis, Ben Ledi, Ben Lomond. The word Bryn, so often used. in Wales, is Celtic, and means a ridge, Bryn- mawr. Dun means a bill fortress, as in Dun- keld, Dundalk, Dundee, Dunkirk.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0264.txt | included=true | included =====
SCANDINAVIAN.

A number of names used in connection with rivers and mountains come from Norwegian or Scandinavian sources, sometimes called Vie Old Norse, such as beck, a stream; fell, a mountain side; the mountain, Snaefell (Snow- fell) ; scarf, gap, etc.; these are of local rather than of general use. We can fairly well mark the land occupied by the Scandinavians by the help of the names from this 'language. We find such names in abundance in the Isle of Man, in Shetland, Cumberland, and Westmor- land. Both Celtic and Scandinavian names are very descriptive and indicate sOme dis- tinctive quality or character in the object to which they refer, and are expressive of the leading feature ,of the scenery. Thus the name of a hill frequently indicates if it is high and peaked, low and round, rough or smooth, dark or pale, elongated or short. The same thing applies to valleys, dales, rivers, and so forth.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0265.txt | included=true | included =====
SAXON.

When we come to the Saxon place names we find these have reference more to individuals and made conditions, rather than natural features. Places are often named after a great

CRUEL RUMOUR.—Continued.

assure you that after doing from 6 a.m. to 9 p.m. we sleep like tops, and nothing can waken us before our time. A short time ago we were given a great deal of attention by enemy airmen, who came over and dropped bombs. These raids are always interesting, for one never knows where the pears' will drop. However, we have not had /any dangerously near us, so-have not had the chance to pick a bit up as a souvenir for you. Fine Gunnery.

'I have been to the new landing at Sulva Bay, and on the occasion of my visit I saw a piece of fine gunnery work—a ship lying well ' off shore' dropping shells in. a line right across the enemy's position. The , Turks try their best to hit transport and stores trawlers as they stand in the bay, bytt the nearest they got to the trGwler I was on was about 30 feet astern. There is very little that is spectacular to see, for trench warfare has practically put an end to those stirring battle scenes of bygone days. The only real chance now, is when there is a charge. When there is a heavy bombardment the noise is terrific, but we get .used to hearing it, and have no trouble in sleeping at night. " Well, I think that is all I dare tell you at present, so in conclusion let me say in answer to peace cranks: We delft want peace until that which we set out to accomplish has been accom- plished.' Of course, we should like to be home very much. Wouldn't anyone else? But if the price to be paid is a dishonourable and dis- creditable peace we say 'No, thank you!' " Yours faithfully, "FRANK KAY."

===== 0002601_19151127_art0269.txt | included=true | included =====
ARKER.

warrior or the first proprietor, and sometimes from the' character of their buildings or en- campments. Thus Haddington was the original residence or town of Haden; Edin- burgh, the burgh or fortified -settlement of Edwin, both Anglo-Saxon chiefs. We have the Saxon Weorthing, from which ite .get tho English. Worth, which denotes a valuable point warded or protected, as in Worthing, Bos- worth, Wandsworth, Handsworth, Walworth. A weir 'in a stream springs from the same root, a weir meaning a warded or protected portion of a stream. Borough is, Saxon, mid means a walled enclosure, embodying the idea of a Shelter, as Middlesbrough, Peterborough (German, Borg, Borgen). We also have Ton, or Tim, which forms the termination of so many of our English towns and villages. This would mean among the Saxons something in the character of a parish with a central group forming a village, but of a civil character and not ecclesiastical. The word Ham, which also forms the termination of,many place names, indicates st. house or a hamlet, and is the origin of our word home; Scotch halm, Lanca- shire whoam. Ford means a crossing, say, for rivers, as Oxford, Waterford,. Hereford, and Knutsford. This is the same word as in the Celtic and Old Norse, but in the Saxon is applied to rivers and never to the. sea. ,

The evident Saxon 'connection with some of the names of counties will be familiar to most: Essex (East Saxons), Wessex (West Saxons), Sussex (South Saxons), Middlesex (Middle Saxons), Norfolk (North folk), Suffolk (South folk). Referring to Scotland, we are apt to think that the Scotch are descendants of the Scots, or of the Picts and Scots. These were only comparatively small tribes. The Scots came over from Ireland and occupied part of Argyll- shire, and in some mysterious way gave their name to the whole of the country. But the Lowland Scotch as far north as thb 'Firths of Tay and Clyde are descendants of Teutonic races, mainly Saxon. There' is little doubt these people came over 'from the Continent, and probably a portion of them in advance of the time we usually ascribe to the Saxon invasion of Eng- land. They droye the Celts and the other tribes in possession of the country to the Highlands of the west and north. For con- firmation of this fact we again rely upon the place names of the Lowlands of Scotland. The Scotch dialect as used by Burns is therefore allied to our English Anglo-Saxon, and has little or no affinity with the tongue spoken by the ancient tribe of Scots. Its inherent characteristics confirm this.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0270.txt | included=true | included =====
DANISH.

The Danish names give us some indication of the portions of the country at one time occupied by these North Sea rovers. We have scarcely a single Danish place name in Lanca- shire. There are sixty-three Danish Thorpes (village) in Lincolnshire, several in Yorkshire, not one in Lancashire, and only one in Cum- berland, :while the Scandinavian Thwaite occurs forty-three times in Cumberland and seldom in other counties. It is somewhat curious that in the Cheshire Wirral Peninsula a great number of the names are Danish. It is asserted by those Who have investigated the matter that of the old place names there is not one of Saxon origin. I question if the same can be said of any other part of the country.' There are other proofs that have been ascoVered confirniing the supposition that this peninsula was at one time a popu- lous Danish settlement.

A:question is discussed by Doctor Whittaker which shows how names help us to locate the divisions of the people that used to occupy our own county. There was a dispute, now set at rest, as to whether under the Ileptarehy the Mersey or the Ribble was the dividing line between Northumbria and Mercia. It IS now gegerally acknowledged that the Ribble is, or is k bout, the proper boundary, and not the Mersey. Dr. Whittaker claims support for this in the different terms employed to name natural objects and places north and south of the boundary, indicating that the Northum- brians and Mercians, although mainly of Saxon origin, were separated somewhat :by ifferent dialects. The following words give support to this contention : Northumbria. Mercia.

Fell. Edge, as Blackstonedgc. Beck. 1 Brook (Scotch burn). Tarn. Mere (a small lake). How. Know (a small hill). Toft. Bolt (a tuft of trees). Gill. Clough (Scotch glen). ' Thorpe. Wick. The Northumbrian terms, some of which are Norse, apply and are used north of the Ribble, and the Mercian to the south.

FARM AND GARDEN.

A USE FOR POACHERS.

A Dereham man in the Bth Norfolks, in ti letter home, says :—" I am very glad now that I did a lot of night poaching, as I am very quick to catch the slightest sound when I am on sentry, and very quick to spot anything in the dark. The boys can sleep in peace when I am on duty."

===== 0002601_19151127_art0279.txt | included=true | included =====
DEMAND FOR RABBITS.

So great has become the demand for rabbits in some of the big centres of population in the Midlands and the North that thousands of West- country trappers are having to work at high pressure. ' Rabbit trains are being run over POMO of the branch lines, and in districts re- mote from the main line enterprising dealers have established services of motor lorries to bring the rabbits from the farms at which they have been caught. It is said, states the "Standard," that farmers in some a the moor- land districts of Devon will pay their rent this year out of rabbits trapped on their farms.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0280.txt | included=true | included =====
MILK FROM HEIFERS AND COWS.

Which is the richer, milk from cows or heifers? The question is frequently propounded and dairymen are not at all unanimous in giving a reply. Prof. W. A. Henry in "Feeds and Feeding," states that the milk of heifers is richer than that of the same individuals at a later date. A booklet • recently has been sent out by the Holstein-Freisian Association which is important, as it contains records of 634 cows on test. By reveiwing the data we find that the heifers apparently gave slightly richer milk than the mature cows. These records cover only tests for a week and a month, with a few extending over 60 days, but yearly records would probably substantiate the data for the shorter periods.—" Breeders' Gazette."

===== 0002601_19151127_art0281.txt | included=true | included =====
PIGS ON-PASTURE.

It is now generally conceded, writes W. F. Purdue in the "Indian Farmer," that the cheapest as well as the easiest way of growing pork animals is to feed grain while the pigs have the run of good pasture. Any gain made from pasture or forage crops is the cheapest gain possible to make, and at the same time the pasture keeps the hog's system in fine shape. Forage crops answer a triple purpose in pork production. First, while the pig is primarily adapted to concentrated feeds, at the same time it requires some roughage. Grain feeds do not bulk up well in the stomach and are not easily penetrated by the gastric juices of the stomach, and for this reason the appetite is better,main- tained and digestion is improved by the use of some bulky feeds at all t:mes of the year. Secondly, most forage crops supply sufficient protein to balance a corn ration nicely; and finally, they furnish the pigs with exercise, and exercise is essential for active and healthy vital organs in the growing animals. Even during the finishing process, it is considered advisable to allow the pigs afli much exercise as they care to take. The more concentrated their feed, the greater the necessity for some exercises every day. Substantial fences around the hog lots and pasture fields are essential if the hogs are to be kept under modern conditions.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0282.txt | included=true | included =====
WOMEN AS FARM WORKERS.

The subject of the suitability of farm work for women is naturally opening up controversy among all classes and in all parts of the country. One thing is very certain, viz., that, even if it does not exist at the present moment, there will be a very great scarcity of male labour in the coming year in the country districts. For many years the practice has died out of the farm labourer's wife helping on the thrashing days, or with root cleaning, or even hay-making, and with the exception of parts of Scotland, it is quite a novelty to see a woman working in the fields. But the question of labour must be faced now that all ranks- of male workers are depleted in order to supply and maintain the c6mplement of soldiers needed. This is where the women of England must at once come for- ward, and unhesitatingly show their courage and capabilities., There are many who say that women themselves are the obstacles; but Lord Selborne quoting the statement that "Jt is no good going to the women, and asking them to work on the land," said he did not believe it. and added that Lord Kitchener told him that thm was "nothing he had done in making hie I new armies " which people had n9t said "was impossible."

Although farrning Is hot usually tlti§sod among

===== 0002601_19151127_art0284.txt | included=true | included =====
skilled work, yet it is perfectly obvious to any- one who has had any experience of the work that it is useless for a woman who is quite foreign to it, and the customs of the country, to offer herself to a farmer with the idea that she can be of use straight away, because she must at the outset require some apprenticeship, as would be the case in any other calling. The Pity of it is that more landowners do not plact! the opportunities which tliey have for offering training to women at the disposal of those who are seeking it, and by so doing carry on a really patriotic work. Personally I am in the fortu- nate position of being empowered by ,my employer to offer free board and lodging and training to a limited number of women who wish to qualify as workers, as he himself 'con- siders it of real importance to the country that there should be women ready to carry on farm work, and therefore has originated and, is pro- viding the funds for putting into action a scheme of training on the estate.-31. IN THE GARDEN.

THE cpouN MOTH.

If during the past season the codlin moth has been troublesome no time should be lost in taking steps to check it by applying grease bands. The first thing to do is to wrap strips of brown paper round the stems of the trees at about one foot from the ground, smearing these with. cart grease or. Any sticky substance. On no acCount allow the grease to come into contact with the bark of the tree. Never allow the bands to become dry, but if this should happen smear them again without loss of time.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0286.txt | included=true | included =====
TOO MANY APPLES.

I am very often asked to give a list of the best half-dozen dessert apples, and the best half- dozen cooking kinds. This is very difficult, as tastes Vary greatly. The nurseryman's list of varieties is far too long, but he has to study his customers and their varied tastes, . while ,considerationlias alse.to- be,kiien. to the, district and soil in which the trees are to be grown. We often- find that a certain apple: grOwN,..tuld crops freely in one district,' while' in the neighbonr- ing county it may he a. total failure. It is all very well for the man who is thoroughly con- versant with the various sorts of apples and their peculiarities to make a selection, but how bewildering must be these long lists • to the amateur and the cottager who have space only for a few trees. This is not all, as thc visitor to a fruit show is led astray by the huge. highly-coloured specimens on view. These, to fl! them for the exhibition table, have been thinned and the trees gorged with liquid manure. The visitor to the exhibition, too. finds the same varieties repeated time after time in the several classes.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0287.txt | included=true | included =====
PLANTING FRUIT TREES.

Many people, no doubt with good intentions, when planting fruit trees mix a lot of manure with the soil, thus causing gross, sappy wood which will never do any good so far as fruit bearing is concerned. Far better ,is it, if the soil is not very good, to remove some of it and in its place put some good fibrous loam, which will answer for several years. To encourage the formation of fibrous roots on the surface mulch with some well-rotted manure. Another fault of adding manure round the roots is that they go down deeply, failure sooner or later following, and necessitating in time root prun- ing and the lifting of the trees. For the amateur I should advise trees on the Paradise, as on this they soon come into bearing. When this happens liquid manure or top dressings of rotten manure should be given, more especially when the fruits are swelling.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0288.txt | included=true | included =====
RHUBARB AT CHRISTMAS.

If any reader has some well established stools of rhubarb it is an easy matter to have a dish for the Christmas dinner by placing a tub or box over one or two roots and covering with leaves to a distance of 2.feet on all sides beyond the stool, to be forced,. and from a foot to 18 inches above the top of the. box. Leave an open- ing at the top of the tub so as to allow • rank steam to escape. To prevent the leaves blowing about it is well to place some strawy litter over them. If a little horse manure can be mixed with the leaves it will help the leaves to heat, and thus hasten the production of the rhubarb stalks.—Wick, in "Farm and Home."

===== 0002601_19151127_art0289.txt | included=true | included =====
CHESHIRE ICE TRAGEDY.

Eight Victims Of A Sunday Morning _ _ _

Accident.

An inquest was held at the David Lewis Epileptic Colony, Chelford, Cheshire, on the eight victims of the ice fatality in the neigh- bourhood of the 'colony last Sunday morning. The deceased were Edith Walker (20), Dorothy Gething (30), Elizabeth Mullens (26), and Marion Hannah Frost (24), nurses at the colony; William Wyse (21), George Davey (25), and Walter Barrable (27), patients at the colony; and Elsie Parry (19), daughster of a farm' bailiff, on whose property the accident urred.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0290.txt | included=true | included =====
Father's Terrible Ordeal.

James Parry, farm bailiff, said that at the time of the accident some sixty people were on the pond, which was 14 feet deep. They had been playing football and hockey, and then two sledges were brought out and loosely fastened together with rope. Suddenly, with- out warning, the ice cracked, and the sledges, containing five persons and these pushing and pulling them, were thrown into the water. Witness went on to the ice, but it broke. Someone threw a railing and he got out. He endeavoured to get hold of his daughter, and walked on the ice for a couple of yards. it again gave way beneath him, and he was once more thrown into the water, but some wire was thrown to him and he got out again. He heard \his other daughter, Enid, call, "Dad! Dad and he threw the wire to her. She was close to a sledge, and .he told her to hold fast to it. He then threw a rope to her and she*was. rescued.- His daughter" Elsie had then disappeared. Dr. A.PDougall, medical superintendmt at the colony,' then arrivSil, and. jumped into the water with his clothes on: Ropes- were thrown to him, and .he was 'able ,to save several people... The-\Coroner : Did you not object to sledg+,- ing?—No.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0291.txt | included=true | included =====
Ice Two Inches Thick.

Did you not think it foolish?—No. The lee was two inches thick, aid we thought it was quite safe. The witness added that there was no life- saving apparatus about.

An inmate said he ,Saw the ice moving, but did not take any action, as one of the victims had told him he could 'walk out of the pond. There was no horseplay. The patients who were drowned .Tent to the rescue of the others. T-- 'liinged IMO the' wator without a minute's hegtation, • __ : .

, • Dr, MV-dgall gave evidence that death was tine in . each, case to shock. He stated that whqn he arrived on the scene he went to assist a patient. named Fisher, who was in the water in an epileptic fit. He was struggling, and witness did not know, how ho got out. The accident was caused by too many people being at one point at once. The ice would have held if the people on it had been more scattered., The Coroner Complimented the patients and others who had taken part in thg rescue work, and also Dr. ArDougall, whose modesty, the Coroner said, had prevented his recthinting all he had done.

The jury returned a verdict of "Accidental death."

INTERMEDIATE LEAGUE.

LAST SATURDAYS- 'GAMES.

PAROCHIAL'S SUBSTANTIAL WIN.

The attendance on the Wilderspool ground on Saturday was bad. Not more than fifty spec- tators were present to 'witness the • match be- tween Parochial and St. Mary's, and included amongst these were a few wounded soldiers. Throughout the match the superiority of the Parochial lads was in evidence. Early- on they pressed St. Mary's into their own quarters, and soon a try resulted. This- proved to be the only score during the first half, though both sides tried hard to break through. Much time and effort were wasted by fruitless kicking. Witt. ,ends reversed, the game practically developed into. a one-sided affair, Parochial having the game in their own hands. On many occasions the St. Mary's line was in danger, but their full-back, generally managed to pull the fat out of the fire.. The Parochial for*ards -gave a, few fine exhibifiattsA:lf. passing, and soon :eh in the second half one of these resulted in a try being scored against the touch-line. They wodld have repeated this performance within a •few minutes but for a forward pass :when practi- ,e'iitrk .ep,the line. Forward passing,had been a weakness on the part of the ParoChiallads, but, after this lesson, they did not err in this- respecit again. Keeping up the pressure till the end of the game, the Parochial crossed the St. Mary's line on two more occasions, and at the close , of, the game the seore,.,read-12. points to nil in their favour.. Thouglf:they,scored four.tries, in :each .case., the Parochial'. failed, ,to add.. the additional points, but perhaps,. the greasy con- dition of both, ground and ball was, responsible for this.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0295.txt | included=true | included =====
BEAMONT TOO GOOD FOR SACRED HEART.

The Beamont v. Sacred Heart match, which was also Played at W4derspool, was a very even affair, but if Beamont had taken all their opportunities the game would have resulted in more than a 5 point win in their favour. At the commencement the Sacred Heart had the better of play, but good work on the part of the Beamont backs gradually wore down their attacks. Play ranged from one end of the field to the other, until about 20 minutes from the start, when Beamont were awarded a free-kick pra6tically in front of the Sacred Heart goal. But the7attempt to score the first two points of the game was a- miserable failure. A few minutes later, however, Beamont were given a similar opportunity, and this time they made no mistake, and two points were credited to them. This was the only score during the first half.

Beamont had the game well in hand during the second portion, and about ten minutes be- fore the end they scored a try. The attempt to add a further two points was good—but un- successful, the 1)911 striking the under side of the crossbar. No further points were added and the game ended with the score 5 points to nil in favour of the ex-Council school lads.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0296.txt | included=true | included =====
HEATHSIDE BEAT BOLTON.

The match between Heathside and Bolton in Victoria Park, was a very fast one throughout and, unfortunately, towards the end it became rather rough. _ _ _

Bolton started, and the ball being returned, Heathside were quickly on the attack. They were gradually driven back, however, and were penalised, but the kick at goal was a failure. From the kick out, Heathkide came up again with fine'dash, and a grand passing,movement took play to, the Boltonite's.line. .There one of the defenders did not play the ball, and he 16 S promptly Tenalised: - The goal-kick was. sitccess- Heathivide- were now playing a grand game, and once again invaded their opponents' territory. A number of , desperate scrimmages were fought out on, the liñe, where a Dolton threequarter-back made a wretched kick, send- jag the ball :straight into the hands of the Heathside left-wing. The latter promptly took advantage of the mistake, and running with great dash, scored at the corner, despite the efforts of two or three opponents to stop him. The goal-kick was not a success. Bolton now seemed to lose heart, and before half-time another try and goal had been recorded against them, the interval arriving with the score ten points to nil in favour of Heathside.

In the second half Bolton made desperate efforts to retrieve their position, but could not do so; whereas lleathside added another two noints to their total—a goal for obstruction. Nothing more was scored and, thus, when the final. whistle sounded, the score read: Ileathside, three goals, two tries, 12 points; Bolton, nil. As previously stated, the game became rough towards the end, and as a consequence the referee had occasion to caution several players.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0297.txt | included=true | included =====
RYLANDS'S EASY WIN.

Rylands's and St. James' met in Victoria: Park before a fair number of spectators. The commencement of the match was considerably delayed owing to several of the St. James' men failing to put in an appearance. From the kick-off Rylands's wore soon, on the attack, but a good punt by one of the St. James' three- quarters eased the situation. Play was then confined to midfield, where Sloane, the Rylands't half-back, received the ball, and started a pass- ing movement, which did not terminate until the ball had been taken to the " Saints' " line and Mulvey had scored a good try, Molyneux landing the goal In the second half the St. James' men could stop Rylands's forwards from making further progress, tries being scored by Barlow, Butlex and Thomas. Archer also kicked a goal. When time was called the score was :—Rylands's Recs., 16 points; St. James', nil. The most prominent players were Archer, Butler, the Rylands's .pack, and tho St: James' full-back and half-backs.

ASSOCIATION.

On Saturday there was only one game in the Warrington and District League down for deci- sidn',* and the Elmwood ,Congregationalists, as visitors to Paddington, were unable to raise full team. They played a plucky game, as in the first half the home team were only able to register one point, and the game was well ad- vanced in the second half before the home team gained the upper hand. After the home team scored their second point the visitors' defence fell away and at the call of time the score was five against none. Rothwell, the home centre- forward, claimed four goals.

A benefit match had been arranged between Hollins Green and the Warrington Central Club, the proceeds from the "gate" to be devoted to the benefit of W. Mellor, of Hollins Green, who, it -will be remembered, was injured last season in the Starkey Cup ties between the same clubs. It is to be regretted that only five of the Central Club players arrived at the Central Station to travel by train. As it was no use going with so few players, a telegram was sent to the Hollins Green Club notifying them of their inability to raise a team. This- is the second occasion that a match has been arranged,for the benefit of this unfortunate player, with the same result, the visiting team failing to travel.

Saturday was, a date, on. which_ the members of the .Junior League had decided to. havaa rek from 'League' strife, but enthusiasm, for. the game proved too,mxiell for the Fairfield United Reserves and the Bewsey.AthletioXinbS. They decided to play a fixture which had to be post- poned earlier•in the season-. Owing to the tri- umph of the Athletic over their local rivals-the previous week, there were visions of success in the Athletic camp over their 'More formidable rivals, the United Reserves. There was a good crowd of supporters to witness a very fast and keen game. At the interval the visitors were leading by a couple of points, and after the third goal had been scored against them the home team gave up the ghost, and only indulged in defensive tactics. They could not prevent the visitors adding further goals, and in the end the Athletic were defeated by six dear goals.

Holden and Boyle were very good on the United Reserves' left wing.

Lockhart, Tunstall, Cooper and Woodhouse wore a very strong defence.

Bradbury and Roylance were the most promi- nent for the Athletic.

Last week's sham fight between Latchford Cadets and the Stockton Heath Scouts resulted in an easy victory for the Cadets., The Scouts were poorly represented. Not half their mem- bers turned out.

To-day the Junior League will be in full swing. All the clubs are engaged and some keen struggles will be witnessed.

The match of the season so far will be decided on the Arpley ground, where the League leaders. St. Elphin's, will be opposed by the Fairfield United Reserves. The Saints have won all their games played up to date, while the United have only lost one and at the present are playing very strong, and have now got their best team together. Will the Saints be able to hold the United in cheek?

. Another game which is sure to be keenly con- tested is between the:Stockton-Heath scouts and the Latchford Cadet Corps, on the Higher Wal- ton ground. Much rivalry exists between these clubs, and the Scouts. declare they will make amends for their. defeat in the .shum, fight.

Another local ." Derby" is the game between Mersey Juniors and Howley- United: As both clubs originate from the same locality, there is sure to be some keen struggles for the points.

Emmanuel Institute, when they appeared to have got the measure of their opponents, became too confident, ,and were fortunate in making a draw.

Cairo-street Unitarians were disappointed in their fixture, owing to St. John's cancelling the match. They were unable to get a full team to play.

• T. Whitfield, one of the most popular members of the Great Sankey team, has enlisted. He is the fourth member of the club to respond to the call quite recently.

GERMANY RUINED.

Lord Ineheape On Enemys Reckless

'War Finance.

The Rake's Progress of German war finance was remarked upon on Wednesday night by Lord Inchcape,- one of the most eminent of our commercial men, whose talents, throughout a brilliant career, have been devoted both to busi- ness and diplomacy. As President, he was addressing the Institute of Bankers, He said that we had avoided the slippery paths on which our enemies had embarked. The financial policy, of Germany bore the mark of recklessness and improvidence. None of the money required for the war had been raised by increased taxation, and each suc- cessive loan meant a fresh inflation of the cur- rency, which showed itself in a steady rise in prices. The more the procedure was persisted in, the more would prices rise, and all official at- tempts to fix maximum prices, though they might be successful for a time in the case of individual commodities, would be powerless to meet the situation. ; The task of redeeming the securities and re- 'tiring the notes would be left for an exhausted and defeated nation to face after the conclusion of peace. The .Cievernment of this country had resisted all temptations 'to unSounds methods. As certain as the sun will rise to-morrow," he concluded, " Germany and her militarism will be crushed, and the peace of the world, so far as anything she can do to prevent it, will be 'secured for another hundred years."

RAILWAY FARE ALLOWED.

PHYSICAL TRAINING.

Its Importance In School Curriculum. A meeting of the Educational Handwork Association was held in the Wycliffe Memorial Hall on Thursday evening, Mr. Holman King- don being in the chair. The Chairman, in his opening address, empha- sised the need for physical training, especially at the present time, and pointed out that at the works with which he was connected there was, before the war, a company of Territorials, and the members of it were better workmen because of the training they had. The Aim Of Physical Exercises. Mr. Cole (superintendent of. Physical Train- ing for the Sheffield Education Committee) gave. a lecture on "The aims and objects of physical training; the choice of exercises and their uses." He first pointed out the lifted for physical training, especially in towns where men were working over benches, and in bad atmospheres. ' and where, children had little opportunity of getting pure air. The aim of this training . should be the health and physical efficiency of the child. There should be no aim at muscular - strength, but all parts of the body should be developed so that the organs of the body, would he capable of their utmost effort, yet work in co-ordination with all other parts, the whole being under the control of the mind. The lecturer indicated that there were three effects to be aimed for (1) nutritional effect, shown by a healthy appetite (2) structural effect, got iby attention to correct carriage and museu ar balance, and (3) educational effect, chieflY P"- dueed in learning of new exercises. In speakilvg +0 of the choice of exercises. Mr. Cole referred the Board of Education drill book as being an admirable book; but said that it was necessary for the teacher of phys4al exercises to take it course in the subject so that he should first know the exercises and be able to predict the resultant effect. _

===== 0002601_19151127_art0327.txt | included=true | included =====
"Teach A Little And Do A Lot."

The lecturer called attention to the arrange- ment of exercises, -which must be strictly adhered to, so that one set of muscles were rest- ing whilst another set were working. There should be progression. He showed how each exercise could be increased in difficulty so as to be suitable for children of varying ages, and said that he would make it a rule to teach a little and do a lot in one lesson. Running. jumping and games must not be left out of the exercises.

A vote of thanks was accorded the lecturer on the proposition of Mr. Appleton, seconded by Miss Gandy, and supported by Mr. Morgan. Mr. Longshaw proposed a vote of thanks to the Chairman, and Mr. White having seconded, this was also carried.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0328.txt | included=true | included =====
" PICTURES " FOR PATRIOTS.

Effort To Provide Cinema For Wounded Warriors.

The " Tommies " tea arranged to take place at the Parr Hall on Thursday, December 9th. is sure to be a great success, for the objects for which it has been arranged are such that thare can be no hesitation on the part of anyone about lending a helping hand. The chief object, for instance, is to provide funds, for the installa- tion of a permanent cinematograph at the Lora Derby War Hospital at Winwick. There are over a couple of thousand patients at the hos- pital, and it is essential that they should bo provided with some kind of recreation. That' the " pictures " are popular with the troops is evidenced by the fact that in the garrison towns of England, thousands of the men flock to the cinema shows night after night. And if. " Tommy " when well revels in "the pictures," " Tommy " when convalescent is twice as keen on them, .for they are wonderful and pleasant time-passers. Ergo, all who subscribe to the provision of cinematographs in military hos- pitals will have their reward in the knowledge that they have brightened a few hours for the men who have gone through deadly peril and sore pain for them.

===== 0002601_19151127_art0329.txt | included=true | included =====
WINWICK'S DAILY CIGARETTE BILL.

At the present time it costs at least £2 a day to keep the men in cigarettes, so it is easy to see that substantial funds are required to pro- vide the men with the more substantial luxuries which grateful admirers would wish them to have.

The committee who have the arrangements for the tea in hand are under the presidency of the Mayor of Warrington (Alderman Peter Peacock), whilst the chairman is Councillor Henry. Roberts. The tea will be under the patronage of the Right Hon. the Earl of Derby. Sir Gilbert Greenall. Lady Greenall, Sir Arthur Crosfield, Lady Crosfield, Mr.- Harold Smith, M.P., Lieutenant-Colonel Simpson, and others. There will be six stalls in the'Parr Hall on the occasion of the tea party, and visitors are asked to send or bring there articles for sale and also to make a purchase. Gifts of needle- work. groceries, confectionery, fruit, flowers. household "articles and farm produce will be appreciated. The band of the 2nd South Lanca- shire Regiment will play selections, under 01' conductorship of Mr. R. E. Cahill. The charge for admission is very low, being only ed.

Preston, whose home address is in Winwick-road and who wrote that he saw private Edmonds's body hanging in some wire entanglements in front of the German trenches. Three men vol- unteered to go and try to get the body, and in the attempt one of the trio was killed and an- other wounded. His Last Letter. Mrs. Crozier received the last letter from her nephew just before the battle of Loos. In that letter he said: "Give my, best respects to all, at home, as we are about to go in for a warm ' time. I think I shall get through it, but if I do not it will not matter.. Good-bye and good luck." Father and Many Relatives in the Ranks. Private Edmonds, who was quite a youth, en- listed soon after the outbreak of war, having, previously been employed by Messrs. monks, Hall and Co. He attended the Sacred Heart Church, and at one time played football with the team organised in connection with that place of worship. He had been at the Front since March. His father is serving in France with the 2nd Battalion of the South Lancashire Regiment. He also fought in the Boer War, during which campaign he was wounded. In- cluded among other relatives of the dead man who are also fighting are three uncles and several cousins. SAPPER E. CHILTON. Shot In The Back By A German . Sniper. sports he took more than ordinary interest, and waa'a faithful follower of the Warrington Rugby Football Club. As a youth he attended the Wycliffe School and worshipped at St. Paul's Church, being a member of Mr., Wilkes' Bible Class connected with this place of worship. Previous to enlistment, in September of last year, he was a plater in the employ of the Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Co., Ltd. A brother-in-law of Sapper E. Chilton, Private Leigh, is at present in training with a new battalion of the King's (Liverpool) Regiment. A Big Friend To All. The following letter is from Sapper H. Jack- son, a comrade in the same regiment as the decease 4 "It is with deepest regret that myself and Sapper Worcester, on behalf of the section, senu our sympathy in your sad bereavetnent on the loss of your son, Eddie. Personally, I was there with him in the front line of trenches on the morning, of the 11th inst., when he was shot in the back by a German sniper, his death being instant. We did all in our power for him. The next day he was buried by the Chaplain of the North Lancasters in a British ,cemetery just behind our lines. All his personal property we have handed over to headquarters, which will be sent' on to you at the earliest date. He was a big friend to every-body:in the section, and is missed'very much by all of us." '

• TWENTY-TWO. TWENTY-TWO At the end of the week, if he'd seen it through. We left his grave in the cure's hands; I'met him as I was coining away— A white-haired man in cassock and bands— And I showed him whore it lay. "Twenty-two-- Yet he's older than you or me, lesieu, And the riddle of time for him is read. Yes. I will see the grave kept trim, And after the prayers for our own are said I will add a prayer for him." Twenty-two— Someone will bitterly weep for you; Yet she'll lift her head with a wonderful pride: "He was my son, and his life he gave. Shall I grudge slidh a gift, tho' my heart has • died? He was brave: I must be brave." Twenty-two= , • Ah! for the dreams that can never come true: All that the world should have had in store! He was willing to die, thongh he loved to live. •We must be ready to follow—the' more) That we've many less years to give.-."Puneh."

WOUNDED. GUNNER G. C. SANT. One Of Three Soldier-Brothers. Gunner George Charles Sant, of the Royal Garrison Artillery, who was wounded in August,

is in hospital at Towchester,, where he has undergone several operations. He is now pro- gressing favourably. One Brother a Prisoner in Germany. Gunner Sant—who is brother to Private Oswald Sant, who was taken prisoner at the battle of Mons, and whose photograph we repro- duced in our last issue, along with that of one or two others—is 21 years of age. He attended as a boy the Wycliffe and Beamont Council Schools. He was formerly employed in the galvanising department at Messrs. Rylands Bros.' works. He has been in the Army three years. Another brother, Sergeant Thomas Sant, is in training with the Lancashire .Fusiliers, and is expecting shortly to be drafted to the Front. He is a married man, having served for 15 years on the Salford Police Force. VICAR OF BURTONWOOD'S SON WOUNDED. News reached the Rev. A. M. Mitchell, Vicar of Burtonwood, yesterday morning, to the effect that his youngest son has been wounded in the head, whilst serving. with the N.P.S. Battalion, and has been admitted into hospital. It is rather less than two weeks since he was drafted to the Front. PRIVATE WILLIAM WIIEELER. "Tried To Make A Pepper Box Of My Head." Private William Wheeler, of the 2nd South Lancashire Regiment, whom we reported as wounded in our last issue,has this week written us the following interesting letter from the Gravesend Hospital, where he is at present a patient I was wounded in two fingers, but that I thought merely a scratch. I think the square heads' (Germans) tried to make a pepper box of my poor old head, as there have been enough pieces of shrapnel found in it since I was hit on November let, but, thanks to a successful operation and tender care from the sister and nurses here, I am coming on champion, and at present feel in the pink.' "I can assure you it is grand to be in a nice bed again after being in the trenches and dug out' since last December. I must say we are treated very well here by the civilians, who arrange motor drives, picture shows, teas and concerts 40., for us Who would not fight and

, be wounded after that lot? I may remark that I was not a reservist when the war broke out, as I finished with the Reserves two years after the Boer War—in which I saw three years' service and was wounded. The Slackers. "I joined the Army again Ili; I thought it my duty to do so, when we wanted men so very badly at the Front. They also want them now, and this is mostly the reason why I am writing this letter. Perhaps—l say, perhaps !—it might ' induce some of the young slackers in Warring- ton to join now and be in the final charge to Berlin. Roll on, Warrington !" The accompanying photograph, 'forwarded to us, shows Private Wheeler as he is at present in hospital, and is interesting, in view of its being the first photograph of a wounded soldier with his head bandaged that has appeared in our columns. Private Wheeler's home is at 111, Church-street, and before the outbreak of war

MISSING. PRIVATE ERNEST HOWARD Vague News Of Old "Examiner" , Employe. Mrs. Howard, of Bewsey View, Winwick-road, Orford, has received the following letter from Captain G. R. Anderton, commanding C Com- pany of the Bth Seaforth Highlanders, concern- ing her son, Private Ernest Howard, of the same regiment, who has been reported missing: "I very much regret my inability to give you any definite news about your son. Nothing official has come •to hand with regard to his fate. I have questioned all the men now sur- viving from the 25th September, and none of them appear to have seen anything of him. I sincerely wish I was able to tell you something definite, but I am unable' to do so." Comrade Says Missing Man is a Prisoner. Lance-Corporal Fraser, who is in the same Company, has written to his wife at 4, Forster- street to say that he knows that Private Hower(' is a prisoner of war in Germany, along with one of his officers. An official intimation reached Mrs. Howard yesterday morning to the effect that her son was missing. Before Private Howard was reported missing; he was officially reported wounded. Prior to enlisting Private Howard was a :winter at the " Examiner " Office. He is 26 years of age, and, as a boy, attended the Pad- gate and Orford schools. PENKETH. Gems From The Poets.-1-A very interesting evening was spent by the Wesley Guild and friends, on Tuesday evening, when Mr. D. Johnson, of Widnes, gave a recital of selections from various poets. A crowded room and a very hearty vote of thanks showed Mr. John- son how well his efforts were appreciated. Postal Facilities.—Shortage of postal workers has made it necessary to -curtail the hours of postage in the Penketh district. Formerly the letter box was .cleared at 8.40 p.m. for the night mail. It will be cleared next Monday and onwards at 8 p.m. The following letter- boxes will be correspondingly cleared at 7 p.m.: Fiddlers' Ferry, Penketh Lodge, Station-road, Stocks-lane. Halftime: Paddington, 1 goal; Elmwood, nil. • The game was evenly contested for a while in the second half, but gradually the handicap on the visitors was too much for them, and the home team gained the mastery. Fifteen min- utes from time Rothwell gained the lead ter the home team. This reverse appeared to take all heart out of the visiting players, and Roth- well scored three times before the final whistle blew. Roberts, in goal for the visitors, did very well under the adverse conditions. Result: Pad- dington, 5 goals; Elmwood Congregatior3alists, 1 , goal. JUNIOR LEAGUE. BEWSEY ATHLETIC v. FAIRFIELD UNITED RESERVE. On the Lovely-lane ground. Bewsey: Bradbury; Stephens, Blease; Kirk, Roylance, Davies; Pearson, Payton, Dickenson, Smallman and Edwards. Fairfield Reserves: Lockhart; Tunstall, Cooper; Knowles, Woodhouse, Bolton; George, Lygo, England, Boyle and Holden.. Referee, F. Kidd. There was a good crowd when England set the ball rolling for the visitors, whose forwards showed good combination, and were met by a ; pair of strong backs. For fifteen minutes play was very even in midfield. Then' there was an occasional breakaway by the home forwards, Cooper, Tunstall and Woodhouse defending well ' for the visitors. Play was transferred to the home end, where several corner kicks were con- ceded, but not improved upon, and the danger was gradually cleared. The visitors' forwards returned to the attack,. and following good head work, Lygo succeeded in beating Bradbury. The game was very fast and from a breakaway Eng- land registered the visitors' second goal. half- time: Fairfield Reserves, 2 goals; Bewsey, nil. The home. team adopted the kick-and-rush tactics, but the visitors' defence was on the alert. Woodhouse, securing the leather_made a grand individual effort and scored the third goal. This reverse-disheartened the home team, who acted on the defensive and their goal was hotly bombarded. Lygo netted the fourth goal and directly after succeeded in scoring again. , Woodhouse scored again before the call of time. , , Result: Fairfield United Reserves, 6 goals; Bow. soy Athletic, nil. FRIENDLY MATCH. EMMANUEL INSTITUTE v. WIDNES AtHLETIC. In Victoria Park institute: T. Knight; Crozier, Hughes; Parr, Hardy, Ayres; Eaton, Stubbs, T. Hughes, Roberts and Oakes. Athletic: Humphries; Dumbell, Johnson; Tegg, Illidge, Brocklebank-; Pickering, Stott, Melia, Broom and Gregory. Hughes set the ball rolling, and it was soon evident the game would be very keenly con- tested. The home team got near the visitors' goal, when Johnson cleared with a big kick, and Gregory, securing the ball, raced towards the home goal. Tricking Crozier, he passed the ball to Broom, who easily beat Knight, and opened the score after five minutes' play. The home team now retaliated in strong fashion, and were Soon- in front of the visitors' goal, where Hum- phries saved well, as also did Dumbell. A brief. visit to the home goal, and Knight was called upon to handle. Then the home team made a hot attack upon the visitors' goal, and after several clearances a grand centre from Eaton was headed past Humphries by Oakea, making the scores equal. Alter this the game was very fast, and each goal was visited. A fine shot by Roberts hit the crossbar, and from the rebound play was transferred to the home end, where Hughes was forced to concede a corner. This was well placed by Gregory, and it was only the smart work of Knight that prevented a score being made. Half-time: One goal each. . On resuming the Institute,weresocn• prorni-. 'nent and. a splendid long. shot by Hardy caused - Humphries ‘trouble, to, save, a performanc4-„he •repeated again very smartly. Knight coming_ out, Melia ,placed the ball between. the poste: The home team now attacked with • renewed .vigour; ,and T. Hughes. shet past the poet, and -Roberts, hit *the upright; and after a spell of even play in midfield the Institute returned .to the attack, and Stubbs. equalised. Directly after, Hughes gained the lead for the Institute with a rising shot. Time was drawing near, and the visitors .bombarded the home goal, and gained several corner kicks. Melia equalised the scores and the home goal was nearly captured again before the call of time. Result: Emmanuel Institute, 3 goals; Widnes Athletic, 3 goals. • MATCHES FOR TO-DAY. Great Sankey v. PURSES. FOR BUTTER

Reprint from " Manchester Courier," November 12th, 1915. DURING war time, when many companies have had to hold up dividends, and "mark time," it is a sign of considerable inherent strength to see a business expanding and opening new branches, without extra capital flotation— a thing which many of our readers will know is not permitted by Government until the war is ended. Burgons Ltd., we understand, have since the war started opened several new branches, and have others rapidly approach- ing completion, besides having acquired the old-established businesses of one or two well-known firms of high standing in their respective spheres of influente. Underneath will be found a list of the addresses of, the branches open, or shortly to be opened. A business commenced so long ago as 1847-68 years since—and showing invariable progress from one branch to nearly 60 well-appointed establishments, without ask- ing the public for a single penny of capital, must have some characteristic feature that appeals to the general householder. A business that has stood the test of time, and that can stand the test of the times through which the nation is passing, must have secured a large share of public confidence. Mere "cheapness" without quality has never been known to produce lasting rela- tions between tradesman and customer, and if natural laws apply in business as in other things in this world, it is evident that the successful growth of this business must be the result of something that has met with the continued appreciation of the consuming public. The average English man or woman likes to feel that he or she gets honest goods at an honest price, avoiding the extremes of those Who simply .seek out the lowest price, taking the quality for granted, and those who think by paying the highest price that they are necessarily getting the- highest quality. It may be taken as an axiom that the tradesman in either case must live, though recently many have found it difficult between increasing expenditure on the one hand, and "impossible" retail prices for certain commodities, fixed by an "all wise " loyal Commission, on the other. In times like these, however, the trades- man must be content to bear part of the burden, and to retain his business must be more than ever particular as to quality, and be satisfied if he can carry on business without serious loss • A business that is "found wanting" in times of stress can never expect to find in more halycon days "a place in the sun." The relations between Burgons Ltd. and the householders of Lancashire, Cheshire, and Derbyshire, have been of an unbroken, pleasant character, and the spirit of mutuality has grown with the business. The provision of 45,000 free £1 Ordinary shares by the late Governing Director, for distribution amongst customers and em- ployes, was a unique instance of the spirit of reciprocation on the one side, and the enormously increased "turnover" as a consequence has been an exemplification of the appreciation of the other. In continuing to pursue the same policy of supplying only goods of known quality on the lowest remunerative margin of profit, there seems no doubt that a well-satisfied public will promote the growth of the business "from strength to strength."

Any Shares sold by Customers at any time are reserved, as far as possible, at a moderate price for the benefit of Customers who desire to increase their proprietary interest in the business.
